# Decision Log

This log is the single source of truth for *why* the Code Skeptic Scanner backend is built the way it
is. Each entry records one decision that a competent engineer could reasonably have made
differently, together with the alternatives that were available, the reasoning that selected this
option and the risks the option carries.

Code in this repository never carries rationale. A comment states facts — the source file and line
range the construct was ported from, or that it is net-new, and the behavioural contract it delivers
— and points here with `see docs/DECISION_LOG.md`, optionally naming the entry that governs it.
Reasoning, alternatives and risks live in this file only. `DL-058` owns that convention in full,
including the one contrast a comment may state: a contrast that *is* the contract.

**Completeness invariant.** Every `DL-` identifier cited by any artifact in this repository has a
row in the table below carrying all four of its content columns — decision, alternatives, rationale
and risks. A citation is never satisfied by an entry that lives somewhere else.

**Revision.** When a decision changes, its entry is rewritten in place under the same identifier so
that a citation always resolves to the behaviour the code has now; the earlier form survives in
version history and, where it aids the reader, as a named alternative in the rewritten row. The
maintenance policy itself is recorded in `DL-099`.

**Scope of this migration.** The backend was re-platformed from Python 3.9 / Flask onto Java 21 /
Spring Boot 3.5.16. The boundary held throughout is: the same eleven unprefixed HTTP routes with
their methods, path variables, query parameters, defaults and status codes; the same four tables
(`tweets`, `responses`, `ai_tools`, `settings`) with the same twenty columns, no added `NOT NULL`,
`UNIQUE`, length or index, and the single `Tweet` 1–N `Response` association; the same four external
systems (X/Twitter, Google Cloud Natural Language, OpenAI, Notion); and the same two business rules —
the doubt rating `clamp((1 - sentimentScore) * 5, 0, 10)` and the popularity gate
`likeCount >= TWEET_POPULARITY_THRESHOLD` with a default of 100. Nothing in the code or in this log
may widen that boundary. Where an entry below says a change was not made because it lies outside the
migration, this paragraph is the boundary it means.

## How the identifiers are allocated

`DL-001` … `DL-060` are the identifiers of the decision inventory that was fixed for this migration
before any Java file existed. Each one names the subject it was allocated to and is never reused for
a different decision.

`DL-061` onward are allocated by this log, in ascending order and never reused, for decisions the
original inventory could not name — it was written before the tree existed, so it could not
anticipate a provider API's actual behaviour, a framework mechanism, a defect found while validating,
or a limitation that had to be accepted with reasons. These identifiers are cited from code, tests,
this log and `docs/TRACEABILITY_MATRIX.md`. Where one of them records a departure from the original
inventory's reading of a requirement, the departure is argued in that row's own Rationale column,
which is what an explainability obligation requires of a deviation.

**Population state.** Every identifier from `DL-001` to `DL-289` names exactly one row in the table
below — **two hundred and eighty-nine rows, with no gap and no repeat** — so a citation of any of
them resolves and no identifier is reserved without content. The two ranges together are one unbroken
table: `DL-001` … `DL-060` from the fixed inventory and `DL-061` … `DL-289` allocated by this log
(289 rows). This count is the whole of the log; it
is not a subset of it. The bidirectional mapping between source constructs and delivered artifacts is
`docs/TRACEABILITY_MATRIX.md`, which names the entry that governs every net-new target and carries no
rationale of its own; every identifier it names is a row here.

**Ownership.** One entry owns one subject, and one subject is owned by exactly one entry. A citation
names the entry that owns the subject the reader is looking at, never an entry that merely mentions
it: an entry about the comment convention does not authorise a mapper, and an entry about choosing a
client library does not cover that client's lifecycle. When an entry was found to carry more than one
subject it was split, and each successor took one subject — `DL-077` was split this way into
`DL-048`, `DL-123`, `DL-217`, `DL-225` and `DL-232`, which is the same successor set the pointer
enumeration below states for it. When two entries were found to describe one subject, the cited one
was kept and the other became a pointer to it.

**Superseded entries.** An entry that no longer describes the delivered tree is rewritten in place —
never deleted and never renumbered — either as a correction or as a pointer opening
`**Superseded by DL-xxx.**` that names the entry which now owns its subject. This keeps every
identifier cited from any commit resolvable. These 29 rows are pointers of that kind, and this is the
complete set: `DL-077` → `DL-048`, `DL-123`, `DL-217`, `DL-225` and `DL-232`;
`DL-087` → `DL-245` and `DL-249`; `DL-093`, `DL-144` and `DL-176` → `DL-111`;
`DL-091`, `DL-125` and `DL-158` → `DL-180`; `DL-126` → `DL-090` and `DL-219`;
`DL-134` and `DL-137` → `DL-068`; `DL-135` → `DL-074` and `DL-103`;
`DL-139` and `DL-163` → `DL-080`; `DL-140` → `DL-092`;
`DL-141` → `DL-108` and `DL-186`; `DL-160` and `DL-173` → `DL-106`;
`DL-161` and `DL-172` → `DL-107`; `DL-165` → `DL-041` and `DL-180`;
`DL-167` → `DL-081` and `DL-177`; `DL-168` → `DL-178`; `DL-171` → `DL-240`; `DL-124` and `DL-179` → `DL-159`;
`DL-182` → `DL-248`; `DL-204` → `DL-241`; `DL-229` → `DL-270` and `DL-271`. This enumeration is asserted
against the rows themselves, so prose and table cannot fall out of step. A row rewritten as a
correction rather than a pointer keeps its own subject: `DL-100`, `DL-101` and `DL-169` each record the
version override in force for the subject they always owned, and `DL-147`, `DL-152`, `DL-155`,
`DL-102`, `DL-103`, `DL-104`, `DL-105`, `DL-136`, `DL-276`, `DL-283` and `DL-285` each record that the
mechanism they described was withdrawn and what the delivered tree does instead. `DL-102` … `DL-105`,
`DL-136` and `DL-276` were withdrawn together, when the operations delivery was restored to the
line-level carve-out `DL-215` states; `DL-283` and `DL-285` were withdrawn together, when the delivered
tree was realigned to the migration boundary stated above.

**Consistency between rows.** Two rows must never describe the same construct differently. `DL-083`
owns the adapter's `IllegalStateException` codes and the service-boundary conversion to
`ResponseGenerationException`; `DL-178` defers to that contract and owns the decision to fail rather
than return empty text, plus the check order.

**What the no-rationale rule governs.** The prohibition is on *comments*: a comment states provenance
facts and points here. It does not govern program output. One runtime diagnostic —
`config/DatabaseUrlTranslator`'s warning for the `mariadb` scheme — explains in its own text why the
bundled driver cannot serve a MariaDB server and what to do instead, because a warning an operator
cannot act on is not a warning (`DL-187`; a logged warning has to be actionable to be worth
emitting). That text is emitted to the log, not written in a comment, and it is the only place in the
tree where reasoning appears outside this file.

**Where the authoritative statement lives.** For a build coordinate, `backend/pom.xml` is
authoritative and this log explains it; a comment in that file, or a row here, that contradicts the
file's own declarations is a defect, which is what `DL-170` records and closes — it owns the boundary
between the versions the file states and the versions the BOM resolves. `DL-100`, `DL-101`, `DL-169`
and `DL-240` own the four property overrides the file declares, `DL-241` owns the one raise written on
a coordinate because the BOM publishes no property for it, and `DL-171` and `DL-204` are pointers to
those owners for the subjects they once carried. For behaviour, the code is authoritative and this log explains
it. For scope, the boundary stated above is authoritative.

## Where to find a decision

Entries are ordered by identifier, because an identifier is never renumbered and a citation must
always resolve: `DL-n` is found at position n in the single table under **Entries**, which is how any
citation is resolved regardless of subject.

### Index of the original inventory (`DL-001` … `DL-060`)

This index covers the sixty identifiers of the inventory that was fixed before any Java file existed,
grouped by the subject areas that inventory itself named, so those sixty can be reviewed area by area.
It is **not** an index of the whole log — the 229 implementation-phase identifiers are reached the two
ways described below it. It moves no row and renames no identifier.

| Area | Entries |
|------|---------|
| Structure and build | `DL-001` … `DL-009`, `DL-055`, `DL-056`, `DL-057`, `DL-060` |
| Dependencies | `DL-010` … `DL-013` |
| Authentication | `DL-014` … `DL-021` |
| Wire contract | `DL-022` … `DL-025`, `DL-038`, `DL-048`, `DL-059` |
| Persistence and configuration | `DL-026` … `DL-031`, `DL-050`, `DL-051`, `DL-052` |
| Language model and sentiment | `DL-032` … `DL-037` |
| Net-new services | `DL-039` … `DL-043` |
| Ingestion and scheduling | `DL-044` … `DL-047`, `DL-049` |
| Scope judgements and rule compliance | `DL-053`, `DL-054`, `DL-058` |

`DL-061` … `DL-289` are the implementation-phase allocations. `DL-240` … `DL-278` were allocated while
resolving the findings of the code reviews that followed the first delivery: they cover the dependency
and runtime patches, the validation-parity and query-shape corrections, the analytics window, the
bounded list and prompt processing, background ownership, the stream hardening set, the adapter
lifecycles, the pool surface, the auth ingress obligation, the single declaration site of the
transport-security headers, the one clock bean and the test-hygiene decisions. `DL-279` was allocated
for the build-scoped `test`-profile guard, when a review of the citations found four of them
naming an entry that does not own that subject. `DL-280` … `DL-285` were allocated last, by the
security review that followed: the finite reconnect horizon on a reported rate-limit reset, the
background-ownership lease and the reserved `settings` key it is held in, the per-pass candidate
ceiling, and two mechanisms that are no longer delivered — `DL-283` and `DL-285` each record what was
withdrawn and what the tree does instead. `DL-286` … `DL-289` were allocated by the citation audit that
followed, for four delivered choices no entry owned: the Python truth test the `tweet_id` guard applies,
the one shared unresolved-placeholder inspector, the Natural Language client's lifecycle and call
deadlines, and the header-safety gate on the Notion transport.
They are not grouped here because each
one names the artifact it governs in its own Decision column, and because
`docs/TRACEABILITY_MATRIX.md` already maps every delivered artifact to the entry that governs it —
that mapping, not a second grouping, is the way to reach the entry for a given file. Two placements
in the table above are worth stating outright, because a reader working from the original inventory
would expect them the other way round: `DL-053` owns the one-line `scripts/deploy.sh` change and
`DL-056` owns the `.github/workflows/cd.yml` build-context decision. `docs/TRACEABILITY_MATRIX.md`
cites them in exactly that sense, so the assignment is fixed by live citation rather than by
preference — see `DL-058`.

## Entries

| ID | Decision | Alternatives considered | Rationale | Risks |
|----|----------|-------------------------|-----------|-------|
| DL-001 | The Java module replaces `backend/` in place: `backend/pom.xml` and `backend/src/**` are created and the twenty Python files under `backend/app/**` and `backend/tests/**` are deleted in the same change. | (a) A new sibling `backend-java/` module beside a retained `backend/`. (b) A nested `backend/java/` subtree. (c) A separate repository or a submodule. | The prompt names the artifact `backend/pom.xml` and instructs "From `backend/`, run `mvn clean install`", which fixes the module root. Every operational file already addresses `backend/` by that exact path — `COPY ./backend .`, `docker build … ./backend`, `cd backend`, `working-directory: ./backend` — so keeping the path makes every operational edit content-only instead of a path rewrite. (a) and (b) would leave twenty files of non-importable Python beside the Java tree, which contradicts "do not interleave Java and Python source" and would keep failing the pipeline's lint, type-check and test steps. (c) was excluded by this migration's scope. | A side-by-side diff of the two implementations is not available in the working tree; it is available through git history and through `docs/TRACEABILITY_MATRIX.md`. Any external tooling that still expects Python under `backend/` breaks at once rather than degrading quietly. |
| DL-002 | Maven coordinates are `com.codeskeptic:code-skeptic-scanner-backend`, and the base package is `com.codeskeptic.scanner`. | (a) `com.codeskeptic.scanner:backend`. (b) A reverse-DNS group derived from a hosting domain, e.g. `io.github.…`. (c) `com.codeskeptic:backend`. | Both names are derived from identifiers already in the repository rather than invented: the Celery application name `'code_skeptic_scanner'` at `backend/app/tasks/response_generation.py:L8` and the container image `code-skeptic-backend` at `.github/workflows/cd.yml:L12`. (b) asserts ownership of a domain the repository does not evidence. (c) is ambiguous in a repository that also contains a frontend. | The artifactId is long, so it appears in full in the jar name and in `spring-boot:repackage` output. Renaming later would touch the Dockerfile, the CD workflow and the deploy script. |
| DL-003 | The Maven parent is `org.springframework.boot:spring-boot-starter-parent:3.5.16`, and it stays there. **Support horizon.** 3.5.16 is the final open-source release of the 3.5.x line: the 3.5.x branch receives no further free patches, and a subsequent fix on the 3.x line is available only under commercial support. No CVE affects this exact version at the review cutoff. **Upgrade plan.** The move off 3.5.x is a 4.x migration, not a 3.x patch bump, and it is gated on three conditions, any one of which triggers it: a published advisory that affects 3.5.16 and is reachable from this application's own code paths; a commercial-support decision for the 3.x line; or a scope that admits the Spring Framework 7 baseline 4.x carries. The migration itself is a single coordinated change — the parent version, the BOM-managed transitive set `docs/TRACEABILITY_MATRIX.md` §1.5 records, and whatever of the Jakarta, Hibernate and Spring Security majors 4.x moves with it — and `BuildDependencyContractTest` is what makes it visible: it pins the parent version, the `java.version` property and the version floor of every explicitly pinned coordinate, so an upgrade that is attempted without updating the contract fails the build rather than passing quietly. The manifest itself carries a comment on the parent naming this row, and `BuildDependencyContractTest` asserts that pointer is present. | (a) Spring Boot 4.1.0, the newest release overall. (b) An earlier 3.x patch such as 3.3.x. (c) Import `spring-boot-dependencies` as a BOM instead of inheriting the parent. (d) Move to 4.x now, on the grounds that the 3.5.x line is closed. (e) Record no plan and revisit when an advisory appears. | The prompt specifies "latest stable 3.x", and 3.5.16 is the newest stable 3.x on Maven Central; 4.1.0 would contradict an explicit instruction and additionally pull in Spring Framework 7. (b) forgoes fixes for no stated benefit. (c) loses the parent's plugin management, which is what allows every plugin version in this POM to stay unpinned. (d) is the same instruction breach as (a) — the frozen dependency set fixes the coordinate, and the review that raised the support horizon stated the same conclusion: record a plan and do not change the frozen 3.x parent for an informational item. (e) is what this row previously did, and it leaves the next reader without the trigger conditions or the blast radius, which is the part of an upgrade that is expensive to reconstruct. | The 3.x line has a finite support window, so a future move to 4.x is inevitable and will carry a Spring Framework 7 upgrade with it. Inheriting the parent means a Boot version bump silently moves every BOM-managed transitive version at once. The trigger conditions are stated here and checked by nobody: no scheduled job, no advisory feed and no automated proposal watches this coordinate, which is the gap DL-276 records. Between the closure of 3.5.x and whichever trigger fires first, a newly published advisory against 3.5.16 has no free remedy inside the 3.x line at all — the only responses are the commercial channel or an accelerated 4.x migration, and this row is where that is written down rather than discovered. |
| DL-004 | The build tool is Maven, driven from `backend/pom.xml`, and every operational file invokes it: `.github/workflows/ci.yml` runs `mvn -B clean verify` and `scripts/deploy.sh` runs `mvn clean package`. | (a) Gradle with a Kotlin or Groovy DSL. (b) Maven with a wrapper (`mvnw`) committed alongside. | The prompt names Maven as the primary build tool and states the definition of done as `mvn clean verify`. (a) would contradict that. (b) is a reasonable convenience, but the CI job pins the JDK through `actions/setup-java` with `cache: maven` and the deploy script runs on a host that already has Maven, so a wrapper would add two committed binaries and a `.mvn/` directory without changing any outcome. | Without a committed wrapper, the Maven version is whatever the executing environment provides, so a build could behave differently across machines. The parent POM pins every plugin version, which keeps that exposure to Maven core itself. |
| DL-005 | Java 21 is the language level and the runtime: `<java.version>21</java.version>` in the POM, `actions/setup-java` with the Temurin distribution and `java-version: '21'` in CI, and an `eclipse-temurin` 21 JRE runtime image (delivered as the reviewed patch tag `eclipse-temurin:21.0.11_10-jre` beside its registry digest — DL-106 owns that pinning). | (a) Java 17, the previous LTS. (b) Java 23 or another non-LTS release. (c) A different vendor build such as Zulu or Corretto. | The prompt specifies Java 21 (LTS), which Spring Boot 3.5.x fully supports, and records were compiled and run on OpenJDK 21 during analysis. (a) forgoes records-adjacent language features already relied on and contradicts the instruction. (b) has no LTS support window. (c) is functionally equivalent; Temurin was chosen because an `eclipse-temurin` 21 JRE is the specified runtime base image, so using Temurin in CI keeps the build and runtime JDKs from the same vendor. | Pinning a major version in three places means an upgrade has to change all three together; a mismatch between the CI JDK and the image JRE would only surface at runtime. |
| DL-006 | Lombok is not a dependency. Constructors, getters, setters, `equals`, `hashCode` and any `toString` override are written by hand. | (a) Add `org.projectlombok:lombok` and annotate entities and DTOs. (b) Use Lombok on entities only. | Java 21 records already remove the boilerplate from every DTO in this project, and JPA entities need mutable fields and a no-argument constructor regardless, so Lombok would save little. Skipping it removes an annotation processor that has to stay aligned across JDK upgrades and keeps the generated bytecode readable in a stack trace. The prompt explicitly invited this decision to be flagged. | The four entity classes carry visible accessor boilerplate, and each new column means another getter and setter pair by hand. Hand-written `equals`/`hashCode` can drift from the field set if a column is ever added without updating them. |
| DL-007 | JaCoCo is not configured; no coverage report is produced or enforced. | (a) Bind `jacoco-maven-plugin` to `prepare-agent` and `report`. (b) Add it with a coverage threshold that fails the build. | Coverage reporting is a new capability this migration does not request, and the retired Python project had none, so adding it would be scope creep rather than translation. The definition of done is a green `mvn clean verify`, which JaCoCo does not contribute to. | Test coverage is not measured, so a gap in the suite is invisible to the build. Nothing prevents adding the plugin later; it needs no source change. |
| DL-008 | `maven-failsafe-plugin` is not configured; every test runs under Surefire in the `test` phase. | (a) Add Failsafe and move `@SpringBootTest` and `@DataJpaTest` classes to an `*IT` naming convention bound to `integration-test`. (b) Add Failsafe and keep Surefire for unit tests only. | Spring Boot's test slices run in-process against H2 and need no external service, so they complete under Surefire inside the same `mvn clean verify` that is the definition of done. A separate integration-test phase would add a plugin and a naming convention without adding a single assertion. | Slow context-loading tests share the `test` phase with fast unit tests, so there is no way to run only the fast ones. If a future test ever needs a real external service, the phase split will have to be introduced then. |
| DL-009 | The test-time database is `com.h2database:h2` at `test` scope, resolved by the parent BOM to 2.3.232. The scope was narrowed from `runtime` to `test` under DL-242 and the `h2` scheme was removed from `config/DatabaseUrlTranslator` under DL-071, so no main code path and no deployed artifact reaches it. Testcontainers is not used. | (a) `org.testcontainers:junit-jupiter` plus `org.testcontainers:postgresql`, running a real PostgreSQL per test class. (b) A developer-supplied external database for tests. (c) H2 at `runtime` scope, so the same in-memory database is also available to a local `spring-boot:run`, which an earlier revision of this entry argued for. | Testcontainers' `junit-jupiter` and `postgresql` modules publish only up to 1.21.4 while the standalone artifact has moved to 2.0.5, so mixing them would break resolution; Testcontainers also needs a working Docker daemon, which the no-manual-intervention requirement cannot assume. (b) contradicts the same requirement. (c) is the position this entry once took and it is withdrawn: it ships a database engine and an unauthenticated console servlet into the deployed artifact to serve a developer convenience, and a developer who wants it can point `scanner.database-url` at a running PostgreSQL instead — DL-242 owns that scope decision. | H2 is not PostgreSQL or MySQL: DDL and dialect differences mean a mapping can pass on H2 and behave differently in production. The entity mappings are therefore verified against generated DDL for every supported dialect, not against H2 alone — see DL-068, DL-069 and DL-166. A local `spring-boot:run` now needs a real database, or the H2 coordinate added back at `runtime` scope by whoever wants it. |
| DL-010 | Sentiment analysis uses the official Google client `com.google.cloud:google-cloud-language`, pinned explicitly to 2.96.0, and `LanguageServiceClient` is obtained with Application Default Credentials. | (a) Import `com.google.cloud:libraries-bom` and leave the artifact unversioned. (b) Call the REST API directly through `WebClient` or `RestClient`. (c) A third-party wrapper. | The artifact is actively maintained and is the same client family the retired `backend/app/services/sentiment_analysis.py:L1` used, so the port stays a substitution rather than a redesign; Application Default Credentials reproduce `LanguageServiceClient()` at `:L8` with no key property, which is why no Google credential appears in `application.yml`. (a) hides the resolved version behind a chain of imported BOMs, so the explicit pin is more auditable. (b) would hand-roll authentication that the client already implements. | An explicit pin has to be advanced by hand and can fall behind the BOM's own alignment. Application Default Credentials mean the service needs an ambient credential in every environment it runs in, and a missing credential surfaces only when the first analysis is attempted. |
| DL-011 | Response generation uses the official `com.openai:openai-java`, pinned explicitly to 4.49.0. | (a) `com.theokanning.openai-gpt3-java:service`, the long-standing community client. (b) Call the REST API directly through `WebClient`. | The official SDK is actively published — 4.49.0 dates from within days of the analysis — and supersedes the community wrapper, whose name still carries `gpt3`. Its Chat Completions builder was compiled during analysis and accepts `model`, a user message, `maxCompletionTokens`, `temperature` and `n`, so every parameter the retired `backend/app/services/llm_service.py:L19-26` passed maps across exactly. (b) would hand-roll request shapes that change with the API. | The SDK brings OkHttp and, through it, the Kotlin standard library onto the classpath. A major-version bump of a fast-moving SDK can change builder shapes, which is why every call site is confined behind the `LlmService` adapter. |
| DL-012 | The X (Twitter) integration is hand-rolled on `WebClient` from `spring-boot-starter-webflux`; no Java X client is used. | (a) `org.twitter4j:twitter4j-core` plus `twitter4j-stream`. (b) `com.twitter:twitter-api-java-sdk`. (c) `io.github.redouane59:twittered`. | No actively maintained Java client for X exists: twitter4j-core was last published in 2022 and twitter4j-stream targets the retired v1.1 `statuses` API, `twitter-api-java-sdk` was last published in 2022, and `twittered` is not on Maven Central at all. Adopting a four-year-stale client that targets an API version which no longer exists would be worse than a small hand-rolled client, and a plain REST client over `WebClient`/`RestClient` is explicitly sanctioned for this situation. `WebClient` is also the right shape for a long-lived chunked NDJSON stream. | The request shapes, the rules syntax and the reconnection behaviour are this project's responsibility and will need maintenance when the API changes. The exposure is bounded because every call lives behind the `TwitterService` and `TweetStreamClient` adapter boundary, so adopting a maintained client later is a local change. |
| DL-013 | The Notion integration is hand-rolled on `org.springframework.web.client.RestClient`, which ships with `spring-boot-starter-web`, so it adds no dependency. | (a) `com.github.seratch:notion-sdk-jvm-core`. (b) `tw.yukina.notion.sdk:notion-sdk-java`. (c) `WebClient` instead of `RestClient`. | The retired source made exactly two Notion calls — `pages.create` and `databases.query` at `backend/app/services/notion_service.py:L23-26,L32-38` — so an SDK would buy very little. (a) was last published more than two years ago and would add a stale transitive graph. (b) has negligible adoption. (c) is available but blocking calls are what these two operations are, and `RestClient` expresses them without a reactive type; it was compile-verified during analysis to need no additional coordinate. | The Notion API version header and the two request bodies are this project's responsibility. Notion is a secondary mirror and the relational database remains the system of record — see DL-052 for what is logged when a mirror call fails — so a Notion outage degrades a side effect rather than the service. |
| DL-014 | JWTs are minted and parsed with `io.jsonwebtoken` jjwt 0.13.0, split across `jjwt-api` at compile scope and `jjwt-impl` and `jjwt-jackson` at runtime scope. | (a) `spring-boot-starter-oauth2-resource-server` with a `NimbusJwtDecoder` over `MacAlgorithm.HS256`. (b) Nimbus JOSE + JWT directly. (c) Declare all three jjwt modules at compile scope. | This service mints its own tokens; it is not a resource server consuming a third-party issuer's tokens. (a) is built around decoding tokens issued elsewhere and would still need a separate minting library, so it adds a starter without removing work. The prompt names jjwt first. (c) would put implementation classes on the compile classpath, where application code could bind to them instead of the API. | A hand-written authentication filter of roughly thirty lines replaces framework-provided decoding, so its correctness is this project's responsibility; `security/JwtServiceTest` and `api/AuthControllerTest` exist to hold it. Splitting the modules by scope means a mistaken compile-scope import of an `impl` class fails the build, which is the intent, but the failure message is not obvious. |
| DL-015 | `scanner.jwt.algorithm` is a declared property bound from `${ALGORITHM:HS256}`, and the only value it accepts is `HS256`. The accepted string is read from the signer the implementation applies — `Jwts.SIG.HS256.getId()` — and any other non-blank value fails startup. An absent or blank value leaves HS256 in effect. | (a) Omit the property and hardcode HS256. (b) Support the full JWS algorithm set the property could name, including the RS and ES families. (c) Accept the whole HS family — HS256, HS384 and HS512 — while continuing to sign with HS256. (d) Accept the HS family and select the signer from the configured value. | `backend/app/core/security.py:L11` read `settings.ALGORITHM`, which `backend/app/core/config.py` never declared, so the key must exist for the configuration drift to be closed and (a) does not close it. HS256 is `flask_jwt_extended`'s default and PyJWT's typical symmetric use, and the agreed design fixes it, so the token format is not a deployment choice. (c) was delivered first and is a silent contradiction: a deployment configuring HS384 started successfully and then minted HS256 tokens, so the property described one thing and the service did another. (d) would honour the value but contradicts 0.6.1.2 and turns the accepted set into a real branch to key-manage and test on every path. (b) additionally needs asymmetric key material, a key store and a rotation story the source has nowhere. Deriving the accepted string from the signer rather than writing the literal twice means the two cannot drift apart in a later change. | A deployment carrying `ALGORITHM=HS384` — a value the property's type permits and the previous behaviour accepted — now fails to start rather than being quietly ignored. That is the intent. No shipped configuration sets anything but HS256, and `ALGORITHM` is not among the environment keys the deployment supplies, so the `HS256` default applies everywhere. |
| DL-016 | `scanner.jwt.secret` is bound from `${SECRET_KEY}` with **no default**, so the application fails to start when the variable is absent. The value the variable carries is an encoding of the key material, and the encodings accepted and the floor applied to the decoded material are DL-186's subject. | (a) Ship a development default such as `changeme`. (b) Generate a random secret at startup when none is supplied. (c) Bind an empty default and fail later, at first use. | A committed development secret is the kind of value that reaches production, and jjwt 0.13.0 additionally rejects an HS256 key shorter than 256 bits, so a short placeholder would fail anyway. (b) silently invalidates every previously issued token on restart and breaks any multi-instance deployment. (c) turns a configuration error into a request-time failure far from its cause. Failing fast at boot is the behaviour a missing mandatory secret should have. | Every environment, including a bare local run, must supply `SECRET_KEY` before the context will refresh. The test profile supplies its own key so that `mvn clean verify` still needs no manual step. |
| DL-017 | The access-token lifetime is `scanner.jwt.expiration-minutes`, defaulting to 60, and the value is validated when `security/JwtService` is constructed: below 1 minute or above 60 fails startup. Minutes are converted to the seconds the token endpoint reports with a checked multiplication. | (a) A shorter default such as 15 minutes. (b) A longer default such as 24 hours. (c) Access token plus refresh token. (d) Declare the bound with Bean Validation `@Min` and `@Max` on the properties record. (e) Clamp an out-of-range value into the range instead of failing. | `documentation/Technical Specifications.md:L580` states a one-hour maximum JWT lifespan, which makes 60 minutes a documented ceiling rather than a preference — and therefore something to enforce. The first delivery accepted any `long`, including zero, negative values and `Long.MAX_VALUE`, the last of which overflows the seconds conversion. 60 is the largest value honouring the maximum and mirrors `exp = datetime.utcnow() + expires_delta` at `backend/app/core/security.py:L9-10`. (a) is safer but forces re-authentication in a system with no refresh flow. (b) exceeds the documented maximum. (c) introduces a token type, a store and a revocation surface the source does not have. (d) would add a Bean Validation constraint, and validation parity limits validation to the two the source declares — so the guard sits beside the secret-length and algorithm guards already in the same constructor, which also keeps every startup failure in one place. (e) hides a misconfiguration: an operator asking for a day would silently get an hour. | A deployment configured above 60 minutes, or at zero, now fails to start rather than running with a lifetime nobody intended. A leaked token still lives for up to an hour and there is no revocation path short of rotating `SECRET_KEY`, which invalidates every token at once. Clients must handle a 401 by re-authenticating. |
| DL-018 | Every minted token carries exactly three claims — `sub` (the principal name), `iat` and `exp`. No role, scope, permission or caller-supplied claim is copied in. | (a) Copy an arbitrary caller-supplied claim map, as `create_access_token(data: dict)` did at `backend/app/core/security.py:L7-8`. (b) Add role or authority claims. (c) Add the registered `jti`, `iss` and `aud` claims. | The source signature accepted a dictionary and copied it wholesale into the claim set, so any caller could place anything — including contradictory or privileged values — into a signed token; it had zero call sites, so nothing depended on that freedom. This service authenticates exactly one configuration-backed principal (DL-020) and RBAC is out of scope, so the subject is the only claim any code path reads. `iat` and `exp` are derived from a single captured instant, so the pair cannot disagree. (c) would add registered claims that no validator in this application checks, which is ceremony rather than security. | A future multi-principal or authorization model needs an additive change to both the claim set and the filter that reads it. Because no `jti` is issued, an individual token cannot be revoked before it expires; the exposure is bounded by the 60-minute lifetime of DL-017 instead. |
| DL-019 | A net-new endpoint `POST /auth/token` accepts `{"username": "admin", "password": "<plaintext>"}` and returns `{"access_token": "<compact-jws>", "token_type": "bearer", "expires_in": 3600}`. It is the only unauthenticated route; all eleven pre-existing routes require an authenticated principal. | (a) `POST /login` returning a bare token string. (b) `POST /auth/login` with a nested envelope. (c) HTTP Basic authentication on every route and no token endpoint at all. (d) No token endpoint, leaving tokens to be minted out of band. | The retired tree registered no authentication blueprint and `create_access_token` had zero call sites, so there is nothing to port and the shape is a choice that must be flagged rather than slipped in. The OAuth 2 conventional body is what a future `frontend/src/services/authService.ts` would most naturally consume, and `frontend/src/utils/api.ts:L13-16` already sends `Authorization: Bearer ${token}`, so a bearer token is the expected currency. (a) and (b) are equally workable but neither matches the convention the frontend's header already implies. (c) would send credentials on every request. (d) leaves the requirement for a working token-issuance path unmet. The endpoint is deliberately the only member of its group: no refresh route, because the token lifetime is fixed and re-authenticating costs one request (DL-017); and no logout, registration or password-change route, because the token is stateless and user management is out of scope (DL-020). | This is a genuinely new public route, so it is a new attack surface: it is unauthenticated by necessity and has no rate limiting, because new rate limiting is out of scope. Its path and body shape are now a contract that a client can depend on. |
| DL-020 | The credential store is configuration-backed: a single principal registered through an `InMemoryUserDetailsManager` from `scanner.auth.username` (default `admin`) and `scanner.auth.password-hash` (a bcrypt hash, no default). No `app_users` table is created. | (a) A JPA `app_users` entity with a `UserDetailsService` over it. (b) A plaintext password property compared directly. (c) An external identity provider. | Adding a table would break the schema-parity mandate that the target has exactly the same four tables as the source, and there is no user-management requirement anywhere: no registration route, no user CRUD, no password reset, and RBAC is explicitly out of scope, so the table would be an unused capability. The only `User` entity the project documents describes a Twitter account, not an application principal. A hash property still exercises `BCryptPasswordEncoder` for real, which is what makes the passlib translation live code instead of dead code. Publishing a `UserDetailsService` bean at all is also what displaces Spring Boot's `UserDetailsServiceAutoConfiguration`: leaving that auto-configuration in place would register a `user` principal whose generated password is written to the log at every startup, which is a credential in the log and an unintended second principal. (b) would put a plaintext credential in configuration. (c) is explicitly out of scope. | One shared credential, no per-user accounts, and no rotation without a redeploy. The extension path is a JPA-backed `UserDetailsService` behind the same interface, which is a localised change. `AUTH_PASSWORD_HASH` has no default, so it must be supplied or the context will not refresh. |
| DL-021 | Authentication is genuinely enforced in the Java service, on all eleven pre-existing routes, where the Python decorators enforced nothing. | (a) Reproduce the source's effective behaviour by leaving every route open. (b) Enforce on write routes only. (c) Enforce, but behind a flag defaulting to off. | `@jwt_required` was applied bare, without parentheses, at all eleven sites, which in `flask-jwt-extended` 4.x registers the decorator factory instead of the guard — so the source's *intent* was unambiguous and only its wiring was broken. The prompt orders a working token-issuance path, and the frontend already sends a bearer header. (a) would preserve a defect as a feature. (b) is arbitrary in a system whose read data is the product. (c) ships a switch whose off position is a vulnerability. | This is a deliberate behaviour change: any client that reached these routes unauthenticated now receives 401. It is called out here because a reader could otherwise mistake it for a regression. Every controller test therefore supplies authentication. |
| DL-022 | JSON member names stay `snake_case`, declared per component with `@JsonProperty`. `spring.jackson.property-naming-strategy` is deliberately not set. | (a) Set a global `SNAKE_CASE` naming strategy and drop the per-component annotations. (b) Switch the wire to `camelCase` to match the frontend's Zod contracts. | The wire contract is defined by the retired Flask handlers, which emitted `snake_case` keys, and the preserved wire contract requires it to survive verbatim. (a) is tempting but would also rename members this project does not intend to expose implicitly, and it makes the wire name invisible at the declaration site, where a reviewer checks it. (b) would be an improvement to the frontend's benefit, but this migration forbids fixing the frontend and forbids changing the contract; the divergence is left in place — see DL-059. | Every DTO component carries an explicit annotation, so a new component silently defaults to the Java member name if the annotation is forgotten. The frontend's `camelCase` contracts still do not match, which is a known, deliberate divergence. |
| DL-023 | Identifiers serialise as `String` on the wire while the persistence types stay numeric: `TweetDto.id`, `ResponseDto.id`, `ResponseDto.tweetId` and `AiToolDto.id` are `String`, and conversion happens only at the DTO boundary. `equals`/`hashCode` on an entity compare the persistent identifier and are net-new Java persistence mechanics with no Python counterpart. | (a) Serialise identifiers as JSON numbers, matching the column types. (b) Make the persistence types `String` as well. (c) Overload the X post identifier onto `tweets.id`. | `backend/app/schema/tweet.py:L6` and `frontend/src/schema/tweet.ts:L4` both type `id` as a string, and `frontend/src/schema/aiTool.ts` does the same, so a string identifier is the only typed contract the repository actually contains; JSON numbers also lose precision past 2^53 in a JavaScript client. (b) would change the column types and break schema parity. (c) would change the meaning of a generated surrogate key and risk collisions with the generator — see DL-049. The entity `equals`/`hashCode` form yields `false` whenever either identifier is `null`, so two unpersisted instances never compare equal, and `hashCode` is derived from the entity type so it is stable across the transient-to-persistent transition. | Two conversions exist per identifier — entity to wire and wire back to a lookup — and a malformed inbound identifier is a not-found rather than a bad-request condition, which is what DL-048 records. A type-constant `hashCode` puts every instance of an entity in one hash bucket, which is irrelevant at the collection sizes this service handles. |
| DL-024 | `tweets.media` and `tweets.ai_tools_mentioned` remain single delimited `String` columns and are exposed on the wire as JSON arrays. `util/DelimitedStringListConverter` performs the conversion with a comma delimiter, is applied explicitly per attribute and is not auto-applied. | (a) An `@ElementCollection` side table per list. (b) A JSON column type. (c) A foreign key or join table from `ai_tools_mentioned` to `ai_tools`. (d) A different delimiter, such as a vertical bar or the ASCII unit separator. (e) `@Converter(autoApply = true)`. | `backend/app/db/models.py:L15,L18` declare both columns as a plain `String`, while `backend/app/schema/tweet.py:L11,L14` and `frontend/src/schema/tweet.ts:L9,L12` both declare `List[str]`, so the conversion is required and the column definition must not change. (a) and (b) would add a table or change a column type, breaking schema parity. (c) is explicitly forbidden for this migration. (d) is safer against embedded delimiters but a comma is what a human editing the column would write, and the source stored no structured value at all. (e) would silently apply the conversion to every `List<String>` attribute in the model, including any future one that should not be delimited. | The conversion is lossy: an element that itself contains a comma is split into two on the way back, and a `null` or blank column normalises to an empty list rather than to `null`. Both are accepted because the source stored free text with no escaping either. Because the converter is not auto-applied, a future entity attribute that needs it must name it explicitly or it will silently persist as `[a, b]` through the default rendering. |
| DL-025 | The JPA entity for the `responses` table keeps the source class name `Response`, with `@Table(name = "responses")`, and its identifier is typed `Integer`, as `entity/Tweet.id` and `entity/AiTool.id` are. | (a) Rename the type to `GeneratedResponse` so it does not read like an HTTP type. (b) Type the identifier `Long`, which the literal `JpaRepository<Response, Long>` prose signature assumes and which this entry authorised in an earlier form. (c) Map `tweet_id` as a scalar column alongside a separate association attribute, mirroring the two source declarations literally. | `backend/app/db/models.py:L20` declares `class Response`, and name parity is precisely what this refactor preserves; the name does not collide with `org.springframework.http.ResponseEntity`, which is a different type, and the entity is referenced only from the persistence layer. On the identifier: `:L23` declares `Column(Integer, primary_key=True)` and `:L27` declares `Column(Integer, ForeignKey('tweets.id'))`, so `Integer` is the one-to-one mapping and the requirement of identical column types settles it; the entity-mapping rule admits `Integer` and `Long` alike, so the narrower parity goal governs. (b) was measured to generate `bigint` on H2, PostgreSQL and MySQL, widening both the primary key and the foreign key beyond the source, and its one argument — that the foreign key must not be narrower than the key it references — is satisfied either way now that both sides are `Integer`. (c) would map one column twice, which Hibernate rejects unless one side is read-only, so the single `@ManyToOne @JoinColumn(name = "tweet_id")` carries both the column and the relationship, as the association-mapping rule directs. | `responses` is capped at 2^31−1 rows, which is what the source declared. The wire contract is unaffected: the identifier serialises as a `String` either way (DL-023). Because `ddl-auto=update` never narrows a column, a database created while this entry authorised `Long` keeps `bigint` and diverges from a freshly created one; `repository/JpaMappingIntegrationTest` asserts `integer` in live metadata and in the generated DDL of all three supported vendors, so the drift cannot recur unnoticed (DL-166). |
| DL-026 | Schema bootstrap is `spring.jpa.hibernate.ddl-auto=update`, with `spring.jpa.open-in-view=false`. There is no migration tool. | (a) Flyway. (b) Liquibase. (c) `ddl-auto=validate` plus a hand-written DDL script. (d) `ddl-auto=none` and an out-of-band schema. (e) Leave `open-in-view` at its default of `true`. | Nothing in the retired tree ever created a table: there is no `Base.metadata.create_all()` call, no migrations directory and no CLI, so the implicit expectation was that the schema simply exists. `update` is the minimal equivalent of that expectation and satisfies the schema-bootstrap expectation without introducing a capability the source lacked; introducing Flyway or Liquibase would be a new capability, not a translation. (c) and (d) would leave a clean checkout unable to serve a request, which contradicts "no manual intervention". (e) keeps a persistence context open for the whole request, which hides lazy-loading mistakes behind working code and holds a connection longer than the transaction needs. | `update` never drops or narrows anything and never widens an existing column, so a database created before a mapping change keeps the old physical type — the divergence that DL-069 weighs. It also means the annotations *are* the DDL, so a mapping error becomes a schema error, which is why the mappings are verified against generated DDL for every supported dialect. There is no versioned migration history, so a schema change cannot be reviewed as a diff. |
| DL-027 | `DATABASE_URL` binds to `scanner.database-url`, and `config/DatabaseUrlTranslator` converts it into a JDBC URL plus separate credentials. `spring.datasource.url`, `.username`, `.password`, `.driver-class-name` and `spring.jpa.database-platform` are all deliberately absent. | (a) Bind `DATABASE_URL` straight to `spring.datasource.url`. (b) Require operators to supply JDBC-form settings and drop `DATABASE_URL`. (c) Set `spring.jpa.database-platform` to a dialect. (d) Set `spring.datasource.driver-class-name`. | A SQLAlchemy URL such as `postgresql://user:pass@host/db` is not a JDBC URL, so (a) fails at connection time; the source passed exactly that string to `create_engine` at `backend/app/db/database.py:L7`. (b) would change the operational contract this migration preserves. (c) and (d) would hardcode a vendor, which vendor-agnosticism forbids — Hibernate detects the dialect from JDBC connection metadata and the driver is resolved from the URL, which is precisely how "dialect-agnostic" is honoured with both drivers on the classpath. | A translation step is a piece of security-relevant parsing this project owns; its behaviour is pinned by an exhaustive case set covering the six agreed cases plus every credential-bearing and no-normalisation shape. Because no dialect is declared, a wrong `DATABASE_URL` surfaces as a driver-level failure at first connection rather than as a configuration error at binding time. |
| DL-028 | Both `org.postgresql:postgresql` and `com.mysql:mysql-connector-j` are declared at `runtime` scope in one artifact, and they are the whole set of vendors `config/DatabaseUrlTranslator` resolves a scheme onto. The delivered versions are 42.7.13 and 26.7.0: the PostgreSQL driver takes the `postgresql.version` property this POM overrides (DL-169) and Connector/J is versioned on the coordinate itself because `spring-boot-dependencies:3.5.16` publishes no property for it (DL-241). This entry decides only that *both* drivers ship and that they ship at `runtime`; which versions they carry and why is DL-170's subject, which owns it for every coordinate. | (a) Declare only the driver a given deployment needs, selected by a Maven profile. (b) Declare them at `compile` scope. (c) Leave both coordinates entirely to the BOM, which an earlier revision of this entry described as delivered — that revision also stated the POM overrode no version property at all, which the file contradicts. (d) Add `com.h2database:h2` as a third runtime driver so an H2 URL is servable in production. | `DATABASE_URL` is a single opaque string resolved at runtime, so the artifact cannot know at build time which vendor it will face; a single jar that serves either URL is what vendor-agnosticism requires. `runtime` scope keeps both drivers off the compile classpath, so no application class can import a vendor type by accident. (a) would produce vendor-specific artifacts and a build-time decision the configuration is meant to defer. (b) would make that accident possible. (c) is what the BOM alone would give — 42.7.11 and 9.7.0 — and it is not what the file declares: both coordinates carry a raise to a patch release with a published security fix, which is DL-170's standing position. (d) is rejected by DL-242 and DL-071: H2 stays `test`-scope and the translator resolves no scheme onto it, so the scheme set and the packaged drivers name the same two vendors. | Two drivers ship in every image, one of which is always unused, which adds a few megabytes and two more CVE surfaces to track. Because both are raised above the BOM, each override has to be revisited when the framework version moves; `BuildDependencyContractTest` asserts the declared and resolved state so a stale override is a test failure rather than a silent divergence. A `DATABASE_URL` naming any other vendor, H2 included, is rejected at startup by the translator rather than silently unsupported. |
| DL-029 | `src/main/resources/application.yml` sets `server.port: ${PORT:5000}`, so the service listens on 5000 unless the environment injects `PORT`. | (a) Leave Spring Boot's default of 8080. (b) Hardcode 5000 with no override. (c) Set 8080 and change the Dockerfile's `EXPOSE`. | Flask defaulted to 5000 at `backend/app/main.py:L53` and the container exposes 5000 at `infrastructure/docker/Dockerfile.backend:L17`, so 5000 is the observable contract; without an explicit setting the port silently changes and the exposed port stops matching the listener. Honouring an injected `PORT` is what Cloud Run requires, and because 5000 is preserved no Terraform or Cloud Run change is triggered. (b) would break on a platform that injects `PORT`. (c) would ripple into the Dockerfile and the deployment for no gain. | Two mechanisms can set the port, so a `PORT` variable left over in an environment silently overrides the default. Port 5000 collides with some desktop services, which is a local-development annoyance rather than a deployment issue. |
| DL-030 | `spring.main.web-application-type` is set explicitly to `servlet`. | (a) Omit it and let Spring resolve the type. (b) Set `reactive` and run the whole application on WebFlux. | Both `spring-boot-starter-web` and `spring-boot-starter-webflux` are on the classpath, because `WebClient` is needed for the long-lived X stream while the eleven routes are ordinary blocking handlers. Spring already resolves that combination to `SERVLET`, so this setting changes nothing — it makes an otherwise implicit and surprising resolution explicit, which is what an explicit web-application type asks for. (b) would rewrite every controller and repository interaction into a reactive pipeline, which is a redesign this migration forbids. | The setting looks redundant and could be "tidied away" by someone who does not know both starters are present; removing it would restore the implicitness rather than change behaviour. Carrying both starters means two web stacks are initialised on the classpath even though only one serves requests. |
| DL-031 | The three Twitter drift keys are declared as aliases with nested placeholder defaults pointing at the canonical pair: `api-secret-key: ${TWITTER_API_SECRET_KEY:${TWITTER_API_SECRET:}}`, `consumer-key: ${TWITTER_CONSUMER_KEY:${TWITTER_API_KEY:}}`, `consumer-secret: ${TWITTER_CONSUMER_SECRET:${TWITTER_API_SECRET:}}`. | (a) Declare all five as independent keys with no defaults, so each must be supplied. (b) Declare only the canonical pair and drop the three drift names. (c) Resolve the aliasing in Java, inside `ScannerProperties`. | `backend/app/services/twitter_service.py:L12` reads `TWITTER_API_SECRET_KEY` and `backend/app/tasks/tweet_monitoring.py:L46-47` read `TWITTER_CONSUMER_KEY` and `TWITTER_CONSUMER_SECRET`, while `backend/app/core/config.py:L5-6` declares only the canonical pair — so all three names must resolve for configuration completeness to be met. The aliases are sound because they name the same credentials: X's developer portal labels them "API Key (Consumer Key)" and "API Secret Key (Consumer Secret)". (a) would demand three duplicate secrets. (b) would leave the drift open. (c) would hide the relationship from anyone reading the configuration. The nested-default technique was verified empirically against Spring Boot 3.5.16 on JDK 21 rather than assumed. | An operator who sets only one of an alias pair gets the canonical value silently, which is the intent but is not obvious from an environment listing. Every Twitter key carries an empty-string default so the context refreshes without them, which defers a missing-credential failure to the first API call. |
| DL-032 | Reply generation calls the OpenAI Chat Completions API, replacing `Completion.create(engine="text-davinci-002", ...)` at `backend/app/services/llm_service.py:L19-26`. | (a) The legacy `/v1/completions` endpoint the source used. (b) The Responses API. | The completions-endpoint models the source named were shut down on 2024-01-04, and the stated replacement `gpt-3.5-turbo-instruct` is itself scheduled for retirement, so the legacy endpoint is a dead end for new work. Chat Completions is the surface every currently offered general-purpose model serves, and every parameter of the source call maps across one-to-one: `max_tokens` becomes `max_completion_tokens` (DL-034), while `temperature` and `n` keep both their names and their transcribed 0.7 and 1 values. The Responses API is documented as the successor to and a superset of Chat Completions, but adopting it would change the request and response shape for no behaviour this port needs — the single call site sends one user message and reads one choice. | A later move to the Responses API is a change confined to this adapter. Chat Completions returns a list of choices, so the first is read and any others ignored, which is correct only while `n` is 1; `n` is configuration, so a value above 1 would silently discard output. |
| DL-033 | The OpenAI model is configuration, `scanner.openai.model`, with a documented default rather than a hardcoded identifier. | (a) Hardcode a specific model in `LlmService`. (b) Keep `text-davinci-002`, the identifier the source named. (c) Omit the property and rely on the SDK's default. | OpenAI retires models in dated waves — the GPT-3 and GPT-3.5 completions models were shut down on 2024-01-04, `gpt-3.5-turbo-instruct`, `babbage-002` and `davinci-002` are scheduled for 2026-09-28, and the `gpt-3.5-turbo` and `gpt-4` lines for 2026-10-23 — so any hardcoded identifier has a known expiry and a retirement would require a code change and a redeploy. (b) is not available: the model no longer exists. (c) makes the model invisible and unpinnable. | The default will itself age and must be revisited on OpenAI's published retirement schedule; a stale value produces a runtime API error rather than a startup failure. Different models produce different response quality and cost, so changing the property changes behaviour without any code review. |
| DL-034 | The token-budget property is named `scanner.openai.max-completion-tokens`, carries the source's value of **150** as its default in `ScannerProperties.Openai`, in `application.yml` and in `application-test.yml`, and is passed as the SDK's `maxCompletionTokens`. | (a) Keep the source's name `max_tokens` and the SDK's older parameter. (b) Drop the cap entirely. (c) Raise the cap above the source literal, which an earlier revision of this entry and of DL-202 did. | `backend/app/services/llm_service.py:L22` set `max_tokens=150`; `max_completion_tokens` is the modern parameter name in the current API and is what the official SDK's builder exposes, verified by compiling the call during analysis. Keeping the number at 150 preserves behaviour while the name tracks the API, and 150 is a configuration default to be transcribed verbatim. (a) sends a parameter the current API no longer accepts for these models. (b) and (c) change the generated output, which is a business-behaviour change nobody requested; (c) was additionally justified only by a reasoning budget that no longer applies, because `scanner.openai.reasoning-effort` now defaults to `none` (DL-145), so the whole 150-token budget reaches visible output (DL-202). | The property name no longer matches the retired source's name, so a reader diffing the two has to know they are the same knob — which is what this entry records. 150 tokens truncates a long reply, exactly as the source did, and a truncated reply arrives as `finish_reason` `length`, which DL-202 makes legible. |
| DL-035 | The prompt template at `backend/app/services/llm_service.py:L16` is preserved verbatim — segments, punctuation and newlines — as named constants that are concatenated, and the post body is interpolated into it verbatim: no character is folded, escaped or cut, and a `null` body interpolates as the empty string. The unresolvable `{tweet.context}` placeholder is populated deterministically from the post's `ai_tools_mentioned` and `doubt_rating`; that value, and that value alone, is bounded — DL-265 owns its bound. `LlmServiceTest` asserts a five-thousand-character body and an embedded CRLF both reaching the prompt unchanged. | (a) Reword or restructure the prompt. (b) Drop the `Context:` clause, since the value it interpolated never existed. (c) Add a system message describing the assistant. (d) Fold the body's line breaks and cut it to 1,000 characters with a trailing ellipsis, which an earlier revision of this entry and of DL-203 described as delivered. | The prompt is the behavioural contract of this feature, so any edit to it changes the generated text; holding the segments as constants makes that contract explicit and leaves the apostrophe inside the quoted body exactly as the source left it. The source interpolated `{tweet.content}` directly, so verbatim interpolation is the faithful port and is what the migration boundary requires. `{tweet.context}` cannot be resolved as written, because `backend/app/schema/tweet.py:L5-14` declares no `context` attribute, so the placeholder had to be filled from something: the two fields that record why a post was captured at all are the tools it mentions and its doubt rating, both already present on the DTO, which makes the value deterministic and reproducible rather than invented per call. (b) would drop a clause the template itself declares. (c) would send instructions the source never sent. (d) is the departure two entries once claimed and the tree never had: no folding or truncation code existed, the constant it referred to was never read, and the delivered tests assert the opposite — so the claim is withdrawn here and in DL-203 rather than implemented, because bounding third-party text inside the prompt is a change to the ported contract and the request cost is already bounded by `scanner.openai.max-completion-tokens`. | The `Context:` value is an interpretation of an attribute that never existed, so it is the one part of the prompt that is not a transcription. It is confined to a single private method, so revising it touches one place. A very long post produces a proportionally long prompt, so the request cost of a generation is a function of the stored body; nothing truncates it, exactly as nothing truncated it in the source, and the completion side is bounded by configuration. DL-203 carries the prompt-injection exposure that verbatim interpolation implies. |
| DL-036 | `SentimentAnalysisService` exposes `analyzeSentiment(String text)` returning a `double`, invoked with the tweet's content. | (a) `analyzeSentiment(TweetDto)` reading a field, as the source method signature suggests. (b) Add the `analyze(...)` method the controller called, alongside the real one. (c) Return a structured result carrying the score, magnitude and language. | Both sides of this call are broken in the source, in complementary ways: `backend/app/api/tweets.py:L46` calls `sentiment_analysis.analyze(tweet.content)`, a method that does not exist, while the real method `analyze_sentiment(tweet)` reads `tweet.text` at `backend/app/services/sentiment_analysis.py:L14`, a field the schema does not have. What the two agree on is "the text of the tweet", so taking a `String` is the resolution that honours both. (a) would preserve the non-existent field. (b) would ship two names for one behaviour. (c) is a contract expansion — see DL-037. | The service no longer knows which tweet it is analysing, so a log record cannot name the row; the caller holds that context. Magnitude and language are discarded, so a future feature needing them changes this signature. |
| DL-037 | `POST /tweets/{tweetId}/analyze` returns `{"tweet_id": <the path value as received>, "analysis_result": <the sentiment score>}`, and `analysis_result` is the score as a floating-point number. | (a) Return a composite object carrying both the score and the derived doubt rating. (b) Return the doubt rating instead of the score. (c) Return the full Google response. | `backend/app/api/tweets.py:L52-55` returns exactly two keys, and the only analyze method that exists returns a single value, so a single number is faithful to the source contract. (a) and (c) expand a contract the wire contract requires preserved. (b) would silently change what the key means. The persisted side effect still sets `tweets.doubt_rating` through the transcribed formula, so nothing is lost — it is simply not echoed. | A client that wants the doubt rating has to read the tweet back. `tweet_id` echoes the path value verbatim, including a value that matched no row, which is deliberate: the handler answers 404 in that case, so the echo is only ever seen for a real row. |
| DL-038 | The pagination envelope keys are `page`, `per_page`, `total` and `total_pages`, derived from Spring Data's `Page` as `getNumber() + 1`, `getSize()`, `getTotalElements()` and `getTotalPages()`. `page` is 1-based on the wire. | (a) Expose Spring Data's own serialised `Page` shape. (b) Use `pageNumber`/`pageSize`/`totalElements`/`totalPages`. (c) Keep the wire 0-based, matching Spring Data. | `backend/app/api/tweets.py:L18-21` and `backend/app/api/responses.py:L17-20` both return a `pagination` object, but the producing methods never existed, so the internal keys are undefined in the source and the frontend types the response as a bare array — there is no evidence either way and the keys must be chosen. `snake_case` follows DL-022, and the four names are the minimum that describes a page. (a) leaks a framework shape and is unstable across Boot versions. (b) is `camelCase`. (c) would contradict `page` defaulting to 1 at `backend/app/api/tweets.py:L12`, which is a preserved query-parameter default. | The off-by-one between the wire and Spring Data is a standing trap on every paginated route; it is converted in one place per controller and covered by a test. Because the keys were invented, a future client contract has to be derived from this entry rather than from the source. |
| DL-039 | `GET /settings` returns a JSON **array** of `{key, value, description}` objects. | (a) A key-to-value map, as `backend/tests/test_api.py:L37-39` expects. (b) A map of key to a nested object. (c) An envelope such as `{"settings": [...]}`. | The array of three-field objects is the only shape that carries all three columns the `settings` table actually has at `backend/app/db/models.py:L40-44`, and it matches `frontend/src/schema/setting.ts:L3-7`, the sole typed contract for this shape. The contrary test evidence is explicitly discounted: that test requests `/settings/` with a trailing slash rather than the registered route, drives FastAPI's `TestClient` against a Flask application, and its sibling `PUT`s to `/settings/`, which is not a route at all — the file cannot execute. A map shape would also silently drop `description`. | A client that wanted `settings["auto_response"]` has to index the array itself. Discounting an in-repository test expectation is a judgement call, recorded here so it reads as a decision rather than an oversight. |
| DL-040 | The popularity gate is declared in two overloads so a caller handling many records may resolve the threshold once per cycle rather than once per record — see DL-255. `SettingsService` seeds exactly three `settings` rows — `tweet_popularity_threshold`, `response_generation_delay` and `stream_keywords` — insert-if-absent on `ApplicationReadyEvent`, each with a `description`, and a key already present is never overwritten. This establishes the precedence contract that a `Setting` row overrides the corresponding configuration default. | (a) Seed nothing and leave the table empty. (b) Make `PUT /settings/{key}` an upsert so an unknown key creates its own row. (c) Seed from a `data.sql` or `import.sql` script. (d) Seed from a `@PostConstruct` method. (e) Seed a wider set including the `auto_response` key `backend/tests/test_api.py:L37-39` names. (f) Overwrite the three rows on every start so configuration always wins. | Nothing in the source tree ever creates a `settings` row: there is no `Base.metadata.create_all()`, no migration, no fixture and no seed path anywhere, so the table starts empty. `backend/app/api/settings.py:L21-22` then returns 404 for any key that is not already present, which makes 404 the *only* reachable outcome of `PUT /settings/{key}` and leaves `GET /settings` rendering an empty array permanently — two of the eleven preserved routes would be preserved in name only and nothing in the system would be runtime-configurable, contradicting the instruction to complete rather than merely port. Insert-only is what lets an operator's edit through `PUT /settings/{key}` survive a restart, which is the behaviour `documentation/Code Structure.md:L1078` describes for `check_popularity_threshold` ("Get popularity threshold from settings") and what the SRS requires at L57 (customizable popularity thresholds), L319 / F005-2 (Threshold Management) and L766 (the glossary's configurable Popularity Threshold). Three keys is the smallest set that covers the two defaulted properties at `backend/app/core/config.py:L10-11` plus the keyword set DL-044 makes operator-editable. (b) would change the `PUT` contract from update to upsert, which the route this migration preserves does not do. (c) moves row content out of the class that owns the key names and out of the transaction the service layer demarcates. (d) is not merely stylistic: `@Transactional` has no effect on a `@PostConstruct` method because the transactional proxy does not exist when it runs, so the seed would execute outside a transaction; `ApplicationReadyEvent` fires after the context is refreshed, and it does not fire in a `@WebMvcTest` or `@DataJpaTest` slice, so test fixtures stay deterministic. (e) is refused for the reasons recorded under DL-039. (f) defeats runtime configurability entirely. | Three rows appear in a database the operator did not populate, and a row deleted deliberately outside the application reappears on the next start. The seeded value duplicates the configuration default at the moment of first start and then diverges silently: a later change to `scanner.popularity-threshold` does not reach a row that already exists. `stream_keywords` is stored as one comma-delimited string, so a keyword containing a comma cannot be expressed. The seed adds three `existsById` reads to every start. Two instances starting at the same moment can both observe a key as absent and both attempt the insert. The loser's insert is rejected on the primary key, and `seedIfAbsent` then re-reads the key: a key the winner has committed is treated as seeded, recorded at `DEBUG` and the start continues, while a rejection that leaves the key still absent is raised and that instance does not start. Both outcomes are reachable on Cloud Run, whose `scaling_parameters` allow up to ten instances. Measured correction to a common assumption: `ApplicationReadyEvent` is published by `SpringBootContextLoader` too, so a `@DataJpaTest` slice that imports this bean begins from the committed three-row baseline rather than an empty table; a slice asserting on `settings` must expect those rows. Because the seed is insert-only that baseline is deterministic, and a `@WebMvcTest` slice is unaffected because it holds no `DataSource`. That correction is now an executed expectation rather than a note: `service/SettingsServiceSeedingIntegrationTest` runs a real Boot context over H2, observes the three rows the startup event already committed, republishes `ApplicationReadyEvent` and asserts that the row count and every stored value are unchanged and that an operator's edit survives — so single-instance idempotence and the no-overwrite contract are proven against the real event rather than a directly invoked listener method. The concurrent-start collision above stays the residual limit in its second form only: a rejected insert whose key is present afterwards is absorbed, while a rejection over a key that is still absent — a rejection with any other cause — stops that instance from starting. It is a property of two processes racing on one table, so no single-process test covers the racing half, and the operational mitigation is a single instance during the first start or a pre-seeded table. Seeding is an addition the source did not perform at all, recorded here so it reads as a decision rather than scope creep. |
| DL-041 | The analytics summary returns `total_tweets`, `total_responses`, `approved_responses`, `pending_responses`, `average_doubt_rating`, `average_like_count` and `tracked_ai_tools`. `total_responses` and `approved_responses` come from the one closed projection `ResponseRepository.findApprovalCounts()`, and `pending_responses` is their difference; the tweet count, both tweet averages and the tool count come from `TweetRepository.findAggregates()` and `AiToolRepository.count()`. The three statements run inside one repeatable-read service transaction (DL-091, DL-180). | (a) Return only the two keys the source test names. (b) Read total and approved responses through one custom projection query. (c) Issue field-specific native SQL for every metric. (d) Return a generic `{metric, value}` list. (e) Add per-tool and per-author breakdowns. | `total_tweets` and `total_responses` are evidenced by name at `backend/tests/test_api.py:L50-51`. The other five are the complete aggregates the four frozen tables can answer without a new column. For responses, the inherited total count and Spring Data's derived true-value count state their contracts directly and need no custom query or projection; `false` and `null` are excluded from the approved count, so their rows remain pending. The surrounding repeatable-read transaction gives the separate statements one snapshot, preserving `approved_responses <= total_responses` without coupling the repository to an analytics-specific tuple. (a) leaves an endpoint that answers almost nothing. (b) saves one statement but adds a projection and duplicates the inherited count operation. (c) gives up vendor-neutral JPQL derivation. (d) makes the response untypable. (e) adds grouping over unindexed columns and expands the contract. | Three aggregate statements read unindexed columns and hold a repeatable-read snapshot for the duration of the summary call; cost grows with table size, and adding an index is out of scope. A database that cannot provide repeatable-read semantics would weaken the cross-statement invariant. The seven-key set and the treatment of `null` approval as pending are now wire contracts, so changing either is breaking. |
| DL-042 | `GET /analytics/trends` returns `{"trends": [ {date, tweet_count, average_doubt_rating, total_likes}, … ]}`, bucketed by day over `tweets.created_at`, across a window of `scanner.analytics.trend-window-days` (default 30). No query parameter is introduced. | (a) Accept `start_date` and `end_date` query parameters, as `backend/tests/test_api.py:L55-59` probes. (b) Bucket by week or month. (c) Return every day since the first tweet. (d) Make the window a `Setting` row rather than a property. | `get_trends()` takes no arguments at `backend/app/api/analytics.py:L14`, so adding a query parameter would change the route contract this migration preserves; the contrary test is itself annotated as possibly needing adjustment and cannot execute. A configured window is therefore the only way to bound the series. Day buckets match `documentation/Technical Specifications.md:L440-441`, which describes the interface as trend visualisations and sentiment over time, and the SRS Feature 7 / F007-1 requirement to aggregate tweet data for trend analysis. (c) makes the response size unbounded. (d) is a reasonable future step but the window is an operator-tuning knob, not a product setting, and DL-040 keeps the seeded set to three product keys. | The window cannot be varied per request, so a client wanting 90 days needs a redeploy or a configuration change. Thirty daily buckets is a fixed response size that will not grow, which is the point, but it also means older data is simply invisible through this route. |
| DL-043 | `SettingsService` is an injected singleton `@Service` whose operations are instance methods; it declares no static member other than its logger and its private constants, and controllers reach it by constructor injection. | (a) Keep the source's call shape literally, as Java `static` methods on the class. (b) A static utility holder that receives a repository handed in per call. (c) A `SettingsService` interface plus a `SettingsServiceImpl`. (d) Field or setter injection instead of a constructor. | `backend/app/api/settings.py:L10,L20` invokes `SettingsService.get_all_settings()` and `SettingsService.update_setting(key, new_value)` statically on the class, and that shape cannot carry the three collaborators the behaviour needs — a repository, a mapper and bound configuration. The conversion is not cosmetic: a `static` method cannot be proxied, so `@Transactional` would not apply to it and the transaction boundary this layer owns would not exist, and it cannot be replaced by a mock in a `@WebMvcTest` slice. The injection rule converts every per-request instantiation and every module-level `get_settings()` read in the source to constructor injection, and this class follows the same rule as the four ported services, so the seven service beans are uniform. (c) would add a type with exactly one implementation and no second caller. (d) hides required collaborators from the constructor signature and permits a partially constructed bean. | The call shape differs from the source, so a reader diffing the Python controller against the Java controller sees an injected field where a class-qualified call stood; the method names and parameter order are unchanged. Any future caller must obtain the bean from the context rather than calling through the class, which is a visible constraint on utility-style reuse. |
| DL-044 | The composed term set is additionally validated against a literal grammar, bounded to the provider rule cap and drawn from a projection of the `name` column alone — see DL-254 and DL-257. The streaming rule set is the configured base terms in `scanner.ingestion.stream-base-keywords` — `AI coding tool`, `AI code assistant`, `AI generated code`, `GPT-4` — unioned with every `ai_tools.name` row, and the whole set is overridable by the `stream_keywords` setting row. | (a) Keep the source's empty list. (b) Hardcode a keyword list in Java. (c) Derive the set from `ai_tools` alone. (d) Derive it from configuration alone. | `backend/app/tasks/tweet_monitoring.py:L53-55` leaves `keywords = []` behind a deferred-work comment and then calls `stream.filter(track=keywords)`; an empty track list is invalid, so ingestion could never start. A keyword set therefore cannot be ported and must be designed. Scanning all four documentation artifacts for named AI coding tools returns only "GPT-4" alongside the generic phrase "AI coding tools", so the base terms are traceable to the documentation's own vocabulary rather than invented. Unioning `ai_tools.name` makes the set grow without a redeploy, which is what that table exists for, and the setting override keeps it operator-editable through the existing `PUT /settings/{key}` route. (c) yields an empty set on a fresh database, reproducing the original defect. (d) makes `ai_tools` decorative. | The default terms are broad, so the stream will match posts that are not about skepticism at all; filtering happens downstream through the popularity gate and the doubt rating. Multi-word terms must be quoted per the X rule syntax, so a term added to `ai_tools.name` with unusual punctuation could produce an invalid rule. A rule set assembled from three sources is harder to reason about than a literal list. |
| DL-045 | Rule reconciliation compares each registered rule's tag as well as its expression and sends its mutations in batches — see DL-261 and DL-254.
