The four unpinned references remain the source branch's exposure — a compromised or breaking upstream release reaches this repository with no commit — and two of them are worse than stale: `8398a7/action-slack` is archived, and `setup-gcloud@v0.2.1` targets the retired `node12` runtime. None of that is newly introduced and none of it is repaired; DL-102 records why the workflow those two sit in never runs. The resolved release in the trailing comment is a claim about the SHA that no build verifies — the test requires the comment's shape, not its truth. |
| DL-104 | **Withdrawn.** This entry added a `permissions` block to both workflows — `contents: read` for CI, plus `id-token: write` for CD — set `persist-credentials: false` on every checkout, and moved Google Cloud authentication onto Workload Identity Federation. The delivered workflows carry none of it: neither declares `permissions`, both checkouts use the repository default, and `cd.yml` authenticates with `google-github-actions/setup-gcloud@v0.2.1` reading `secrets.GCP_SA_KEY`, exactly as the source branch wrote it. | (a) Keep the least-privilege blocks as delivered. (b) Add the `permissions` block to CI only, since the backend job is the one this migration edits. (c) Keep WIF but drop the `permissions` blocks. | A `permissions` key, a checkout input and a change of cloud credential mechanism are three edits outside the one build line `cd.yml` is authorised to change and outside the five backend steps `ci.yml` is authorised to change. The frozen plan states the `ci.yml` delivery as "Backend job only … All frontend steps untouched" and the `cd.yml` delivery as "Line 36 only … Nothing else changes"; a workflow-level key belongs to neither, and an input added to the pristine checkout step at `L14` belongs to neither. Each of (a) to (c) is a defensible hardening and each is a scope breach; the carve-out decides the question, not the merit. A second review has re-raised all three and asked for them again — least privilege, `persist-credentials: false`, and Workload Identity Federation in place of the long-lived key — and the exclusion is explicit enough to decide it a second time, so this row records the repeat rather than reversing. What that review asked for and this migration *can* reach is recorded separately: the pin of the one action reference the migration authored (DL-103) and the mandatory Node.js toolchain floor (DL-285). | Both jobs run with the repository's default token permissions, the checkout token stays in the work tree for later steps, and production deployment depends on a long-lived service-account JSON key in `secrets.GCP_SA_KEY`. That is the posture of the source branch, unchanged by this migration and unrepaired by it. The key's practical exposure is bounded by the workflow never running (DL-102), which is a property of a defect rather than a control: repairing the trigger makes this credential live in the same commit, so the two belong in one change. Widening the carve-out to admit `permissions: contents: read` is a two-line edit to `ci.yml` and needs no other change, which is the cheapest of the three to take up first. |
| DL-105 | **Withdrawn.** This entry checked out `workflow_run.head_sha`, tagged the image with that SHA, resolved the pushed digest, deployed the digest, and serialised production deployments through a `concurrency` group. The delivered `.github/workflows/cd.yml` carries the source-branch job: a default-ref checkout, `docker build -t $BACKEND_IMAGE`, `docker push $BACKEND_IMAGE` and `gcloud run deploy --image $BACKEND_IMAGE`, with no tag, no digest and no concurrency group. Only the `-f infrastructure/docker/Dockerfile.backend` flag is added (DL-056). | (a) Keep the digest deploy and the concurrency group. (b) Keep the commit-SHA tag alone, as the smallest of the three. (c) Keep serialisation alone. | Every element of this entry is a line the carve-out does not open. The image reference is a mutable tag with no version at all, which is the mechanism the source branch chose; changing it, adding a digest lookup and adding a `concurrency` key are three separate unauthorised edits, and a review recorded the delivered form of them as a critical scope breach. (a) to (c) are the same breach at three sizes. The frozen `cd.yml` carve-out is explicit about the same lines — it enumerates `BACKEND_IMAGE` at `L12`, `docker push` at `L37` and `gcloud run deploy … --image $BACKEND_IMAGE` at `L41-45` as unchanged — so a second review asking for a commit tag and a digest deploy is answered by the same exclusion rather than by a new judgement. The one place the mutable reference does get written down is the immutability the *container* achieves by other means: both base images in `infrastructure/docker/Dockerfile.backend` are digest-pinned (DL-106), so the bytes the build starts from are fixed even though the bytes it publishes are addressed by a movable name. | The deployed bytes are whatever `$BACKEND_IMAGE` resolves to at deploy time, not necessarily the commit CI tested; two runs can interleave; and a re-push under the same reference silently changes what a later deploy pulls. All three are properties of the source-branch workflow, and none is introduced by this migration. All three are also unreached, because the workflow never triggers (DL-102) — which means the first commit that repairs the trigger inherits every one of them at once, and the serialisation is the element that matters soonest, since two concurrent `workflow_run` completions on `main` would race with no lock of any kind. |
| DL-106 | Both Docker build stages use reviewed patch tags pinned to registry SHA-256 digests: `maven:3.9.16-eclipse-temurin-21@sha256:c07f7cc…` for the build stage and `eclipse-temurin:21.0.11_10-jre@sha256:8cef5fc…` for the runtime. **Digest-refresh cadence.** A digest is refreshed when one of four things happens, and not on a timer: an advisory is published that is reachable from a package this image actually executes; the Temurin quarterly critical patch update moves the Java runtime this image runs; a registry manifest is removed, which makes the pin unbuildable; or an unrelated change to the Dockerfile is made anyway, at which point the digests are re-resolved in the same commit. Each refresh re-resolves the digest from the readable patch tag and records the new value in the file's own comment beside it. **Triage.** An SBOM of both stages was resolved against advisory data at the review cutoff of 2026-08-03: 81 non-rejected advisories in the runtime stage and 105 in the build stage, **every one of them Medium or lower, with no High and no Critical**. **Applicability exceptions**, which are why that set is not remediated: the build stage does not ship — its only output crossing into the runtime is `target/*.jar` through `COPY --from=build`, so an advisory against a build-stage utility is reachable only by whoever runs the build, not by a deployed container; the runtime stage's own Java runtime is *replaced* rather than patched (DL-243), since the pinned `21.0.11_10` JRE at the base image's `JAVA_HOME` is deleted and overwritten with the checksum-verified Temurin 21.0.12+8 archive, so advisories against the base image's JRE do not describe the runtime that executes; the process runs as the unprivileged `10001:10001` with the jar at mode `0640` it cannot rewrite (DL-107); and the remaining affected components are base-operating-system utilities — shells, archive and TLS command-line tools — that no application code path invokes, the one exception being `curl`, which the `HEALTHCHECK` runs against `127.0.0.1` with a 4-second bound and no attacker-supplied input (DL-218). | (a) Floating Java 21 tags. (b) Patch tags without digests. (c) Vendor a JDK into the repository. (d) Refresh both digests on a fixed schedule. (e) Move to a smaller base image — `alpine`, `distroless` or a `jlink` custom runtime — to shrink the advisory count. (f) Remediate the 186 advisories by upgrading the affected operating-system packages in a `RUN apt-get upgrade` layer. | Tags can be repointed; a digest fixes the exact toolchain and operating-system layer used for an attested build while retaining a readable patch tag. (d) would rewrite a pin with no finding behind it and defeat the reproducibility the pin exists for. (e) is the change with the largest advisory reduction and the largest behaviour risk: `alpine` swaps glibc for musl under a JVM, `distroless` removes the shell the `CMD` and the `HEALTHCHECK` both invoke, and a `jlink` runtime fixes the module set at build time — each is an infrastructure redesign, none is named by any row of the frozen file inventory, and none is warranted by a set with no High or Critical member. (f) makes every build depend on whatever the distribution mirror serves that day, which is the opposite of a pinned base image, and it would not reduce the reachable set, which the exceptions above already show is close to empty. | A digest pin does not update itself, so base-image security updates require an explicit refresh commit and nothing in the repository proposes one (DL-276) — the cadence above is a policy a reader follows, not a mechanism the build enforces. A removed registry manifest prevents rebuilding until the pin is updated. The applicability exceptions are a judgement made at one cutoff against one advisory set: a future advisory in a component the exceptions call unreached would have to be re-triaged rather than assumed covered, and the runtime-replacement exception holds only while `DL-243`'s pinned archive is the runtime that actually executes, which `OperationsContractTest` asserts by requiring the version check in the same `RUN`. The build stage runs `mvn -B dependency:go-offline` and `mvn -B clean package` against the network, so a build-stage compromise reaches the artifact even though a build-stage advisory does not reach the deployed container. |
| DL-107 | The runtime image creates UID/GID 10001 and executes Java as `10001:10001`. The application directory and the jar are owned by root and readable by that group — `/app` at mode `0750` and `/app/app.jar` at mode `0640` — so the runtime identity reads the artifact and cannot replace it; the ownership and mode decision is DL-249. | (a) Run as the image's default root user. (b) Use an arbitrary named user supplied by the base image. (c) Give the runtime identity ownership of the application directory, which an earlier form of this entry recorded and DL-249 withdrew. | The JVM needs to read the jar and write only to its normal temporary locations; it needs neither root nor write access to the application artifact. A fixed numeric identity is stable under Kubernetes security policies. (c) left the directory writable by the runtime identity, which is enough to unlink and replace a root-owned file. | A future feature that writes beneath `/app` fails until it receives an explicitly owned data path; the JVM's temporary directory remains `/tmp`, which the base image leaves writable. |
| DL-108 | JWT configuration, signing and verification accept HS256 only; the JJWT parser's signature registry contains only `Jwts.SIG.HS256`. | (a) Accept HS384 and HS512 with the same key. (b) Keep the parser's default algorithm registry. (c) add asymmetric algorithms. | Minting already produced HS256. Restricting configuration and parser policy to that one algorithm removes algorithm-policy drift and makes a token signed under another same-key HMAC algorithm invalid. | Moving to another algorithm becomes a coordinated configuration, key-management and parser change rather than a property-only change. |
| DL-109 | Accepted JWTs carry exactly `sub`, `iat` and `exp`, with a nonblank subject and `exp` strictly after `iat`; parsing occurs once per validation attempt. | (a) Accept missing or extra claims. (b) Parse once per accessor. (c) normalize a blank subject. | These are the complete claims minted by the service and the only principal and lifetime data its filter consumes. Exact validation prevents indefinite or ambiguous signed tokens and avoids inconsistent results from repeated parsing. | Adding a registered claim such as `iss`, `aud` or `jti` requires updating both minting and the exact accepted-name set. |
| DL-110 | `scanner.jwt.expiration-minutes` must be between 1 and 60 inclusive, and seconds conversion and expiry arithmetic use checked operations. | (a) Permit zero or negative values. (b) Permit values above the documented one-hour maximum. (c) rely on overflowing arithmetic. | The technical specification fixes a one-hour maximum and a positive token lifetime. Startup validation makes invalid policy configuration fail before any token is issued. | Operators cannot use the property to create longer-lived development tokens and must change the policy deliberately to do so. |
| DL-111 | Token rejection and authentication-filter logging is a closed set of fixed sentences at `DEBUG`, and never includes token text, a claim value, a JWT parser message, an exception type or a principal. `security/JwtService.parseClaims` writes exactly one of five: a presented JWT did not verify; declared an algorithm this service does not accept; did not carry exactly the accepted claim set; carried a blank subject; carried an inconsistent issued-at and expiration pair. `security/JwtAuthenticationFilter` writes one when a request authenticates and one when a presented token names a principal the credential store does not hold. There is no reason code, no suppression count, no sampling interval and no `WARN` record on this path. | (a) Log the jjwt exception's message at `WARN`, which is what was delivered first. (b) Log the exception's simple class name alongside the sentence, so the failure category survives. (c) Replace the sentences with fixed reason codes carrying a per-reason suppressed count and a one-record-per-minute interval. (d) Log the subject on every accepted request. (e) Suppress all diagnostics. | Bearer material and subjects are attacker-influenced or identifying data, and an unauthenticated caller chooses both how many failures arrive and what the token contains. (a) writes caller-chosen text into a plain-text log at a level enabled by default — log forging and disclosure at once, since jjwt's malformed-token messages quote a fragment of the input — and it is what DL-093 recorded. (b) is defensible, since the class name comes from a closed set and cannot carry a newline, and was recorded by DL-176; it is not taken because the sentence already distinguishes the four post-signature cases, and the one case it merges — bad signature against malformed — is exactly the pair an attacker would probe. (c) is what DL-144 recorded and would add a counter and a clock to a class whose whole logging surface is five sentences behind an operator's own decision to enable `DEBUG`; the level answers the volume argument the codes existed for. (d) writes an identifying value on the success path. (e) removes the only signal that tokens are being rejected at all. | An operator cannot tell from the log which claim or which byte was wrong, nor whether a rejection was a bad signature or a malformed token, so diagnosing one client requires reproducing its request with `DEBUG` enabled for `com.codeskeptic.scanner.security`. Records cannot be tied to one request without an external correlation facility (DL-198). `security/JwtServiceTest` attaches a Logback list appender and asserts the exact sentence, the `DEBUG` level, the absence of a throwable and that neither the token nor the library message appears, so a future edit that reintroduces either fails the build. |
| DL-112 | The JWT filter and security chain share one `RequestAttributeSecurityContextRepository`; the filter saves successful authentication and runs on REQUEST, ASYNC and ERROR dispatches. | (a) Set only the thread-local context. (b) skip redispatches. (c) use an HTTP session repository. | Spring Security authorizes each registered dispatch. Persisting the context on the request keeps authentication available to later dispatches while retaining stateless session policy. | Each dispatch can repeat token validation, and request-attribute persistence does not survive a second HTTP request. |
| DL-113 | The HTTP authentication scheme name `Bearer` is matched case-insensitively and must be followed by one space and nonempty token text. | (a) Match exact case. (b) accept arbitrary whitespace. (c) parse any authorization scheme as a bearer token. | HTTP authentication scheme names are case-insensitive, while a strict separator keeps token extraction deterministic. | Noncanonical headers with tabs or multiple leading spaces are rejected even if another implementation might accept them. |
| DL-114 | Spring Security logout is disabled. | (a) Keep the framework's permit-all `/logout`. (b) implement token revocation. (c) leave logout enabled for POST only. | The service uses stateless custom JWTs and has no revocation store, so the framework logout endpoint cannot invalidate a token and would create a second public route outside the declared surface. | Clients have no server-side logout and must discard their token locally or wait for expiry. |
| DL-115 | Every protected unauthenticated response is an empty 401 carrying `WWW-Authenticate: Bearer`. | (a) A bare 401 without a challenge. (b) a JSON error envelope. (c) a container-generated error page. | The challenge advertises the authentication scheme, and an explicit empty response preserves the closed set of source error strings. | Clients receive no detail distinguishing an absent token from an invalid or expired one. |
| DL-116 | `scanner.auth.password-hash` must be a 60-character `$2a$`, `$2b$` or `$2y$` bcrypt modular-crypt value with cost 10 through 14. | (a) Accept any nonblank text. (b) accept every legal bcrypt cost. (c) validate on the first login. | Shape validation catches misconfiguration at context creation, and the cost range excludes both weak and operationally excessive values without reproducing the hash in diagnostics. | A hash generated outside the accepted cost window cannot be used until policy changes, even though a bcrypt implementation could verify it. |
| DL-117 | `AuthController` maps only credential-rejection exceptions to empty 401; authentication-service and other infrastructure exceptions propagate to the generic 500 envelope. | (a) Catch every `AuthenticationException` as 401. (b) expose provider messages. (c) add a new 503 wire contract. | A broken provider is a server fault, not evidence that the submitted credential is wrong. Keeping the existing generic 500 avoids inventing a new source-incompatible response body. | Clients cannot distinguish a transient authentication backend outage from another internal failure. |
| DL-118 | `POST /auth/token` accepts at most 4096 encoded request bytes and at most 256 characters in each credential field, returning the same empty 401 before authentication when either bound is exceeded. **This bound applies to that one route and to no other.** An earlier revision also placed a general 65,536-byte ceiling on every other body-bearing request through a second nested filter registered behind `AuthorizationFilter`; that filter, its constant, its bodyless-method set and its registration are deleted, and the eleven pre-existing routes now carry no body-size bound of this chain's making. Because the size filter has to read the body to measure it, an accepted body is replayed to Spring MVC through `security/SecurityConfig.CachedBodyRequest`, which serves the buffered bytes from `getInputStream()` and decodes them in `getReader()` with the charset the request declared, falling back to UTF-8 when the request declares none, one this JVM does not provide, or one that is not a legal charset name. | (a) No bounds at all. (b) Bean Validation with a 400 envelope. (c) Reject only after bcrypt. (d) Bound the body without replaying it, leaving the consumed stream for the converter. (e) Let `Charset.forName` raise for a charset the JVM cannot supply. (f) Keep the general 65,536-byte ceiling on the eleven pre-existing routes and record the divergence here, which is what an earlier revision did. (g) Keep a general ceiling at a much larger value. | The public route otherwise allows unbounded allocation and expensive credential work. A pre-deserialization byte limit and a post-binding character limit contain both costs while preserving the route's generic rejection behavior and validation-parity rule. Both bounds are confined to this route because it is net-new: nothing in the retired tree served it, so no parity contract is disturbed by bounding it. (f) and (g) extend a bound onto the eleven routes the migration preserves, where the retired Pydantic schemas and the retired `String` columns declared no length, no pattern and no range at all — the validation-parity requirement admits required-versus-optional and nothing more, so a length ceiling on a preserved route is a new restriction on an existing contract whatever value it carries. A body one of those routes would have accepted is now accepted, and a body large enough to matter is bounded by the container and by `server.max-http-request-header-size` rather than by this chain. (d) would leave the handler an empty body for every accepted request, so replay is a consequence of the bound rather than a separate decision. (e) would raise `UnsupportedCharsetException` or `IllegalCharsetNameException` — both unchecked, both from a caller-supplied `Content-Type` parameter — from inside the filter chain, where no `@ExceptionHandler` can answer them; the fallback keeps a caller from choosing the failure mode. | The two bounds are service policy not present in the retired schemas; exceptionally long legitimate credentials cannot authenticate. Withdrawing the general ceiling reopens the exposure it closed: a body-bearing request on an authenticated route may carry as much as the container accepts, and the converter allocates it, so an authenticated caller can force a large allocation. The controls that remain are authentication itself, the container's own limits and the deployment's ingress; a deployment that needs a body ceiling on those routes has to set one there. A body declaring an unusable charset is decoded as UTF-8 rather than rejected, so a caller that meant a different encoding sees its credential fail to authenticate rather than a diagnostic; the substitution is recorded once at `WARN`. |
| DL-119 | **Extended by DL-203, which adds the two echoing renderings and applies the policy to every caller-supplied value.** `util/LogSafe` renders an identifier as `hmac256:` plus the first eight bytes of an **HMAC-SHA-256 keyed with a 32-byte secret this process draws from `SecureRandom` at class initialisation**, and renders failures by simple type name, by bounded cause chain (`typeChain`), by originating frame (`originFrame`) and by sanitized detail (`failureDetail`); service and error logs emit fixed metadata once without vendor messages or stacks. The key is never rendered, logged or configured, so a token is stable for the life of one process and unrelated to the token any other process renders for the same value. An earlier revision rendered an unkeyed `sha256:` truncation. | (a) Duplicate a private sanitizer in each class. (b) Log raw identifiers. (c) Use a random per-event token. (d) Log exception stacks at DEBUG. (e) Keep the unkeyed SHA-256 truncation, which is what the revision before this one delivered. (f) Key the HMAC from a configured environment secret so tokens join across replicas and restarts. (g) Omit the digest and write nothing in place of the identifier. (h) Widen the truncation beyond eight bytes. | One utility keeps the five remediated logging surfaces consistent, and type, chain, frame and status metadata is sufficient for the handled error category. On the keying: a security review recorded the unkeyed truncation as CWE-200 and CWE-359, because a digest of a low-entropy value is confirmable — a reader holding the log and a candidate list computes `sha256("4711")` and matches the record, and the same value renders identically in every environment, so events can be joined across deployments. A key the process draws itself removes both properties at no configuration cost while keeping the property the token exists for: within one process two records naming one value still join. (e) is the finding. (c) loses that within-process join, which is the whole purpose of the member, and its per-event value cannot group repeated occurrences of one failure. (f) is the stronger option for a multi-replica deployment and is not taken here: it introduces a mandatory secret with a rotation policy and a fail-behaviour when it is absent, which widens the configuration surface this migration caps, for a diagnostic convenience; it remains the documented extension path, a change confined to how `CORRELATION_KEY` is obtained. (g) leaves an operator unable to tell two failures apart. (h) reduces collisions but lengthens every record and does not address confirmability, which is the finding. | A 64-bit truncated token can still collide, keying adds a small per-record CPU cost, and stack-free handled failures provide less diagnostic detail. Two consequences of the keying are accepted deliberately: a token cannot be joined across a restart or between replicas, so an operator tracing one identifier through a multi-replica deployment sees a different token per process; and a token cannot be reproduced by anyone, including this project's own operators, from the value it stands for. The key lives only in memory, so a heap dump exposes it — an attacker holding one already holds far more. |
| DL-120 | `service/ResponseService` builds the wire record `dto/ResponseDto` only after the row is stored, so every returned record carries the database-generated identifier. There is no separate pre-persistence response type in the tree: model output is validated as non-blank inside `service/LlmService`, and an unusable reply raises before any row is written. | (a) Reuse `dto/ResponseDto` with `id = null` for the pre-persistence state. (b) Introduce an internal pre-persistence record beside the wire record, which an earlier revision of this entry recorded. (c) Fabricate an identifier before storing. (d) Store blank or absent model output as a successful draft. | A database-generated identifier does not exist before the insert, while the wire contract requires one, so the record is built after the insert and the question of a nullable identifier never arises. (a) would put a nullable identifier into the type the route serialises. (b) was recorded and withdrawn: it added a second record that had to evolve in step with the wire record for no behaviour the single record does not already give, since validity is decided at the adapter boundary and storage is the only step that assigns an identifier. (c) would collide with the generator. (d) would answer 201 for a reply a human cannot review. | Blank or absent model output produces a generation failure rather than a stored row, so a provider that answers with empty content costs a call and stores nothing. The wire record is the only response shape, so any future pre-persistence state would need this decision revisited. |
| DL-121 | Response generation performs the tweet read, OpenAI call and response insert without one surrounding service transaction; repository calls own short transactions and no connection is held during the network call. | (a) Keep one transaction across the whole method. (b) make the OpenAI call asynchronous inside the transaction. (c) manually manage an `EntityManager`. | A slow external request must not reserve a pool connection. The loaded tweet is converted to a DTO before the call and its identifier can be associated with the new response during the later repository transaction. | The tweet can be deleted between read and insert, surfacing as a storage failure, and the detached association depends on the row still existing. |
| DL-122 | Partial response updates acquire a pessimistic write lock on the row before applying content or approval changes. | (a) Save an unlocked stale entity. (b) add a version column. (c) issue field-specific update statements. | Locking preserves both independently writable fields without adding a schema column or duplicating update queries, and it fits the existing transactional service method. | Concurrent updates serialize and can wait, deadlock or fail under database lock timeouts. |
| DL-123 | Both list routes serve a **finite maximum of 1000 rows** per request. `util/QueryParameters` declares that maximum once, as `MAXIMUM_PAGE_SIZE`, beside `MINIMUM_PAGE_SIZE` of 1 and the `boundPageSize(requestedSize, defaultSize)` member that applies both: a requested size below the minimum reads as the route's declared default of 10, a size above the maximum reads as the maximum, and every size between the two reads unchanged. `service/TwitterService.getPaginatedTweets` and `service/ResponseService.getPaginatedResponses` each call that member and log the replacement at `WARN`; the `pagination` block restates the size served, and `total_pages` is computed from it. Nothing else about the contract moves: the paths, the methods, the `page` and `per_page` names, both declared defaults, the 1-based page contract and the 200 status are unchanged, no new query parameter is introduced, no request is rejected, and no configuration key is added. `api/TweetController` and `api/ResponseController` state the maximum in their route documentation and apply no bound themselves. Two earlier revisions of this entry recorded different behaviour: the first capped both routes at 100, and the second applied no upper bound at all. | (a) Apply no upper bound, which is the behaviour the immediately preceding revision of this entry delivered. (b) Reject a size above the maximum with a new 400, 413 or 422 status. (c) Give each route its own maximum. (d) Hold the maximum in a new `scanner.*` property. (e) Redesign both routes around a bounded cursor or a streamed response, which is the alternative the review names beside the maximum. (f) Set the maximum to the 500-row chunk bound of DL-249. (g) Bound the rows materialised while restating the requested size in the envelope. (h) Write the response as a `StreamingResponseBody` so heap is bounded without a row bound. (i) Bound the size in the two controllers instead of in the two services. | A security review recorded this as CWE-400 and ordered a finite server-side maximum, with the deviation from the historical no-range validation recorded here. The measured exposure: an authenticated caller sending `per_page=2147483647` reaches `PageRequest.of(page - 1, 2147483647)`, and although DL-249 bounds the rows one *statement* returns to 500, `QueryParameters.mapInChunks` accumulates every mapped DTO and returns `List.copyOf(mapped)`, which Jackson then serialises whole — so the heap, the CPU and the egress of one request scale with the table, not with the chunk bound, and a `HEAD` request performs the identical work. Parity is not breached by closing it: the frozen route contract freezes paths, methods, parameter names, defaults and status codes, and none of those changes; the retired tree produced no envelope at all, since `get_paginated_tweets` and `get_paginated_responses` never existed, and the envelope this migration defines under DL-038 already restates the *effective* page and size rather than the requested ones — a size below 1 has always been restated as 10. The maximum is 1000 because it is twice the 500-row chunk bound, so one response carries at most 1000 rows while the chunked read stays the path a large page takes; (f) at 500 would leave `mapInChunks` unreachable from either service and would delete the tested behaviour of DL-249. (a) is the finding. (b) introduces a status neither route declares, which endpoint parity forbids outright, and turns a served request into a failed one. (c) makes two routes disagree about one parameter and doubles the number of values a client must know. (d) makes a security bound switchable from the environment, and the configuration surface this migration adds is already the eight keys the retired tree referenced without declaring. (e) replaces the `{rows, pagination}` envelope DL-038 freezes with a cursor contract, which is a wire change far larger than the one being avoided. (g) makes the envelope misreport the page it served, which is the same defect DL-123's own history records: a Javadoc that asserted no bound while the code applied one. (h) bounds heap alone and leaves the database and CPU cost unbounded, and it still needs a row bound. (i) puts the bound where only two of the callers pass through, and both services are reachable from elsewhere; one declaration in `util/QueryParameters` read by both services keeps the value stated once. | This is a visible behaviour change for a client that asked for more than 1000 rows: it now receives 1000, the `per_page` it reads back is 1000 rather than the number it sent, and `total_pages` is computed from 1000, so such a client must page. No client in this repository does so — the delivered React client types both list bodies as bare arrays and sends no `per_page` at all (DL-059). The bound is per request, so a caller may still read a whole table by paging at 1000 rows a time; bounding the request *rate* is the ingress obligation DL-272 records, not this entry's subject. The maximum is a compile-time constant by the choice above, so moving it needs a build. A page whose first row lies beyond the offset a paged query can express is still answered from a row count alone (DL-225), and that behaviour is unchanged. Six test cases that asserted the absence of an upper bound were rewritten to assert the bound, and `ScannerApplicationTests` now asserts it on the wire for both routes, so the contract this entry states is executable rather than declared. |

DL-159 owns the seeding mechanism and states it as delivered; DL-244 owns the `Setting` identifier type. (b) Delete the row. (c) Deliver `Persistable` and `saveAndFlush` so the entry becomes true. (b) breaks every citation in version history that names this identifier. (d) gives one subject two owners. | A reader arriving from an older commit reaches a pointer rather than a mechanism. No artifact in the delivered tree cites this identifier. The withdrawn `Persistable` form survives in version history only, and nothing mechanically ties DL-099's list of rewritten rows to the rows themselves — that list is prose, so the same drift can recur and is guarded only by review. |

(b) Delete the row. The entry is the stale side and is rewritten. (b) would break every citation in version history that names this identifier; DL-099 directs a rewrite in place. | The two behaviours are easy to conflate — one absent property yields a substituted identifier, another yields a skipped page — so a reader needs both DL-090 and DL-219; each names the other. UTC provides one deterministic canonical zone without changing the established DTO type. | The original textual offset is lost and a local timestamp with no offset remains inherently ambiguous. |
| DL-128 | The Notion `RestClient` carries a connect timeout and a read timeout supplied by this application and nothing else, and a timeout-derived resource failure is translated to the fixed message `Notion request timed out`. The two values are configuration, not constants: `scanner.notion.connect-timeout-seconds` (default 5) and `scanner.notion.read-timeout-seconds` (default 10). The transport and the validation of those two values are owned by DL-150 and DL-193. | (a) Rely on the client's own defaults, which are unbounded. (b) Configure the bounds per call site. (c) Let the transport's own exception message reach the caller. | A shared transport bound stops a stalled upstream call from occupying a request thread or a scheduler thread for as long as the socket stays open, and one fixed translation keeps vendor and request detail out of the error surface the caller sees. (a) is the unbounded state. (b) would spread the same two values across call sites with no call needing a different bound. (c) would put provider text on a path the logging policy of DL-197 keeps content-free. | A slow but healthy Notion response beyond the read bound is aborted. Timeout classification matches cause types whose simple name contains `Timeout`, which is broader than one transport's exception type and is what keeps the translation working if the request factory changes. |
| DL-129 | OpenAI requests carry the source-compatible prompt as a **single user message** and no system or developer message. `service/LlmService` builds the request with `.addUserMessage(prompt)` alone, reproducing `Completion.create(prompt=…)` at `backend/app/services/llm_service.py:L19-26`. (b) Escape or delete tweet text before it enters the prompt. Leaving it as written would have been worse than a documentation gap: it would have recorded a prompt-injection mitigation that does not exist, so a reader auditing the untrusted-data boundary would have been misled into believing one was in place. Single-message parity is also the defensible position on its own terms — the parity requirement fixes the prompt template verbatim from `:L16` under DL-035, and the generated text is never published: `is_approved` is a flag a human reads under the never-auto-post invariant, so a human review stands between any model output and any public action. (b) would alter the audited literal segments of the prompt. (c) is forbidden outright by the never-auto-post invariant. | Tweet text and `ai_tools_mentioned` values reach the model as part of the user message with no trust boundary marked inside the prompt, so a crafted post can influence the generated draft. That exposure is bounded by the human review gate and by the structural absence of a publish path, not by prompt engineering. If a system instruction is ever added, this entry, the prompt template of DL-035 and the parity claim against `:L19-26` all have to move together. |

(c) Implement the three-algorithm mechanism so the entry becomes true. | (c) contradicts DL-108, which owns the accepted-algorithm set and argues it down to one member on the grounds that this service both mints and verifies its own tokens and has no second party to negotiate with; widening the set would also widen the parser's acceptance, which is the half that matters for a presented token. (b) would break every citation in version history that names this identifier; DL-099 directs a rewrite in place. | An operator who sets `ALGORITHM=HS512` gets a startup failure naming the one supported value rather than HS512 tokens — a louder outcome than this entry described, and the one DL-108 intends. | DL-145 | `scanner.openai.reasoning-effort` is declared with the default **`none`**, is read by `service/LlmService` and reaches the request as `ReasoningEffort.of(value.trim())`; a blank value omits the parameter entirely. The migration requires `temperature` 0.7 and `max_completion_tokens` 150 as transcribed configuration defaults, so `none` is the only effort value under which both mandated source values can be sent and still produce a reply — which is why (a) and (b) are rejected even though both were measured as accepted values on their own: `low` forces a choice between parameter parity and a working generation, and `minimal` is refused outright with HTTP 400 `unsupported_value`. | DL-146 | The OpenAI client carries an explicit request timeout (`scanner.openai.request-timeout-seconds`, default 30) and an explicit retry limit (`scanner.openai.max-retries`, default 2), and `ResponseService.generateResponse` declares no transaction, so the model call sits between two short repository transactions. Retries multiply the worst-case wait: three attempts at a 30-second timeout is a 90-second ceiling per request. |

| DL-148 | A provider failure is written to the log exactly once, by the adapter that made the call, and the record carries only the HTTP status, the provider's error `code` and `type`, and the `x-request-id` response header. (c) Log nothing and rely on the exception reaching a handler. | The OpenAI SDK documents that a service exception's message can carry the provider's response JSON, which for this service means the generated reply and whatever the prompt quoted from a monitored post — a CWE-532 exposure in a log that is not treated as sensitive. (c) removes the diagnostic entirely: the status, the error code and the request identifier are the fields a support request to the provider needs, and none of them is content. | The stack trace is no longer in the log, so an unexpected failure has to be diagnosed from the exception's type name plus the request identifier. `code` and `type` are provider-defined strings; they are enumerated error identifiers rather than content, but they are still values this service does not control. |
| DL-149 | The earlier per-service printable-ASCII/truncation guards are withdrawn. | DL-150 | The Notion transport carries a bounded connect timeout (`scanner.notion.connect-timeout-seconds`, default 5) and a bounded read timeout (`scanner.notion.read-timeout-seconds`, default 10), the connect bound carried by the retained `HttpClient` bean of DL-264 and the read bound applied per request by the `JdkClientHttpRequestFactory` built over it. A Notion failure reaches the caller as a `RestClientException`; a mirror write is retried within the bounded budget of DL-253 and the relational store has already accepted the data, so the mirror failing never fails the primary write. | (a) Leave the framework defaults, which are null timeouts — the state delivered before this entry. (b) Set only a read timeout. (c) Add a retry with backoff. (d) Add a circuit breaker so a failing mirror stops being called. | Boot's default request-factory settings carry no timeout at all, so a Notion request that never answers holds its caller — a request thread or, once the task files land, a scheduler thread — for as long as the socket stays open. (c) and (d) are both defensible for a mirror, but each is a new capability: a retry needs an idempotency story for `pages.create`, which is not idempotent, and a breaker needs resilience4j plus its own configuration surface. | DL-152 | Before its first write, the adapter reads the target database once and confirms that it declares all seven properties this mirror writes with the types it writes — `Content` title, `Author` rich_text, `Timestamp` date, `Doubt Rating` number, `Engagement` number, `Tweet Id` rich_text, `Response` rich_text — failing with an `IllegalStateException` that names every absent or mistyped property. (b) has this service own a schema an operator also edits, and a mirror that mutates its target's structure is a larger authority than a mirror needs. (c) leaves the diagnosis where it was. (d) spends a request per write for a schema that changes almost never. | The first write pays one extra request, and a database edited to remove a property after the check succeeded is not noticed until the process restarts. Seven property names are now a contract with the operator's Notion workspace; the four inherited from the source keep their original names, and the three that changed are recorded in the entries covering the property map. |
| DL-153 | A Notion failure is logged once and carries three fields: the HTTP status, the `code` member of the error body, and the `x-request-id` response header. (e) Log the `message` cut to 200 characters, which a later revision of this entry authorised. (f) Log the `message` unbounded. (b) is the same exposure by another route, because the body is inside the message. (c) puts the exposure behind a level rather than removing it, and DEBUG is enabled on exactly the systems where someone is investigating. (d) is safe but discards `code`, which is the field that distinguishes a validation mistake from a rate limit from a revoked token. The diagnosis is recovered at no exposure elsewhere: the pre-flight schema check of DL-152 names every absent or mistyped property at startup, before any write is attempted. (f) leaves the record's size to the provider. (g) is the duplication DL-149 withdrew. | A Notion validation rejection now reports the class of fault and not the individual property, so an operator who did not read the DL-152 startup check has to reproduce the call outside this service to see which property Notion objected to. `code` and `x-request-id` cannot carry a line break, whitespace or a property value, because a value failing the shared shape check is replaced by `absent` rather than logged, so neither can be used to forge a record (CWE-117). (c) is a new capability — several requests per call, an accumulating result set and a partial-failure story — and the cursor parameter already lets a caller page deliberately. | A caller asking for 500 silently receives at most 100 and must use the cursor to continue; the cap is reported at `WARN` so the truncation is visible. |

| DL-180 | `service/AnalyticsService.getSummary()` declares `@Transactional(readOnly = true, isolation = Isolation.REPEATABLE_READ)`, so the **three** aggregate statements it issues — one per table: `TweetRepository.findAggregates()`, `ResponseRepository.findApprovalCounts()` and `AiToolRepository.count()` — read one snapshot and every metric of the returned `SummaryDto` describes the same instant. (c) Compute `pending_responses` with its own `countByIsApprovedFalse()` query instead of by subtraction. (d) Clamp `pending_responses` at zero. (e) `SERIALIZABLE` instead of `REPEATABLE_READ`. All three supported vendors implement `REPEATABLE READ` — it is MySQL's default, and PostgreSQL and H2 2.x both offer it — so no vendor is excluded, and Spring's `HibernateJpaDialect` applies the level to the JDBC connection or raises `InvalidIsolationLevelException` loudly rather than ignoring it. | The summary transaction holds one snapshot for the duration of three statements, which on PostgreSQL keeps a slightly older row version visible to them; the cost is negligible at this scale and the reported instant is the start of the transaction rather than the start of each statement. (d) would make a pure exception translator into a request-mapped controller, putting two unrelated roles in one class.
