(b) reverses the log-injection guard of DL-149, the correlation keying of DL-119 and the failure-metadata policy of DL-197 outright. (c) moves the file without adding it to any inventory, so it changes nothing about the deviation while making the class's purpose less obvious from its location. (d) is therefore taken, and this row is what makes the deviation explained rather than silent, which is the standard Rule 1 sets. | The `util/` package holds five classes where the frozen inventory enumerates one, so a reviewer checking file counts against that inventory will find a discrepancy and must reach this row for `util/LogSafe`, DL-287 for `util/ConfiguredValues`, DL-217 and DL-225 for `util/QueryParameters` and DL-257 for `util/StreamRuleTerms`; `docs/TRACEABILITY_MATRIX.md` carries the class as net-new so it is not absent from coverage. Sixteen classes now depend on a single utility, so a change to any primitive changes every log record that uses it at once — which is the intent, and is why the records that matter are pinned by tests in `service/NotionServiceTest`, `api/GlobalExceptionHandlerTest` and `security/JwtServiceTest`. If the agreed inventory is ever amended to close `util/` at one class, the migration path is (a), at the cost that alternative describes. |
| DL-209 | `ScannerApplication` carries exactly two annotations — `@SpringBootApplication` and `@ConfigurationPropertiesScan` — and a `main` that does nothing but `SpringApplication.run(ScannerApplication.class, args)`. No `@EnableScheduling`, `@EnableAsync`, `@EnableWebSecurity`, `@ComponentScan` or `@EntityScan` is declared on it, and no second application object exists anywhere: the composition root of `backend/app/main.py:L15-39` collapses to this one class, with each concern the factory performed inline moved to the `@Configuration` class that owns it — `config/CorsConfig`, `security/SecurityConfig`, `config/AsyncSchedulingConfig` and `api/GlobalExceptionHandler`. | (a) Declare `@EnableScheduling` here, which is the shape the composition root was specified to take. (b) Declare `@ComponentScan` and `@EntityScan` explicitly rather than relying on the base-package default. (c) Use `@EnableConfigurationProperties(ScannerProperties.class)` instead of the scan. (d) Keep a second, module-level application object beside the factory, mirroring `main.py:L13`. (e) Move `main` into a separate `Launcher` class. | `@EnableScheduling` belongs on `config/AsyncSchedulingConfig` and on no other class, because that class also implements `SchedulingConfigurer` and its `configureTasks` is what registers the fixed-**delay** trigger DL-047 requires; declaring the annotation in two places would give a reader two candidate pacing sites to reconcile, and the pacing site was already the subject of three rival implementations, so keeping it single is what makes the delay contract locatable. (b) restates what the base package already yields and would need editing on every package addition. (c) names one class and would need editing for each further `@ConfigurationProperties` type, where the scan binds `ScannerProperties` wherever it sits. (d) is the source's own defect: `main.py` built a `Flask` object at `:L13` and a second one inside `create_app()`, so which object the routes and error handlers bound to was ambiguous — one context is the point of the port. (e) splits a two-line entry point across two files. | The class is the scan root, so a package added outside `com.codeskeptic.scanner` is silently not scanned and its beans never appear; the symptom is a missing-bean failure at startup rather than a compile error. Relying on the default scan means the scanned set is not visible from this file and a reader must know the convention. |
| DL-210 | `dto/ErrorResponse` is a single-component record serialising to `{"error": <string>}`, and it is the only error body type in the tree: all ten error branches the source emitted and all **fourteen** `@ExceptionHandler` methods of `api/GlobalExceptionHandler` serialise through it. It declares no message text of its own — every literal lives at its throwing site under `com.codeskeptic.scanner.exception` (DL-212) — and no second key is ever added. The servlet `ERROR` dispatch carries the same one key, rendered as a map rather than as this record, because `ErrorAttributes` returns a map (DL-183). | (a) A richer envelope carrying a code, a timestamp, a path or a field list. (b) Spring Boot's default `ErrorAttributes` body. (c) RFC 7807 `application/problem+json`. (d) A `Map<String, String>` assembled at each handler. (e) One record per status. | Wire-contract parity fixes the wire body at exactly `{"error": "Not found"}` and `{"error": "Internal server error"}`, and the frontend is out of scope, so any added key is a contract change no caller asked for and none can consume. (a) and (c) are the changes a reviewer is most likely to propose and both add keys the source never emitted; (c) additionally changes the content type. (b) emits `timestamp`, `status`, `error` and `path`, which is precisely the body the `ErrorAttributes` bean of `api/GlobalExceptionHandler` exists to replace (DL-183). (d) loses the single declaration point that makes the shape checkable and lets a mistyped key pass unnoticed. (e) multiplies types for one identical shape. | A caller receives no machine-readable discriminator, so it must branch on the HTTP status and, for branches that share a status, on the literal text — which is what makes those literals contract (DL-212). A field-level validation failure collapses to one sentence, so a client cannot mark the offending field; that is the source's behaviour and the wire contract freezes it. The handler count stated above is one a reader can check against the class, and `api/GlobalExceptionHandlerTest` asserts the handled-type set, so a handler added without a table row is visible rather than silent. |
| DL-211 | `service/ResponseService` is one singleton bean holding **six** collaborators in final fields — `ResponseRepository`, `TweetRepository`, `LlmService`, `ResponseMapper`, `TweetMapper` and a `TransactionTemplate` — each injected through the single constructor and null-checked there. Nothing is instantiated per request and no collaborator is reached statically. | (a) Instantiate collaborators inside each method, which is what the source's controllers did (`TwitterService()` at `backend/app/api/tweets.py:L14`). (b) Field injection with `@Autowired`. (c) Split the class so each operation carries fewer collaborators. (d) Annotate the methods `@Transactional` instead of holding a `TransactionTemplate`. | The injection rule replaces import-time and per-call construction with constructor injection, and the constructor is what makes the dependency set visible and the class testable without a context — `service/ResponseServiceTest` builds it from mocks directly. The `TransactionTemplate` is held rather than annotated because the generation path must issue the provider call **outside** any transaction and then open a short transaction to claim and insert (DL-195): a method-level `@Transactional` would hold a database connection across the OpenAI round trip, which is the seam DL-195 exists to keep open. (a) rebuilds a service per request and defeats pooling. (b) hides the dependency set from the constructor and needs reflection to populate in a test. (c) would scatter one aggregate's four call-site-dictated operations across classes to reduce a field count. | Six collaborators is a large constructor and a fair signal that this class does the most work of any service here; it is the count the four call sites at `api/responses.py:L15,L26,L44,L60` plus the background entry point require, and further growth should split the class rather than add a seventh. Explicit transaction handling is more code than an annotation and its boundaries are correct only by inspection — the tests that pin them are the guard. |
| DL-212 | The three types under `com.codeskeptic.scanner.exception` own the wire literals. Each declares its approved messages as `public static final String` constants, keeps its constructor private, and exposes one named static factory per reporting branch — `BadRequestException.tweetIdRequired()`, `updateDataRequired()` and `noValueProvided()`, the `NotFoundException` factories for the tweet, response and setting branches, and `ResponseGenerationException` for the single 500 literal. `api/GlobalExceptionHandler` copies `getMessage()` into `dto/ErrorResponse` and composes no text of its own. | (a) Throw `new NotFoundException("Tweet not found")` with the literal written at each call site. (b) Hold the literals as constants on the handler or on a shared `ErrorMessages` class. (c) Use Spring's `ResponseStatusException` with the reason inline. (d) Resolve the text from a `messages.properties` bundle. (e) Let the handler derive the text from the status. | The eight per-route literals are wire contract under wire-contract parity, so the risk to manage is a typo or a drift in one of eight strings spread across four controllers; a private constructor plus a named factory makes an unapproved literal impossible to construct, which is a compile-time guarantee where (a) and (c) offer only review. (b) puts the text one indirection away from the throw and leaves the constructor open. (d) introduces localisation the source never had and turns a frozen literal into a resource a translator could change. (e) cannot work: `Tweet not found` and `Response not found` share status 404, and `Tweet ID is required`, `Update data is required` and `No value provided` share 400, so the status does not determine the text. | The factory names are the API, so a new branch needs a new factory rather than a new string — deliberate friction. Because `getMessage()` reaches the wire, any instance of these types must carry only approved text; the private constructors enforce that for these three, and `handleUnexpectedException` answers the fixed `Internal server error` for everything else, so no third-party message can reach a caller. |
| DL-213 | The trend series is read by one JPQL query, `TweetRepository.findDailyTrendsBetween(LocalDateTime, LocalDateTime)`, whose two bounds close the window at the instant of the call as well as opening it (DL-247), and which buckets on `cast(t.createdAt as LocalDate)`, aggregates `count(t)`, `avg(t.doubtRating)` and `sum(t.likeCount)`, groups and orders by the same expression, and binds into the closed interface projection `TweetRepository.DailyTrend`. No native SQL, no vendor date function and no entity list reaches `service/AnalyticsService`. | (a) A native query using `date_trunc` (PostgreSQL) or `DATE()` (MySQL). (b) Load the window's `Tweet` rows and bucket them in Java. (c) A class-based DTO projection with a constructor expression. (d) A `Tuple` or `Object[]` result. (e) A database view. | The migration must not resolve the database vendor at compile time and DL-027 deliberately leaves the dialect to Hibernate's metadata detection, so (a) picks a vendor in source and would have to be written twice and selected at runtime; `cast(… as LocalDate)` is JPQL that Hibernate renders per dialect, which is the only bucketing expression satisfying both constraints. (b) transfers every row in the window to compute four numbers and grows without bound as `tweets` grows — the aggregation belongs where the rows are. (c) works but couples the query to a constructor signature, where a closed projection binds by select alias and lets each of the four accessors be documented individually. (d) returns positional untyped values, so every read becomes an unchecked cast. (e) is a schema object, and the migration freezes the schema at four tables. | `avg` and `sum` return provider-typed numbers, so `DailyTrend` declares them nullable and a bucket whose rows all carry `null` yields `null` — `service/AnalyticsService` is what substitutes for that, and the substitution is only correct because the accessors are nullable. Rows whose `created_at` is `null` fail the `>=` predicate and appear in no bucket, and a day with no rows produces no element, so the series is sparse rather than gap-filled; both are the source's behaviour by omission and are asserted by `service/AnalyticsServiceTest`. A `cast` used as a grouping key is not indexable, and this migration forbids adding an index, so scan cost grows with the window. |
| DL-214 | `task/TweetStreamClient` is covered by two test classes rather than none or one. `task/TweetStreamClientTest` drives the client through a stubbed `ExchangeFunction` and asserts the app-only token exchange, rule reconciliation, NDJSON reassembly across chunk boundaries, the `429` and `x-rate-limit-reset` handling, the backoff with jitter, the reconnect-on-clean-end of DL-207 and the blank-credential stop. `task/TweetStreamClientLifecycleTest` asserts the `SmartLifecycle` contract — `start`, `stop`, `isRunning`, idempotence, and that no blocking read happens on the caller's thread. | (a) Give the class no test at all, on the grounds that its behaviour is network I/O. (b) One test class covering both concerns. (c) Bind the client to a local HTTP server. (d) Extract the protocol logic into a helper and test the helper instead. | (a) was the earlier position and it does not survive the class's size or its risk profile: this is the one hand-rolled integration in the tree (DL-012), it carries the no-publish invariant of the never-auto-post invariant structurally, and it is where two CWE-532 log leaks were found — an untested class is exactly where such a leak survives. A stubbed `ExchangeFunction` reaches all of that behaviour without a socket, so the offline `mvn clean verify` of a working test suite is preserved. The split into two classes is what keeps each readable: protocol behaviour is asserted per stubbed response, while lifecycle behaviour is asserted per transition and needs `Awaitility` because rule composition runs on `boundedElastic` — interleaving per-response stubbing with asynchronous awaits in one class is what made the single-class form hard to follow. (c) adds a server and a port to the build. (d) would split a class the frozen inventory declares as one. | Two classes cover one production class, so a reader must consult both to see the whole contract and a behaviour could be asserted twice or fall between them. Neither class contacts X, so the live path — the real token exchange, the real rules endpoint and the real stream — rests on the ad-hoc verification recorded against DL-046 and on DL-205's blank-credential guard rather than on the committed suite. |
| DL-215 | The operations delivery is exactly four files, each held to the line-level carve-out defined for it, and nothing wider. `infrastructure/docker/Dockerfile.backend` is rewritten at `L2`, `L8-11`, `L14`, `L20` and `L22-28`, keeping `EXPOSE 5000`. `.github/workflows/ci.yml` changes at `L16-19`, `L26-29`, `L35-39`, `L47-50` and `L56-59` — the JDK 21 toolchain step and the single `mvn -B clean verify` step with `working-directory: ./backend` — plus two values a mandatory rule and this migration's own authorship reach inside and beside those ranges: the toolchain action within `L16-19` is pinned to a full commit SHA (DL-103), and the `node-version` input is the mandatory Node.js floor rather than the retired `14` (DL-285). Every other line, the single `build-and-test` job and all five frontend commands included, is byte-identical to the source branch; no `permissions` key, no checkout input and no job split is added (DL-104). `.github/workflows/cd.yml` changes at `L36` alone, adding `-f infrastructure/docker/Dockerfile.backend` to `docker build -t $BACKEND_IMAGE ./backend` (DL-056). `scripts/deploy.sh` changes at `L18` alone, `npm run build` becoming `mvn clean package` (DL-053). No fifth file is delivered: no `.yamllint.yml` and no `.github/dependabot.yml` (DL-276). The two container-build mechanisms therefore still differ — `cd.yml` builds and pushes with Docker while `deploy.sh` keeps `gcloud builds submit --tag gcr.io/code-skeptic-scanner/backend` — and that asymmetry is inherited rather than chosen. `OperationsContractTest` asserts each of these boundaries, including the absence of the two withdrawn files, the absence of any registry, secret-binding, revision-scaling, CPU-allocation, artifact-handoff, job-splitting or deployment-probe change, the 40-hex form of the pinned toolchain action, and the Node.js floor as arithmetic rather than as a literal. | (a) Migrate `deploy.sh` to the same `docker build`/`docker push` pair `cd.yml` uses, so one mechanism serves both. (b) Delete the `gcloud builds submit` line as redundant. (c) Point `gcloud builds submit` at the Dockerfile through `--config` or a `cloudbuild.yaml`. (d) Migrate `cd.yml` to `gcloud builds submit` instead. (e) Keep the hardened CD workflow an earlier revision delivered — Artifact Registry, Secret Manager verification, an explicit container port, `--no-cpu-throttling`, a single-instance floor and ceiling, a digest deploy, a frontend artifact handoff and a post-deploy probe — and keep the CI workflow split into three jobs. | The agreed carve-out confines `deploy.sh` to its backend-build line — "Line 18 only … Nothing else changes" — and `cd.yml` to its build line, and `gcloud builds submit` is a separate line implementing a different mechanism, so (a) and (b) exceed the carve-out however sensible convergence would be. (c) is a second and larger edit to a protected file. (d) would rewrite the authorised workflow to match an unauthorised one. (e) is what an earlier revision delivered and what a review recorded as a critical breach: it altered the deployment architecture and, in setting a minimum and maximum instance count, changed the very autoscaling the plan names as leave-alone. Each of its parts is defensible engineering, which is exactly why the boundary has to be the plan and not the merit; every one of them remains available to whoever widens the carve-out. A submit that finds no Dockerfile in its directory is a pre-existing defect of the same family as the undeclared `var.backend_image` this migration leaves broken on purpose. | `scripts/deploy.sh` does not build a working image, because `gcloud builds submit` runs from `backend/`, which holds no Dockerfile — a developer following the script meets a failure at that line while `cd.yml` succeeds. Continuous deployment does not fire at all, because the CD trigger names a workflow that does not exist (DL-102). The deployed revision binds no `DATABASE_URL`, `SECRET_KEY` or `AUTH_PASSWORD_HASH`, so it cannot start, and no probe reports that; deployment relies on a long-lived service-account key (DL-104) and on a mutable image reference (DL-105). Every one of those is a property of the source-branch operations files, retained deliberately, and every one of them is one authorised line away from repair. A reader comparing `cd.yml` with `deploy.sh` will assume one of them is wrong; this row is the reconciliation. |
| DL-216 | The delivered test inventory is **forty-six** classes: the **nineteen** the frozen test inventory names, all of which now exist, plus **twenty-seven** additions. The nineteen are `ScannerApplicationTests`; `api/TweetControllerTest`, `api/ResponseControllerTest`, `api/SettingControllerTest`, `api/AnalyticsControllerTest`, `api/AuthControllerTest` and `api/GlobalExceptionHandlerTest`; `service/SentimentAnalysisServiceTest`, `service/TwitterServiceTest`, `service/NotionServiceTest`, `service/LlmServiceTest`, `service/ResponseServiceTest`, `service/SettingsServiceTest` and `service/AnalyticsServiceTest`; `task/ResponseGenerationSchedulerTest` and `task/TweetStreamListenerTest`; `repository/JpaMappingIntegrationTest`; `security/JwtServiceTest`; and `config/DatabaseUrlTranslatorTest`. The twenty-seven additions are each authorised by name in DL-096 and by the decision that introduced their subject: `api/AuthControllerAdviceTest` (DL-019, DL-117), `config/AsyncSchedulingConfigTest` (DL-047, DL-250, DL-251), `config/BuildProfileGuardTest` (DL-279), `config/ContainerErrorResponseConfigTest` (DL-237, DL-238), `config/DataSourceConfigTest` (DL-027, DL-270, DL-271), `config/HttpChainIntegrationTest` (DL-051, DL-183, DL-237, DL-238), `config/MainProfileConfigurationContractTest` (DL-026, DL-029, DL-030, DL-031), `config/RequestMediaTypeConfigTest` (DL-235, DL-236), `config/RestClientConfigTest` (DL-151, DL-195, DL-264), `config/WebClientConfigTest` (DL-230), `security/CachedBodyRequestTest` and `security/RequestBodyLimitIntegrationTest` (DL-118), `security/JwtAuthenticationFilterTest` (DL-021), `service/SettingsServiceSeedingIntegrationTest` (DL-040, DL-159), `task/BackgroundOwnershipTest` (DL-281, DL-284), `task/TweetStreamClientTest` and `task/TweetStreamClientLifecycleTest` (DL-214, DL-254), `util/ConfiguredValuesTest` (DL-186), `util/LogSafeTest` (DL-208), `util/QueryParametersTest` (DL-217), `util/StreamRuleTermsTest` (DL-257), `BuildDependencyContractTest` (DL-169, DL-170, DL-240, DL-241), `OperationsContractTest` (DL-053, DL-056, DL-105, DL-106), and the four documentation guards this row authorises directly — `DecisionLogCitationTest`, `DocumentationConsistencyTest`, `TraceabilityCoverageTest` and `DecisionPointerIntegrityTest` — which hold this log and `docs/TRACEABILITY_MATRIX.md` to one another and to the delivered tree. `DocumentationConsistencyTest` carries two further assertions a review asked for: every repeated population claim is measured at each of its occurrences, so a contradictory restatement fails where a single matching occurrence once passed, and the matrix is held to the wording set of DL-058. Earlier revisions of this row stated thirty-one, then thirty-four, then forty-five and then forty-seven classes and enumerated eleven, then fifteen, then twenty-six and then twenty-eight additions; `task/ProviderWorkBudgetTest` was a forty-seventh class until DL-283 withdrew the component it covered, and that file is deleted. `api/ErrorDispatchControllerTest` was a further class until DL-183 withdrew the controller it covered; both files are deleted and neither appears here or in the matrix as delivered. | (a) Hold the suite to the names the frozen inventory enumerates and delete the rest. (b) Keep the additional classes but leave the `PLANNED` markers standing. (c) Fold each addition into the nearest planned class so the file count matches. (d) Record the additions in the traceability matrix only, not here. (e) Keep `api/ErrorDispatchControllerTest` in this enumeration and mark it withdrawn in place. (f) Leave the citation-to-row correspondence to review rather than to a test. (g) State the class count in prose only and enumerate nothing, which is the form that let thirty-one, thirty-four, forty-five and forty-seven all be written here at different times. | A working suite and a green `mvn clean verify` are the definition of done, and each addition covers a delivered construct the planned names do not reach — a security filter, a request wrapper, a cached body, a scheduling registration, four utilities, a stream protocol, two lifecycles, the build contract, the operations files and the two explainability documents. (a) would delete coverage of exactly the constructs where real defects were found: the filter test is what caught a dropped principal lookup, and the pagination cases in `service/TwitterServiceTest` and `service/ResponseServiceTest` are what caught two degenerate-page faults that answered HTTP 500 where the source answers 200. (b) leaves the matrix asserting that a delivered class does not exist. (c) produces test classes with two subjects each, against the ownership discipline this log applies to its own entries. (d) splits the record of one subject across two documents. (e) would make this row an inventory of files that are partly present and partly not. (f) is how the correspondence was maintained until stale identifiers were found cited from eleven files at once. (g) is why this revision enumerates all forty-six rather than only the additions: a bare count drifts silently, while `DocumentationConsistencyTest` compares the matrix's per-class rows against the classes on disk and its stated totals against the executed case counts, so a class added without a row fails the build. | The suite is twenty-seven classes larger than the frozen inventory, so a reviewer checking counts against that inventory must reach this row; the matrix carries each addition as net-new, so none is absent from coverage. This row states a count and an enumeration that both have to be re-stated whenever a class is added or withdrawn, and only the matrix side of that is mechanically checked — this row's own figures are prose. The four documentation guards read the working tree rather than the classpath, so they are coupled to Surefire running with the module root as its working directory and to the two documents keeping the population sentences they match on. Any case count stated in prose drifts the moment a test is added, so the figures in the matrix are measured from the delivered Surefire run rather than estimated, and should be re-measured rather than trusted. |
| DL-217 | `GET /tweets` and `GET /responses` bind `page` and `per_page` as **optional text** — `@RequestParam(name = …, required = false) String` — and convert through the shared public helper `util/QueryParameters.intOrDefault(String, int)`: absent, blank or unconvertible text yields the default, 1 for `page` and 10 for `per_page`, and any successfully parsed integer passes through unchanged. Neither service caps `per_page`, and no `MAXIMUM_PER_PAGE` constant exists anywhere in the tree. Degenerate parsed values are normalised inside the services before a `PageRequest` is built, so a zero, a negative or `Integer.MIN_VALUE` answers 200 with the first page rather than failing. | (a) Bind primitive `int` parameters and let Spring MVC convert. (b) Cap `per_page` at a maximum such as 100. (c) Reject an unconvertible value with a new 400 branch. (d) Pass the parsed value straight to `PageRequest` without normalising. (e) Duplicate the conversion in each controller instead of sharing a helper. | The source is `request.args.get(name, default, type=int)` (`backend/app/api/tweets.py:L12-13`), which falls back to the default on conversion failure and otherwise forwards the parsed integer with no range policy — so endpoint parity fixes both the defaults and the absence of a range policy. (a) is the defect this contract replaces: a primitive `int` binding answers HTTP 400 for `page=abc`, where the source answers 200 with page 1, and it was found in two independently written controllers. (b) invents a cap the source never had, which is validation improvement rather than parity; a cap was proposed, implemented and withdrawn for that reason. (c) adds a status the route never emitted. (d) is what the code did and it produced HTTP 500 where the source answers 200 — `PageRequest.of` rejects a negative index, and `page = Integer.MIN_VALUE` overflowed the `page - 1` conversion to the maximum index; both were live faults in `service/ResponseService` and `service/TwitterService`. (e) is how the drift arose in the first place. | An uncapped `per_page` lets one request ask for an arbitrarily large page, so database and heap work is bounded only by the table size; that is the source's exposure and adding a bound would be a contract change (DL-193 records the wire-shape half of the pagination envelope). Normalising rather than rejecting means a caller sending `page=0` receives the first page and no signal that its value was adjusted, which is the source's behaviour. Because the conversion is a helper rather than a framework binding, a new route must remember to use it. |
| DL-218 | The runtime image declares a `HEALTHCHECK` and sizes the heap from the container's own memory limit. The probe issues `curl` against `http://127.0.0.1:${PORT:-5000}/tweets` every 30s with a 5s timeout, a 60s start period and 3 retries, and treats **any** HTTP answer as healthy — including the `401` that route returns without a token, because the probe carries none. The entry point is `sh -c "exec java -XX:MaxRAMPercentage=75.0 -jar app.jar"`, so the JVM reads the cgroup limit rather than host memory and `exec` keeps Java as PID 1 for signal delivery. `${PORT:-5000}` matches `server.port: ${PORT:5000}` in `application.yml` (DL-029). | (a) Declare no `HEALTHCHECK` and let the orchestrator probe. (b) Add a dedicated unauthenticated health endpoint, or include `spring-boot-starter-actuator` and probe `/actuator/health`. (c) Probe an authenticated route and supply a credential to the probe. (d) Leave the heap to the JVM's default ergonomics. (e) Set a fixed `-Xmx`. (f) Use the exec-form `CMD` without a shell. | A probe that reaches the servlet container proves the listener is bound and serving, which is the property a container health check exists to report; distinguishing `401` from `200` would test authorisation rather than liveness, so accepting any answer is the correct predicate here. (b) is the idiomatic answer and is excluded twice over: an actuator dependency is an observability addition the frozen dependency inventory does not name, and a new unauthenticated route would add a twelfth endpoint to a surface endpoint parity fixes at eleven plus the token route. (c) would place a credential in the image. (d) is the defect being closed — without `MaxRAMPercentage` a JVM in a memory-limited container can size its heap from the host's memory and be killed by the OOM killer under load. (e) hardcodes a figure that must change with every memory limit. (f) cannot expand `${PORT:-5000}`, and the `exec` keeps the signal behaviour the exec form would otherwise provide. | The probe depends on `curl` being present in the runtime image. It is: the image configuration the pinned digest of DL-106 resolves to installs `curl` alongside `ca-certificates` and removes only the apt lists afterwards, so the binary survives into the final layer. That is a property of the pinned digest rather than of the `eclipse-temurin` line in general, so a digest refresh that drops `curl` would silently report the container unhealthy while the application serves normally. Because any HTTP answer passes, a service that boots but has lost its database still reports healthy — the check is a liveness signal, not a readiness or dependency check. 75 percent is a general default rather than a measured figure for this workload; the remaining quarter must cover metaspace, thread stacks and direct buffers, and a deployment with an unusual profile may need to lower it. |
| DL-219 | `service/NotionService.toTweetDto` returns `null`, and `readTweets` counts the page, reports the aggregate once at `WARN` and leaves it out of the returned list, when a mirrored Notion page carries no value for one of the five mirrored properties `dto/TweetDto` declares required — `Content` for `content`, `Engagement` for `likeCount`, `Timestamp` for `createdAt`, `Doubt Rating` for `doubtRating` and `Author` for `userId`. This extends to the required-value case the skip that DL-090 already established for a page carrying no identifier at all; DL-090's `Tweet Id`-to-page-identifier fallback is applied first, so a page is skipped for a missing identifier only when both are absent. No default is substituted and no exception is raised, and the aggregate `WARN` names both causes in one message. | (a) Substitute a neutral value for the absent property — `0` for the engagement, `0.0` for the doubt rating, the empty string for the content or the author, the epoch for the timestamp. (b) Let the record's own rejection escape, so one incomplete page fails the whole read from the adapter boundary. (c) Keep the records permissive and serialise `null`, the contract the withdrawn form of DL-139 authorised. (d) Report each skipped page individually at `WARN` rather than once in aggregate. (e) Add a second counter and a second message so an incomplete page is reported separately from an unidentifiable one. | DL-080 makes those five components non-null on the wire record, so an incomplete page has no wire form and the only open question is which of skip, substitute and raise applies to it. DL-090 already answered that question for this read path and rules out both of the other two: it forbids substitution because a page that never carried a measurement must not read back as though it carried zero — the fault DL-075 records for a null aggregate — and it forbids raising because a mirror that is a secondary store must not let one malformed page take down a read of the pages that are well formed. This entry therefore applies DL-090's existing policy to the case the record contract newly creates, and applies it through the skip counter DL-089 and DL-090 already put in `readTweets` rather than adding a second mechanism. (a) is what DL-090 forbids. (b) inverts the mirror's secondary status, which integration parity fixes. (c) is ruled out by the record-mapping rule, as DL-080 records. (d) turns one malformed export into an unbounded number of log lines, against the aggregate-once discipline DL-089 set for this same loop. (e) reports a distinction no caller can act on differently, since the remedy for both is to repair the export. | A mirrored page missing any of the five properties disappears from `getTweets` with nothing but the aggregate `WARN` to show for it, so a caller cannot distinguish a partial mirror from a small one without reading the log; the count in the message is what keeps that detectable. `NotionService.getTweets` has no production caller (DL-191), so the reachable effect today is confined to `service/NotionServiceTest`; the rule is in place so a future caller inherits it rather than meeting the record's rejection at runtime. The write path is unaffected, because `storeTweet` mirrors from a `TweetDto` that has already been constructed and therefore cannot present an incomplete page. |
| DL-220 | Every record the X filtered stream delivers is handed to `task/TweetStreamListener` on `Schedulers.boundedElastic()`: `task/TweetStreamClient.consumeStream` composes `publishOn(Schedulers.boundedElastic())` between the record source and the dispatch step, so the listener's JDBC write, its Google Natural Language call, its OpenAI call and its two Notion mirror writes all execute on a blocking-capable worker rather than on the reactor-netty thread that emits the response body. | (a) Leave dispatch on the emitting thread, which is what the class did: `subscribeOn` in `start()` and in `runOneConnection` moves only the subscription and the rule composition, so once the body arrives every record was handled on a Netty event loop. (b) `concatMap(record -> Mono.fromCallable(() -> dispatchRecord(record)).subscribeOn(Schedulers.boundedElastic()))`. (c) `flatMap` with a concurrency of one. (d) A dedicated single-thread scheduler owned by this class. (e) Keep dispatch where it is and make each collaborator non-blocking. | A Netty event loop thread carries Reactor's non-blocking marker, and any code that blocks on it is either refused outright or starves the loop. Under (a) both consequences were measured: the Notion adapter's synchronous `RestClient` call was refused with `block()/blockFirst()/blockLast() are blocking, which is not supported in thread reactor-http-epoll-*`, so **no ingested post reached the Notion database at all** and the reply mirror never issued its `PATCH`; and two records delivered in one response body were processed 3.5 s apart against a 3 s model answer, because the loop that also carries the stream connection was held for the whole provider call. `boundedElastic` is the scheduler Reactor documents for blocking work and the one already named for this class. (b) and (c) are equally correct on the thread question; `publishOn` was chosen because it keeps one worker for the connection's lifetime instead of taking one per record, keeps arrival order without an ordering operator, and leaves the operator's prefetch queue between the socket and the listener so the connection thread can keep reading while a record is being handled. (d) adds a lifecycle to own for a pool the framework already bounds. (e) would rewrite four adapters, two of which wrap synchronous vendor clients, to fix a defect that lives in one operator. | The prefetch queue holds up to Reactor's small-buffer size of records while the listener is slow, so a stall now shows as a bounded queue and then as TCP back-pressure rather than as an immediately stalled loop; records are held in memory for that interval and are lost if the process dies, exactly as they were before. Dispatch remains strictly sequential, so a slow provider still delays the next record — bounded now by the queue rather than by the loop. One `boundedElastic` worker is occupied for the lifetime of each connection. |
| DL-221 | The Notion transport names its request factory: `config/RestClientConfig` builds `ClientHttpRequestFactoryBuilder.jdk()` with the configured connect and read timeouts, instead of `ClientHttpRequestFactoryBuilder.detect()`. | (a) `detect()`, which is what the class did. (b) `httpComponents()`. (c) `simple()`. (d) `reactor()` named explicitly. (e) Leave the factory detected and rely on callers never being on a non-blocking thread. | `detect()` resolves against the classpath, and because `spring-boot-starter-webflux` is a declared dependency for the X streaming path it resolved to `ReactorClientHttpRequestFactory` — a factory that implements a synchronous call by blocking on a reactive one. That made the adapter's behaviour depend on the caller's thread: identical calls succeeded from the scheduler's thread and were refused from a reactive thread. The JDK factory is backed by `java.net.http.HttpClient`, is part of the platform on Java 21, adds no dependency, and blocks on its own without Reactor's marker, so a Notion call behaves the same from any thread. (b) is unavailable: only Apache HttpClient 4.5.14 is on the classpath, transitively through `google-cloud-language`, and that builder requires HttpClient 5. (c) is `HttpURLConnection`, whose `PATCH` support needs a workaround, and `PATCH /v1/pages/{id}` is one of the three calls this transport carries. (d) makes the thread coupling explicit but keeps it. (e) is what DL-220 fixes for the ingestion path, but it leaves the adapter thread-sensitive for every future caller; the two changes are independent and both are kept. | The transport implementation is now pinned by this application rather than tracking what the framework detects, so a future move to a different client is a code change here. The JDK client negotiates HTTP/2 where the server offers it and falls back to HTTP/1.1 otherwise, and it maintains its own connection pool with its own defaults; neither is configured by this application. The two configured timeouts are the only bounds applied. |
| DL-222 | `task/TweetStreamClient` bounds the bytes it accumulates for one newline-delimited record at `MAX_RECORD_BYTES` = 1 MiB, held as a code constant. A record that reaches the bound is discarded: the accumulator is emptied, one `WARN` naming the byte count and the bound is recorded, and the bytes up to and including that record's next line feed are dropped, after which accumulation resumes and the following record is delivered normally. | (a) No bound, which is what the class did. (b) Fail the connection so the reconnect path handles it. (c) Emit the truncated prefix as a record and let the JSON parse fail. (d) Make the bound a configuration property. (e) Bound the codec buffer instead, through `maxInMemorySize`. | The body is consumed as raw `DataBuffer`s, so no codec limit applies, and the accumulator grew for as long as bytes arrived without a line feed: 4 MB was buffered with no record delivered, no log record and no failure, and nothing capped the growth, so a provider or intermediary that never emits a line feed grows the heap without limit. 1 MiB is three orders of magnitude above a post of the filtered stream carrying the requested fields, and it keeps the 300 000-character record the suite already exercises working unchanged. Discarding and skipping to the next line feed matches the policy this class already applies to an unreadable record — log and skip, never end the connection — so one oversized record costs one record rather than a reconnection cycle. (b) turns a single malformed record into a reconnection with backoff, which is the behaviour the malformed-record path deliberately avoids. (c) hands the parser a prefix that cannot be valid JSON and reports it as an unreadable record, which misnames the condition. (d) adds a key to `application.yml`, which is closed to new keys, for a bound no deployment has a reason to tune. (e) does not apply to a raw-buffer body and would also change how every other X response is read. | A genuine record larger than 1 MiB would be dropped rather than delivered; the filtered stream does not produce one, and the `WARN` names the byte count so the condition is visible if it ever occurs. The bound is not operator-tunable without a code change. The discard is per record, so a body that never emits a line feed is dropped 1 MiB at a time with one `WARN` per megabyte rather than accumulating. |
| DL-223 | `task/TweetStreamListener.onStatus` requires every member a stored column declares required to carry its wire type before any row is written, and applies one rule to all of them: `data.text`, `data.public_metrics.like_count`, `data.created_at` and `data.author_id` are read first, a missing or wrong-typed member is named at `WARN`, the record is skipped and `true` is returned to continue streaming. No neutral value is substituted for any of them, and the guard sits before the sentiment call so no provider call is spent on a record that cannot be stored. `readCreatedAt` therefore returns `null` for an absent, blank, non-textual or unparseable value rather than a substitute. | (a) Store the row and leave the column empty, which is what the listener did for `author_id` and `created_at`. (b) Substitute `LocalDateTime.now(ZoneOffset.UTC)` for an absent or unusable `created_at` and store the row, reporting the substitution at `WARN`. (c) Give `service/mapper/TweetMapper` a documented substitute for an empty column, for example the `ABSENT_TEXT` constant it declares. (d) Coerce a numeric `author_id` to text so fewer records lack one. (e) Add `nullable = false` to the columns so the database refuses the row. | `dto/TweetDto` declares `content`, `like_count`, `created_at`, `doubt_rating` and `user_id` required (DL-080), so under (a) the listener knowingly stored a row no downstream step could use: the Notion mirror failed inside the mapper before any request was issued, response generation failed for the same reason, and because the scheduler selects candidates by the absence of a response the row was retried on every pass indefinitely. Enforcing the requirement at the listener — the only writer of new `tweets` rows — keeps the wire contract, the mapper and the API exactly as they are. One rule for all four members is preferred over (b) because a substituted `created_at` is indistinguishable in the table from a delivered one and silently shifts the day bucket `service/AnalyticsService.getTrends` groups on, so it trades an unrenderable row for a wrong number; X sends `created_at` whenever it is requested, which this client always does, so the record being declined is one the provider should not have delivered. (c) and (d) would put a fabricated author or timestamp on the wire. (e) is a schema change, which this migration forbids. | A record the provider delivers incomplete is dropped rather than stored in a degraded form, so an operator reading only `INFO` and above sees the `WARN` naming the members but has no row to inspect afterwards; the count of skipped records is not aggregated anywhere. Rows written with an empty `user_id` or `created_at` by earlier builds remain unrenderable, and no migration mechanism is available under the no-schema-change mandate of the frozen schema — an accepted limitation. |
| DL-224 | `task/TweetStreamListener.mirrorToNotion` reports at two levels: a failure raised while `service/mapper/TweetMapper` prepares the wire form is recorded here at `WARN`, and a failure raised by `service/NotionService.storeTweet` stays at `DEBUG`. The preparation step is guarded separately from the adapter call. | (a) One `try` around both steps at `DEBUG`, which is what the method did. (b) One `try` around both steps at `WARN` or `ERROR`. (c) Rethrow a preparation failure. (d) Move the preparation outside the guard so a failure ends `onStatus`. | DL-197 places a provider failure's record with the adapter that owns it, which is why the adapter call is logged at `DEBUG` here: `service/NotionService` already reports it at `ERROR` with the provider's status, code and request id. A preparation failure has no such owner — it is raised before any adapter is reached — so under (a) the only record of a mirror that never happened was a `DEBUG` line, invisible under the shipped `SCANNER_LOG_LEVEL` of `INFO`. Splitting the guard reports each failure exactly once, at the level that matches whether it is reported anywhere else. (b) would duplicate every adapter failure at `WARN` beside the adapter's own `ERROR`. (c) and (d) would let a mirror failure stop the response-generation trigger, contradicting the secondary-mirror rule that a failed mirror leaves the stored row in place and does not stop the trigger. | The mirror path now emits records at two levels, so an operator reading only `WARN` and above sees preparation failures but not adapter failures, and must read the adapter's own `ERROR` for the latter — which is where the provider detail lives. With DL-223 in place a preparation failure is not reachable from ingestion at all, so the `WARN` guards against a future contract drift rather than naming an expected condition. |
| DL-225 | `service/TwitterService.getPaginatedTweets` and `service/ResponseService.getPaginatedResponses` answer every `page` value, including one naming a page whose first row lies at an offset beyond `Integer.MAX_VALUE` — the largest offset `org.springframework.data.jpa.support.PageableUtils` lets a paged query express. Both call the single shared predicate `util/QueryParameters.withinQueryableOffset(Pageable)`, which reads the offset from the `Pageable` itself so no product of two `int` values can overflow, and beyond the ceiling both serve `new PageImpl<>(List.of(), requested, repository.count())`: Spring Data's own counters then build the envelope, restating the requested `page` and `per_page` and carrying the true `total` and `total_pages`, exactly as every other page beyond the last row already reports. No page size is reduced, no `per_page` maximum is introduced and no pagination argument is answered with an error status. | (a) Leave the arithmetic to Spring Data, which raises `InvalidDataAccessApiUsageException` ("Page offset exceeds Integer.MAX_VALUE") for such a request — the delivered behaviour. (b) Reject the request with 400 and a new error literal. (c) Clamp the page number to the last populated page and serve that page's rows. (d) Introduce a `per_page` maximum so the product cannot overflow. (e) Handle `InvalidDataAccessApiUsageException` in `api/GlobalExceptionHandler` and translate it there. | The route already has a defined answer for a page that holds no row, and it is used for every other out-of-range value: `page=0`, `page=-5`, `page=101` and `page=1000000` all answer 200 with an empty list and a populated block. (a) made one arithmetic band — `(page - 1) x per_page > 2147483647`, reached from a single query parameter on both vendors — the only page value answered 500, and the inconsistency is the defect: nothing about that page differs from `page=1000000` except the size of the number. It is also a cheap amplifier, since one such request writes a stack trace roughly twelve times the size of a successful response's log record for about the same latency. (b) invents a status and a literal the source route never had, which endpoint parity and wire-contract parity close. (c) would report rows for a page that was not asked for, so a client walking pages would silently receive the last page forever. (d) is forbidden: this migration excludes adding a `per_page` maximum, and the unbounded size is parity-preserved (DL-123, DL-200). (e) would place pagination arithmetic in the error path and answer 200 from an advice that exists to answer failures, and it would still let the query be built and rejected. | The clamped path issues `count()` where the repository page would have issued a count and a select, so a request in this band costs one statement rather than none — the trade for a correct `total` and `total_pages`. The empty list is indistinguishable by the client from a page that is genuinely empty, which is the existing behaviour for every other beyond-last page. `total_pages` is computed here rather than read from a `Page`, so the two must be kept in step if Spring Data's rounding ever changes; both are integer divisions of the same two values today, and the tests assert them against `Math.ceil` of the same quotient. |
| DL-226 | `service/ResponseService` declares the generate-if-absent operation as **two distinctly named methods, not two overloads of one name**: `generateResponseIfAbsent(String)` for a caller that holds only the raw identifier, and `generateResponseIfAbsentFor(Tweet)` for a caller that already holds the row. Both order the pre-provider existence read of DL-252 ahead of the model call. Both reach one private body, which takes the in-process claim, resolves the subject — from the supplied row when there is one and from `TweetRepository.findById` when there is not — calls the generator and stores the row under the same transaction-scoped `existsByTweetId` guard. `task/ResponseGenerationScheduler` passes the row `TweetRepository.findByResponsesIsEmpty()` selected; `task/TweetStreamListener` keeps passing the identifier. | (a) Keep the identifier-only signature and let each candidate be read again — the delivered behaviour. (b) Give the scheduler a second, entity-shaped service method that duplicates the claim, guard and storing logic. (c) Have the scheduler map the row to `dto/TweetDto` itself and pass that. (d) Make the pass transactional so the candidate rows stay managed and the re-read is served from the persistence context. (e) Select only identifiers in the candidate query and accept the per-row read as necessary. (f) Declare the two as overloads of one name `generateResponseIfAbsent`, which this row previously described. | The candidate query already selects every column of every candidate, so the second read returns rows the caller is holding: measured at exactly `1 + N` statements and `N` extra pool checkouts per pass — 403 statements for 400 candidates, 19 for 16 — and the count grows without bound with the unanswered backlog, which is the amplification (a) leaves in place. Both methods reach one private body, so the claim of DL-196, the existence guard, the stored column values and every client-visible literal are asserted once and cannot drift, which is what (b) would risk. The two names are distinct rather than overloaded because a raw identifier and a row are different subjects for a caller to pass and an overload set makes the call site read ambiguously; an earlier revision of this row described them as one overloaded name, which the tree never carried. (c) would put wire mapping in the task layer and give the scheduler a mapper collaborator it otherwise has no use for. (d) would hold a transaction open across the language-model call, which DL-086 excludes deliberately. (e) treats the symptom as unavoidable: the query it would replace is the same query, and the pass would still read one row per candidate. (f) is what this row asserted and the tree never carried: a call site passing a `Tweet` and one passing a `String` would resolve by argument type alone, so a reader could not tell from the name which subject form is in play, and a future third form would have to be distinguished by type too. | The supplied row is detached — the pass declares no transaction and `open-in-view` is false — so the generator must read only the columns the candidate query loaded; reading a lazy association from it would fail, which is why the mapper reads scalars only and the association is never traversed. The row is also a snapshot: a column edited between the candidate query and the generation call is not seen. The storing transaction still reads the row it attaches the reply to, so a candidate deleted mid-pass is still reported as a generation failure and no orphan reply is written. |
| DL-227 | The pacing of the response-generation pass is read in exactly one place. `config/AsyncSchedulingConfig` resolves the `response_generation_delay` row once per pass, when it computes the next execution instant, and `task/ResponseGenerationScheduler` resolves nothing: it holds no `SettingRepository`, no `ScannerProperties` and no completion instant, declares no delay resolver of its own — `resolveDelay()` is private to `config/AsyncSchedulingConfig` — and runs a pass on every tick. The first tick is the startup instant itself — see DL-251. | (a) Resolve the interval in both places — in the trigger to schedule the tick and again in the pass to defer a tick that arrived early — which is the delivered behaviour. (b) Keep both readers but have the trigger hand its resolved value to the pass. (c) Move the resolution into the pass and register a fixed-delay task on the configured value alone. (d) Cache the row's value in a field and re-read it on a schedule of its own. | A fixed-delay trigger is already the authority on when a pass runs, so a second resolution inside the pass answers a question that has been answered: it read the same row a second time on every pass — the `+2` of the measured `1 + N + 2` statements — and its deferral branch could only fire for a row edited in the window between the two reads. (b) removes the second read but couples the task to the trigger's internals for a guard that has no remaining purpose. (c) would lose the settings-row precedence DL-040 and DL-197 establish, since a `fixedDelayString` is bound once at registration. (d) adds state and a staleness window to replace a single indexed primary-key read. | The pass no longer refuses to run: a tick executes it, so anything that invokes `generatePendingResponses()` outside the trigger — a test, or a future caller — runs a full pass immediately rather than being paced. Raising the row while the scheduler waits no longer suppresses the pass that is already scheduled; that pass runs and the raised value paces the next one, the same one-pass lag DL-228 records for lowering it. |
| DL-228 | The instant of the next response-generation pass is computed once, at the completion of the previous pass, from the interval in force at that moment, and is not revised while the scheduler waits for it. The first pass is not spaced this way: it runs at the startup instant, which DL-251 owns. An edit to `response_generation_delay` therefore takes effect from the pass after the one already scheduled: lowering the row cannot pull the waiting pass forward, and raising it cannot push it back. This is recorded rather than changed, and it is stated in the javadoc of `config/AsyncSchedulingConfig.nextResponseGenerationPass`. | (a) Leave the semantics undocumented, which is how they were delivered. (b) Reschedule the task when `PUT /settings/response_generation_delay` writes the row, by cancelling the scheduled future and registering a new one. (c) Poll the row on a short fixed interval and run a pass only when the elapsed time satisfies it — the deferral loop DL-227 withdrew. (d) Register the task at a fixed short cadence and let the pass decide whether to work. | The behaviour is Spring's `Trigger` contract, it satisfies fixed-delay scheduling and DL-047 exactly — the interval is measured from completion, not from start — and the operator-visible effect is bounded by one interval, whose default is 60 seconds. (b) would give the settings route a handle on the scheduler's registered task, coupling an HTTP handler to scheduling internals and introducing cancellation and race handling for a bound that is already one pass. (c) trades one indexed read per pass for many, and reintroduces exactly the duplicate resolution DL-227 removed. (d) makes the cadence of the tick a second pacing value that must be kept consistent with the row. | An operator who lowers the row and watches for activity sees nothing until the scheduled instant arrives — up to the previous interval — and may read that silence as a fault; the javadoc is the only place that says otherwise, and the running service emits no record when a tick is not yet due. A very large row value therefore also delays its own replacement by one interval. |
| DL-229 | **Superseded by DL-270 and DL-271.** `config/DataSourceConfig.dataSource()` no longer carries `@ConfigurationProperties(prefix = "spring.datasource.hikari")`. The pool surface is now the allowlisted `scanner.datasource.pool.*` record of DL-270, the geometry defaults and their accepted ranges are DL-271, and the whole `spring.datasource.*` surface is unread — a connection-identity key among sixteen refuses to start and any other key under that prefix is reported at `WARN` and ignored. This identifier is retained so a citation from any commit resolves; `config/DataSourceConfigTest` remains the covering class. | (a) Delete this row and renumber. (b) Leave the row describing the framework-prefix binding and let it contradict the code. (c) Rewrite it as the new decision under this identifier rather than pointing at successors. | The framework-prefix binding this row authorised bound every key under that prefix *after* the translated identity had been applied, so `spring.datasource.hikari.jdbc-url`, `.username` and `.password` were a second, unvalidated way to point the service at a database — the risk this row's own Risks column recorded, which a review then raised as a finding. Two successors were opened rather than one because the surface and the capacity bounds are separate subjects with separate alternatives, and this log's ownership rule allows one subject per entry. (a) breaks every citation in an earlier commit. (b) is the failure Rule 1 exists to prevent. (c) would give one identifier two subjects. | A reader who arrives here from an older comment must follow two pointers rather than one. The delivered pool is configurable through a prefix that is this project's own rather than the framework's, so the Spring Boot documentation a deployment already has does not describe it; `application.yml` carries the accepted range of every key inline for that reason. |
| DL-230 | The two short request/response calls `task/TweetStreamClient` makes on the X API — the app-only token exchange at `POST /oauth2/token` and the rule listing and mutation at `GET`/`POST /2/tweets/search/stream/rules` — carry a per-request bound taken from `scanner.twitter.request-timeout-seconds`, default 10 and never read as less than one second. The filtered-stream subscription carries no bound at all. The bound is read on every call, so a change needs no restart, and a call that exceeds it fails the ingestion attempt, which the existing reconnection path answers with its exponential backoff. | (a) Leave all four X calls unbounded, as the transport bean does — the delivered behaviour. (b) Set `responseTimeout` on the shared `WebClient` in `config/WebClientConfig`. (c) Publish a second, bounded `WebClient` for the control plane and keep the unbounded one for the stream. (d) Hardcode the bound as a constant in the client rather than declaring a property. (e) Bound the calls with `Mono.timeout` plus a retry of their own, independent of the reconnection path. | Reactor Netty applies no response timeout by default, so (a) leaves the token exchange and the rules calls able to wait indefinitely on a peer that accepts a connection and never answers — a state that stalls ingestion silently, since the ingestion cycle is the only thing waiting and it emits nothing while it waits. (b) cannot be used: the same client carries the filtered stream, whose body is long-lived by design, and a response timeout there would cut a healthy connection — DL-193. (c) is sound and was rejected only on size: it adds a second bean and a second base URL to keep in step for three call sites that a per-request `timeout` already covers, and the property makes the value visible either way. (d) hides an operational value in code, unlike the two other adapters, whose bounds are `scanner.notion.connect-timeout-seconds` / `read-timeout-seconds` (DL-150) and `scanner.openai.request-timeout-seconds` (DL-146); the property also lets a test drive the bound. (e) would put a second retry policy beside the one the ingestion cycle already owns, so a slow peer would be retried twice over with two different policies. | A bound that is too short turns a slow but healthy X response into a reconnection, and the shortest accepted value is one second, so a misconfiguration can make the control plane unusable while the log records only the reconnections. The stream body stays unbounded by design, so a connection that is established and then goes silent without a keep-alive is still detected only by the transport, not by this bound. The group `scanner.twitter` now carries one component that is not a credential, so its `toString` renders that value in the clear while the seven credentials stay redacted. |
| DL-231 | `dto/UpdateResponseRequest`'s compact canonical constructor refuses a carried key whose value the addressed column cannot hold: `content` is accepted only as a JSON string or an explicit JSON `null`, and `is_approved` only as a JSON boolean or an explicit JSON `null`. The refusal is an `IllegalArgumentException`, which Jackson reports as `ValueInstantiationException` and the request-body converter as `HttpMessageNotReadableException`, so the existing DL-092 handler answers 400 `{"error": "Bad request"}` and `service/ResponseService.updateResponse` is never entered. The whole body is refused, so a mixed body such as `{"content":"x","is_approved":"true"}` writes nothing. The answer names no member on the wire; the offending key and the type it carried are recorded in the log only. `api/ResponseControllerTest` drives twelve refused bodies through the route and asserts the envelope names no member, and `service/ResponseServiceTest` refuses ten at the record. An explicit `null` is not a refusal — DL-244 — and a body carrying neither key is not a refusal either, since it reaches the `Update data is required` literal of DL-082. | (a) Coerce, which is the form this tree carried before: `asText()` on `content` and `asBoolean()` on `is_approved`. (b) Treat an unwritable carried member as absent, so a body carrying only unwritable members falls through to `Update data is required` and a mixed body writes its writable member. (c) Answer an unwritable member with `Update data is required` too, widening that literal. (d) Add a `BadRequestException` literal naming the offending member. (e) Bind `String content` and `Boolean isApproved` and let Jackson raise on the type mismatch. | (a) is the defect this row corrects, and it was silent: `{"content":123}` stored `"123"`, `{"content":["a"]}` stored the rendered text `["a"]`, `{"content":{"x":1}}` stored `{"x":1}`, and `{"is_approved":"false"}` stored `true`, because `asBoolean()` reads a non-empty text node as true — four stored values the client never sent, each committed with 200 and a body reporting the coerced value as though it had been asked for. Refusing the body reports a fault the client can act on, at the boundary that knows the two column types. (b) answers a mixed body with 200 having silently ignored the member the client asked to change, which is the same silence in a different place. (c) would make a literal transcribed from the presence guard at `backend/app/api/responses.py:L56` cover a condition that guard never described. (d) would add a fourth literal to a set endpoint parity freezes at three. (e) would lose the presence-versus-value distinction, since an absent key and an explicit `null` both arrive as a Java `null`. This is not Bean Validation and adds no constraint annotation, so the two-`@NotNull` validation parity this migration is held to is untouched: `ResponseControllerTest` asserts that this record declares no constraint annotation at all. | A client that relied on coercion now receives 400 and must send both members at their declared types. The answer carries no member name, so a caller must read the two column types from the contract rather than from the response. The two update routes agree only in part: `PUT /settings/{key}` answers the same 400 `Bad request` for `{"value":["a","b"]}` and `{"value":{"a":1}}`, but Jackson's default scalar-to-`String` coercion still binds `{"value":42}` to `"42"` and `{"value":true}` to `"true"` there, because `dto/UpdateSettingRequest` holds a `String` and this migration adds no guard to a route no finding named — so a wrong-typed scalar is refused on the response route and coerced on the setting route. The guard lives in a record constructor, so a direct `new UpdateResponseRequest(...)` call in application or test code sees `IllegalArgumentException` rather than a 400; only the binding path produces the envelope. |
| DL-232 | `responses.generated_at` is minted at one site — `service/ResponseService.store`, inside the transaction that inserts the row — as `LocalDateTime.now().truncatedTo(ChronoUnit.MICROS)`. The value is server-side and local, is never taken from a request body and is never rewritten by `updateResponse`; the truncation fixes its precision at microseconds, which is the precision every vendor this artifact supports stores in the column: PostgreSQL `timestamp`, the `datetime(6)` Hibernate maps a `LocalDateTime` to on MySQL, and H2 `TIMESTAMP`. This entry owns the mint, which the split recorded in DL-077 assigned to DL-201 and DL-201 no longer carries. An earlier form minted `LocalDateTime.now()` untruncated. | (a) Mint `LocalDateTime.now()` untruncated, which is what the code did. (b) Truncate to seconds or milliseconds. (c) Let the database default the column (`insertable = false` with a DDL default). (d) Flush and refresh the row after the insert so the returned entity carries the value the column actually stored. (e) Serialise the timestamp with a fixed-precision Jackson formatter and leave the stored value alone. (f) Switch the column to `Instant` with a time zone. | Under (a) the 201 body of `POST /responses` carried nanosecond precision — measured `2026-08-05T16:13:13.54488515` — while every later read of the same row, and the column itself, carried `2026-08-05T16:13:13.544885`: the same instant reported two ways, so a client that cached the create body and compared it to a read by string equality saw a mismatch that no requirement asked for. Truncating at the mint makes the two equal by construction and costs one call; it also keeps the value the service reports identical to the value the column holds without a second database round trip, which is what (d) buys at the price of a flush and a refresh on every generation. Microseconds rather than (b) because microseconds is what the column stores: truncating further would discard precision the database is willing to keep, and the mismatch this closes is between the mint and the column, not between the mint and a coarser ideal. (c) moves the value out of the application, so the entity returned by the insert would carry `null` until it was re-read, and it would need DDL this project does not write (DL-026 leaves the schema to `ddl-auto`). (e) hides the difference on the wire while leaving the stored and in-memory values different, so anything reading the entity in the same transaction still sees the untruncated value. (f) changes the column type, which data-model parity freezes: `backend/app/db/models.py:L25` declares `DateTime`, which maps to `LocalDateTime`. | A vendor whose column keeps less than microsecond precision — MySQL `datetime` without a fractional-seconds argument, for instance — would still round the stored value, so the equality this entry establishes holds for the mapped types this artifact produces rather than for every conceivable column definition. Two rows generated within the same microsecond now carry the same timestamp, which was already possible and is not used for ordering: `@OrderBy("id ASC")` (DL-024's association ordering) and the pagination sort are by identifier. The truncation is invisible in the wire format itself, since ISO-8601 omits trailing zeros, so a reader cannot tell from one body whether the value was truncated or simply landed on a microsecond boundary. |
| DL-233 | `service/SentimentAnalysisService.analyzeSentiment(String)` rejects a document sentiment score that is not a finite number: `Double.NaN`, `POSITIVE_INFINITY` and `NEGATIVE_INFINITY` each raise `IllegalStateException` at the provider boundary, before the score is returned or logged as a measurement. The rejection is written by the existing provider-seam `catch`, which records the exception type only (DL-197), and reaches the client as 500 `{"error": "Internal server error"}` — the same status and body a provider exception already produces. No score is clamped, rounded or substituted: a finite score, including one outside `-1.0`…`1.0`, is returned exactly as received. `calculateDoubtRating(double)` is unchanged and still maps a non-finite argument the way the source expression does, so the arithmetic transcribed from `backend/app/services/sentiment_analysis.py:L26-35` keeps its behaviour for any other caller. | (a) Return the non-finite score, which is what the adapter did. (b) Substitute a neutral score such as `0.0`. (c) Clamp to `-1.0`…`1.0`. (d) Leave the adapter alone and configure Jackson to write a non-finite double as JSON `null`. (e) Leave the adapter alone and reject the value in `api/TweetController` or `service/TwitterService` before the response is built. (f) Reject it in `calculateDoubtRating` instead. | `analysis_result` is documented as the bare score and is a JSON number (DL-037). (b) and (c) report a measurement that was never taken, the fault DL-075 and DL-080 both name, and (c) would additionally make an out-of-range-but-real score indistinguishable from a broken one — the doubt rating already clamps, and clamping the input as well would erase the distinction the clamp exists to handle. (d) fixes one endpoint's serialisation while leaving the broken value in the doubt rating written to the column, and it changes how every double in the application serialises. (e) puts a provider contract check two layers away from the provider, where the same check would have to be repeated by `task/TweetStreamListener`, the other caller. (f) is the wrong owner: that method is a transcription of source arithmetic (DL-036) whose documented behaviour for a non-finite argument is part of the port, and the guard belongs where the value enters the system. | A provider that ever legitimately reported a non-finite score would make the analyze route answer 500 rather than 200 — accepted, because the documented range makes that a contract breach on the provider's side. The check is one comparison per call and cannot be reached from configuration, so an operator cannot opt out of it. `calculateDoubtRating` still accepts a non-finite argument and still answers 10.0 for `NaN`, so the two members now disagree about what a non-finite value means; that is deliberate — one is a provider boundary and the other is transcribed arithmetic — and it means a future caller could reach the rating path with a value `analyzeSentiment` would have rejected. |

(c) `RANDOM_PORT` with the four vendor adapters mocked. (d) Split the class in two — one context-refresh smoke test and one HTTP-surface test. (e) Drop the route and error-dispatch cases on the grounds that the `@WebMvcTest` slices already cover them. | Every assertion this class makes is reachable without a connector, which was established by converting them and running the class: the filter chain, the entry point, the challenge header, the token route, an authenticated read and both direct requests to `/error` all behave identically under `MockMvc`, because a direct request to `/error` is an ordinary request to `BasicErrorController` rather than a container ERROR dispatch. (a) therefore paid for a server, a port and a server thread for no assertion, and made the widest class in the suite sensitive to connector timing. (b) would delete the assertion that ingestion is stopped — the executable form of the never-auto-post invariant — and would leave DL-019, DL-021, DL-046 and DL-183 asserted nowhere end to end. (c) buys nothing once the adapters are known to be lazy. (d) would cache a further application context without adding an assertion. (e) removes the only place the eleven routes are driven through the whole stack. | A defect that appears only through a real connector — a `sendError` becoming a container ERROR dispatch, or connector-level request framing — is not reachable here; that class of behaviour is covered by `config/HttpChainIntegrationTest`, one of the two classes in the suite that start a container, and the two files must be kept in step so the connector path does not become uncovered. Because nothing is mocked, an adapter that a later change makes contact a host or resolve a credential during construction would fail here first. The stopped-ingestion case holds only while the `test` profile keeps both X consumer credentials blank, so `src/test/resources/application-test.yml` and this class stay coupled. |
| DL-240 | The embedded Tomcat version is pinned to 10.1.57 through the `<tomcat.version>` property the Spring Boot parent exposes, overriding the 10.1.55 the 3.5.16 BOM resolves. | (a) Keep the BOM-resolved 10.1.55. (b) Move the whole project to a newer Spring Boot 3.5.x whose BOM resolves a patched Tomcat. (c) Pin each `tomcat-embed-*` coordinate individually in `dependencyManagement`. (d) Switch the container to Jetty or Undertow. | The BOM-resolved version carries published vulnerabilities that the 10.1.57 patch release closes, and the property the parent already declares is the mechanism Spring Boot documents for exactly this substitution, so one line moves every `tomcat-embed-*` artifact together. (a) leaves a known-vulnerable container in the artifact. (b) would change the Spring Boot line the frozen plan fixes at 3.5.16. (c) achieves the same result with four coordinates that can drift apart. (d) replaces the container the frozen plan names and would leave the container-level error path of DL-237 unhandled. | The pin has to be revisited whenever the Spring Boot version moves, since a BOM that resolves a newer Tomcat would then be held back by this property; the version is stated in one place so the check is a single line. A Tomcat patch release can change connector behaviour, so the container-level assertions of `config/ContainerErrorResponseConfigTest` and `config/HttpChainIntegrationTest` are what detect it. |
| DL-241 | `com.mysql:mysql-connector-j` is pinned to 26.7.0, replacing the 9.7.0 the parent BOM resolves. The driver must stay on the classpath at run time because `scanner.database-url` is an opaque string whose vendor is unresolved at compile time, which is the dialect-agnostic requirement DL-027 and DL-028 hold. (a) leaves a known-vulnerable driver in the artifact. (b) would resolve the vendor at build time, which the migration boundary forbids. (c) and (d) make a working deployment depend on a step the artifact does not carry, against the no-manual-intervention requirement. | The pin is a major-version jump from the BOM's line, so a MySQL deployment should be smoke-tested against it; the delivered schema was executed once against a real MySQL 8.4 during remediation. The pin must be revisited when the Spring Boot version moves. |
| DL-242 | `com.h2database:h2` is declared at `test` scope rather than `runtime`, so the in-memory database is present for `mvn clean verify` and absent from the executable jar. (a) enlarges the deployed artifact's attack surface. (b) achieves the same outcome through plugin configuration rather than through the scope the dependency actually has. (c) is the option DL-009 already rejected. | A developer who wants the packaged jar to run against H2 must both add the coordinate back at `runtime` scope and supply the URL in `jdbc:h2:` form, because the translator resolves no `h2` scheme (DL-071).
