| DL-243 | `infrastructure/docker/Dockerfile.backend` runs the application on Eclipse Temurin 21.0.12+8. The runtime stage starts from the digest-pinned `eclipse-temurin` image the frozen plan names, downloads the 21.0.12+8 JRE from the Adoptium API, verifies it against a literal SHA-256 checksum recorded in the Dockerfile, and replaces the image's own JVM with it. | (a) Use the base image's bundled JVM unchanged. (b) Move the base image tag to a Temurin image that already ships 21.0.12. (c) Install a JDK from the distribution's package manager. (d) Build a custom runtime with `jlink`. (e) Use a different vendor's 21.0.12 image. | The base image's bundled JVM predates the July 2026 quarterly patch set, and Temurin has published no container image for 21.0.12, so (b) is not available — verified against the tag list. Fetching the vendor's own archive and verifying its checksum keeps the vendor, the version and the verification explicit in one file, and leaves the base image the frozen plan names in place. (a) ships an unpatched JVM. (c) introduces a second package source and a version this project does not control. (d) changes the runtime shape and would have to be re-derived on every dependency change. (e) changes the vendor the frozen plan names. | The checksum and the version are literals in the Dockerfile, so a future patch level needs both updated together; a mismatch fails the build loudly rather than silently installing the wrong bytes. The build needs network access to the Adoptium API, so an air-gapped build must mirror that archive. The download adds a layer and roughly one JRE's worth of build time. |
| DL-244 | `dto/UpdateResponseRequest` admits an explicit JSON `null` on either carried key and treats it as a write of `null`, so `{"content":null}` and `{"is_approved":null}` bind without error and reach `service/ResponseService.updateResponse`, while a carried key of any other unwritable type does not (DL-231). The write is then tested against the wire contract: `dto/ResponseDto` declares both `content` and `is_approved` required (DL-080) and those two columns are the only ones this route writes, so an accepted explicit `null` always leaves a member the wire contract declares required empty. `updateResponse` reports that with the route's own literal from `backend/app/api/responses.py:L65` — 404 `{"error": "Response not found or update failed"}` — and the transaction rolls back, leaving the row exactly as it was. `repository/JpaMappingIntegrationTest` measures that against a real transaction: after `{"content":null}` the stored row still carries its original text and flag. `{}` still answers the `Update data is required` literal of DL-082. | (a) Refuse an explicit `null` in the record as an unwritable value, so `{"content":null}` is answered 400 `Bad request` alongside the wrong-typed bodies. (b) Bind the two components as `Optional<String>` and `Optional<Boolean>`. (c) Bind them as plain `String` and `Boolean`. (d) Add a separate `clear` flag per column. (e) Let the `null` reach `service/mapper/ResponseMapper` and escape as the DL-092 500, which is what this tree did before the required-member guard existed. (f) Relax `dto/ResponseDto` so a `null` member is renderable and the clear succeeds. | Validation parity permits only what the Pydantic schemas declared — types and optionality, and no constraint beyond them — and `responses.content` and `responses.is_approved` are both nullable in the frozen schema, so (a) would add a constraint the source never expressed and would make the record stricter than the column it writes. The refusal DL-231 performs is therefore about type and not about nullness. The row that results still has to be renderable, and `backend/app/schema/response.py:L6,L8` declares both members required, so the write cannot be committed: reporting it with the literal the route already declares for a failed update, and rolling back, keeps the answer inside the closed three-literal set and leaves no row in a state the wire contract cannot render. (e) is the defect that guard closes — a required-field failure escaping as `Internal server error` on a body this route can answer itself. (f) would change a DTO transcribed from the source schema, which parity freezes. (b) was tested and does not work: Jackson delivers both an absent key and an explicit `null` as `Optional.empty()`, which is why the record keeps raw `JsonNode` components. (c) makes `{}` and `{"content":null}` indistinguishable. (d) invents wire members the source route never had. | An explicit `null` is admitted at the binding boundary and rejected at the persistence boundary, so two bodies that both fail to write receive two different statuses — 400 for a wrong type, 404 for a `null`. The 404 literal is shared with an identifier that names no row and with a lock that stayed contended (DL-246), so a client cannot tell from the response which of the three happened; the distinction is in the log only. Clearing either column is consequently not a capability of this route, and no route in the service clears them: a caller wanting an empty `content` must send `{"content":""}`, which is a different stored value. The two update routes do not answer an explicit `null` identically — `PUT /settings/{key}` refuses one at binding under the `@NotNull` on `dto/UpdateSettingRequest.value`, answering 400 `No value provided`. If a future `ResponseDto` admitted a nullable member, this guard would have to be revisited, and nothing in the build ties the two together. |
| DL-245 | A page of responses is read as `ResponseRepository.ResponseRow`, a closed interface projection selecting the five `responses` columns plus `tweet.id`, with an explicit `countQuery`. `service/mapper/ResponseMapper` carries an overload that maps that projection, so rendering a page loads no `tweets` entity and traverses no association. | (a) Keep the entity page with an entity graph over `tweet`. (b) Keep the entity page and read only `response.getTweet().getId()`, relying on the lazy proxy's identifier. (c) Select a constructor expression into the DTO directly. (d) Add a plain `tweetId` column to the entity beside the association. | `r.tweet.id` resolves from the owning foreign-key column without joining the parent, so the projection reads one table where the entity page read two. (a) fetches every `tweets` column to render a `tweet_id`. (b) is the delivered behaviour and works only while the proxy is initialised within the session; with `open-in-view` false a detached proxy raises instead. (c) would put the wire shape in a JPQL string, so the DTO's required-column invariant would move out of `service/mapper/ResponseMapper`. (d) duplicates the foreign key in the entity, which data-model parity forbids. | A derived count query cannot be inferred for a projection query, so the count is a second JPQL string that must be kept in step with the selection's `from` clause; both are in one interface and `repository/JpaMappingIntegrationTest` asserts the statement count. The mapper now has two entry points for one wire shape, and a required-column invariant added to one must be added to the other. |
| DL-246 | Both pessimistic-lock reads carry a bounded wait at two levels. At statement level `TweetRepository.findByIdForUpdate` and `ResponseRepository.findByIdForUpdate` both set the portable `jakarta.persistence.query.timeout` hint to `ResponseRepository.LOCK_WAIT_MILLIS` (5000 ms). At transaction level `ResponseService.updateResponse` declares `@Transactional(timeout = 5)`, and the storage path — which runs through a `TransactionTemplate` because its caller is a private method whose self-invocation would bypass the proxy — holds a bounded *copy* of the injected template: `boundedCopyOf` reads the injected instance's `PlatformTransactionManager`, builds `new TransactionTemplate(manager, injected)` so every other attribute is carried over, sets the five-second timeout on the copy, and refuses to construct the service at all when the injected template carries no manager. The shared bean is never mutated. A caller that cannot obtain the lock within the bound receives `PessimisticLockingFailureException` or `QueryTimeoutException`, which the service answers by storing nothing and recording one `WARN` naming the identifier and the bound. | (a) Leave the lock wait unbounded, which is what was delivered before this decision. (b) Use `LockModeType.PESSIMISTIC_WRITE` with no timeout and rely on the database's own `lock_timeout`. (c) Use an optimistic `@Version` column instead. (d) Retry the lock acquisition in a loop. (e) Bound the storage path by calling `setTimeout` on the injected `TransactionTemplate` bean directly. (f) Change the constructor to take a `PlatformTransactionManager` and build the template outright, as `SettingsService` does. (g) Leave the storage transaction unbounded and rely on the statement hint alone. | An unbounded `select … for update` lets one stuck writer hold a scheduled pass indefinitely, and the pass has no other timeout, so a single contended row could stall ingestion for the process's lifetime. The JPA hint is the portable expression of the bound and is honoured by both supported vendors. (b) moves an application bound into per-deployment database configuration. (c) adds a column, which data-model parity forbids. (d) turns contention into repeated work with no ceiling. (e) mutates a bean the context owns and any future consumer shares, so a second consumer would silently inherit this service's bound. (f) is the shape `SettingsService` uses and is equally correct, but it changes a public constructor signature that the test tree and the context already bind, for no behavioural gain over the copy. (g) leaves the transaction that wraps the statement unbounded, so work the statement does not cover — the existence check and the insert — has no ceiling; bounding both levels at the same five seconds keeps one number to reason about. | A pass that loses the race stores nothing for that row and does not retry within the same pass, so the row is picked up by a later pass — bounded by the pass interval. The bound is a constant rather than a property, so changing it needs a build. A vendor that ignores the hint would fall back to its own lock timeout. The copy is taken once at construction, so a deployment that reconfigures the shared template after the context is built does not affect this service. A deployment that supplies a template with no transaction manager fails fast at construction rather than at the first storage attempt, which is the intended trade for an early, legible failure. |
| DL-247 | Three time-window corrections. `TweetRepository`'s trends query is bounded at both ends, `created_at` between `now - window` and `now`; `scanner.analytics.trend-window-days` is validated as a finite positive number of days; and `task/TweetStreamListener` refuses a provider `created_at` that lies in the future rather than storing it. | (a) Leave the trends query open at the upper end, which is what was delivered. (b) Clamp a future timestamp to the current instant instead of refusing the record. (c) Substitute the arrival instant for a future provider timestamp. (d) Accept any window value and let a non-positive one produce an empty series. | An open upper bound let a single row carrying a future timestamp add a bucket outside the window the endpoint documents, so the series a client received depended on data quality rather than on the configured window. Refusing the record is the same treatment DL-080 and DL-223 already give every other wire member the provider does not properly carry, so the stored table stays honest. (b) and (c) both report a timestamp the provider did not send. (d) leaves a misconfiguration presenting as missing data. | A post whose `created_at` is genuinely a few seconds ahead of this process's clock is refused, so clock skew between the provider and this service costs records; the refusal is recorded at `WARN` with the record's correlation token. The window is validated at binding, so a deployment that set an invalid value now fails to start where it previously started and served an empty series. |
| DL-248 | The scheduled response-generation pass drains the unanswered backlog in bounded keyset batches, up to the per-pass candidate ceiling of DL-282. `TweetRepository.findUnansweredBatchAfter` selects at most one batch of rows whose identifier is above the last one seen and which carry no response, and the pass clears the persistence context between batches. The batch size bounds what is resident; the ceiling bounds what one pass attempts; the provider budget of DL-283 bounds what any pass may spend. | (a) Materialise every unanswered row in one list, which is what was delivered. (b) Read one bounded batch per pass and let later passes drain the rest. (c) Page with an offset instead of a keyset. (d) Stream the rows with a JPA `Stream` and no batching. | (a) held every unanswered wide `tweets` row in memory across a series of serial external calls, so both the memory and the duration of a pass grew with the backlog and a large backlog produced one unbounded burst of provider requests. Batching within one pass bounds what is resident, and the separate candidate ceiling of DL-282 bounds the burst, because batching alone bounded memory and not spend. A keyset rather than (c) because the predicate's own result set shrinks as rows are answered, so an offset would skip rows. (b) would tie the drain rate to the pass interval through the batch size, which is an internal memory bound; DL-282 ties it to an explicit operational ceiling instead, so the two bounds stay separately choosable. (d) keeps a cursor and a transaction open across every provider call. | A row inserted during a pass and carrying an identifier below the cursor is not seen until the next pass, which is the same guarantee a single snapshot gave. Clearing the persistence context between batches detaches everything read earlier, so nothing may be held across a batch boundary; the pass holds only identifiers and counters. The batch size is a constant rather than a property, and it is not the pass bound — a reader looking for the number of candidates one pass attempts finds it in DL-282. |
| DL-249 | The wire `page` and `per_page` contract is unchanged — any positive `per_page` including `Integer.MAX_VALUE` is answered — while the rows behind it are fetched, mapped and serialised in bounded internal chunks. `util/QueryParameters.mapInChunks` and `totalPages` are the single implementation, the chunk bound is 500 rows, and every page read is ordered `id ASC`. | (a) Read, map and serialise the whole requested page at once, which is what was delivered. (b) Introduce a `per_page` maximum. (c) Answer an oversized `per_page` with 400. (d) Reduce the page silently to a supported size. | A `per_page` maximum is excluded from this migration and the unbounded size is parity-preserved, so the only available lever is how the rows are handled internally. Chunking bounds the resident row count without changing a single wire value: the same rows, the same order and the same envelope counters. (b), (c) and (d) each change the contract the eleven routes fix. | The client-visible cost of a very large `per_page` is unchanged — the response still carries every row — so this bounds memory rather than work, and a request for a million rows still produces a million-row body. An explicit `id ASC` sort is now part of every page read, so a vendor's default row order can no longer surface; the ordering is asserted in `repository/JpaMappingIntegrationTest`. |
| DL-250 | Which background paths a process runs is bound explicitly under `scanner.background`: `enabled` governs both paths together, `stream-enabled` binds `TWITTER_STREAM_ENABLED` and `response-generation-enabled` binds `RESPONSE_GENERATION_ENABLED`. A path runs only when `enabled` and that path's own switch both hold **and** the process holds the ownership lease of DL-281. The switches are therefore necessary and not sufficient: they state which paths a process is willing to run, and the lease decides which process runs them. `task/TweetStreamClient.isAutoStartup()` reports the conjunction of both, and registers no task, opens no connection and reaches X in no way when either is unmet; `config/AsyncSchedulingConfig` registers the generation trigger only when the switches admit it and each pass runs only while `isOwner()` holds, recording one skip per uninterrupted run of skipped passes. A process with both switches off registers no task at all, and `task/BackgroundOwnership.isAutoStartup()` reports false for it so it claims no lease. | (a) The switches alone, which is what was delivered and what this entry recorded before the lease existed. (b) The lease alone, with no switches. (c) Default the switches off so a deployment must opt in. (d) Elect an owner with a distributed lock — a `ShedLock` table or a Redis lease. (e) Use Spring Cloud profiles to separate a worker deployment from an API deployment. (f) Derive ownership from the replica index the platform injects. | (a) is unenforceable: a switch is a statement of intent, and two replicas started from the same revision carry the same intent, so every replica opened the filtered stream and ran the generation pass. That is what the lease closes, and the switches are kept because they are the only way to express a replica that must never run a path regardless of the lease — a process that should serve HTTP only. (b) would remove that expression and make a single deployment unable to separate the roles at all. (c) would mean a single-process deployment started from the defaults ingests nothing, which is a worse first-run behaviour than the source had, and the lease already makes defaulting them on safe. (d) adds infrastructure and a table, both excluded by the migration boundary at the head of this file — the reasoning for the mechanism actually chosen is in DL-281. (e) adds a profile dimension to a single-artifact deployment. (f) depends on a platform-specific variable Cloud Run does not provide. | The two mechanisms can disagree in one direction only, and it is the safe one: a process holding the lease but carrying a switch that is off runs that path in no process at all until a process willing to run it claims the lease. A deployment that turned `stream-enabled` off on every replica would therefore ingest nothing while still holding a lease, and nothing reports that as an error, because it is indistinguishable from an operator disabling ingestion deliberately. The duplicate-row guard of DL-195 and DL-252 remains in place beneath both mechanisms. |
| DL-251 | The scheduling registration is hardened in five ways. The first pass runs at the startup instant rather than one interval later. The trigger's reads of the clock and of the settings row are guarded, so a failure resolving the next instant leaves the configured interval in force and can never leave the task unscheduled. The resolved delay is bounded between one second and one year. The scheduler declares a pool size, waits for a running pass on shutdown and awaits termination for thirty seconds. And each of the three standing conditions the resolution can meet — an unreadable `settings` row, a stored value that is not a positive integer, and a resolved value replaced by the upper bound — is recorded at `WARN` once per process rather than once per pass, each behind its own guard, so the pacing is still reported and a standing misconfiguration cannot flood the log. `config/AsyncSchedulingConfigTest` paces four passes against each condition and asserts exactly one record. | (a) Leave the first pass one interval out, and leave the trigger unguarded, which is what was delivered. (b) Run the first pass immediately with a separate `ApplicationRunner`. (c) Let a trigger failure propagate and stop the task. (d) Clamp only the lower bound of the delay. (e) Record every occurrence of each standing condition, which is what the revision after (a) delivered for two of the three: the guards were declared and never consulted, so an unreadable row and a bounded value each wrote one `WARN` per pass. (f) Record them at `DEBUG` instead of guarding them. (g) Share one guard across all three conditions. | The source loop is work-then-sleep, so its first pass runs at startup; deferring it by one interval is a pacing change the port did not intend. An unguarded trigger is worse than a paused one: a single failure resolving the instant returns no next execution, which stops the task permanently with no record and no recovery short of a restart. Bounding the delay at both ends keeps a mistyped settings row from either spinning the pass or parking it beyond any useful horizon. Each of the three conditions persists until an operator changes something, so under (e) a five-second pace writes seventeen thousand identical records a day and the one record that matters is the first; one-shot reporting keeps that first record at `WARN` where an operator will see it. (b) adds a second entry point to the same work. (c) is the defect. (d) leaves the upper end open. (f) hides a real misconfiguration behind a level nobody runs in production. (g) would let the first condition observed silence the other two, which are independent faults with independent remedies. | An immediate first pass means a process that starts with a large backlog begins external calls at startup, which is the source behaviour and is bounded by the batching of DL-248. The one-year upper bound is arbitrary in the sense that no requirement names it; it is chosen to be far outside any operational interval while still finite. The thirty-second termination await bounds shutdown, and a pass still running at that bound is abandoned. One-shot reporting means a condition that clears and later returns is not reported the second time, and the guards are per process, so a restart is what resets them: an operator who fixes the row sees no confirmation, and a multi-replica deployment reports once per replica. |
| DL-252 | Two reads stand between a background generation request and the paid provider call, each taken once and each in its own short repository-demarcated transaction, so no database connection is held across the model call. First, before the subject row is read at all, `responses` is asked whether the row already carries a reply — the cross-replica check, and the common case. Then, immediately before the provider call, `tweets` is asked whether the row is still present, so a row deleted since it was selected costs no generation. The definitive check remains the one the storing transaction takes under the parent row's pessimistic lock. | (a) Check only inside the storing transaction, which is what was delivered first. (b) Hold one transaction across the provider call so the check and the insert share a snapshot. (c) Take a database advisory lock on the identifier before the provider call. (d) Rely on the in-process claim alone. (e) Ask `responses` twice — once before the subject read and again immediately before the provider call — which is what the revision after (a) delivered. (f) Keep only the late pair and drop the early reply check, so both questions are asked at the last possible moment. | A row another replica had already answered was paid for and generated before the in-transaction guard discarded the result, so the wasted spend was exactly the cost of the model call. Reading first turns the common case into one indexed read. (e) is the revision this entry withdraws: the second reply read was the same query against the same identifier a few microseconds later, and the window it closed is one the in-transaction guard already covers, so it bought nothing and charged every generation an extra read. (f) narrows that window furthest and was rejected because it moves the cheapest and most likely check behind a subject read, and it makes a row that is both gone and answered raise the generation failure of DL-086 instead of quietly storing nothing. (b) would hold a pooled connection for the whole provider latency, which with a bounded pool is how a pool is exhausted. (c) adds a lock mechanism for a check an indexed read answers. (d) does not see another replica at all. | Neither pre-call read is atomic with the insert, so two replicas can still both pass them and one will be discarded by the in-transaction guard — the reads narrow a window rather than closing it, and the in-transaction guard remains the correctness boundary. A reply written between the early read and the provider call is not seen until the storing transaction, so that generation is paid for and discarded; that is the window (f) would have narrowed. Every generation costs two indexed reads beyond the storing transaction. |
| DL-253 | A Notion mirror write is retried within a bounded budget: `scanner.notion.mirror-max-retries` attempts after the first, spaced by `scanner.notion.mirror-retry-backoff-millis` doubling before each later attempt and capped, and only for a retryable answer — HTTP 429, any 5xx and a transport failure. An answer that is not retryable is not retried. A reply whose mirror was rejected after its budget is counted separately in the scheduler's end-of-pass summary. | (a) Never retry, which is what was delivered. (b) Retry every failure including 4xx. (c) Persist unmirrored replies to a durable queue and reconcile them later. (d) Fail the primary write when the mirror fails. | A mirror lost to one 429 or one connection reset stayed lost with no further attempt, and the mirror is the only copy of the reply outside the relational store. Retrying the answers that a later attempt could answer differently recovers the common transient case at a bounded cost. (b) would retry a rejection that will never succeed. (c) introduces queue infrastructure this migration excludes. (d) inverts the primary/secondary relationship the source establishes. | There is no durable reconciliation: a reply whose mirror fails through the whole budget stays unmirrored until something writes it again, and nothing does. The counter in the pass summary is the only signal. The backoff is computed by a guarded doubling loop rather than a shift, so a very large configured base cannot overflow to a negative wait; the cap bounds the total added latency of a pass. |
| DL-254 | Three bounds on the stream rule set. `AiToolRepository.findNames(Pageable)` projects the `name` column alone and is called with a bound, so composing the term set loads no `ai_tools` entity. The composed term set is capped at `scanner.ingestion.max-stream-rules`, default 25. A `stream_keywords` override is read as at most 512 segments, and rule deletions and additions are sent in batches of at most 25 rules per request. | (a) Read every `ai_tools` entity and every override segment with no cap, which is what was delivered. (b) Cap the terms but keep reading entities. (c) Send every mutation in one request and let the provider reject an oversized one. (d) Derive the cap from the provider's own response rather than from configuration. | The provider caps the rules an account may register, so a term set larger than the cap cannot be registered at all and composing it is wasted work; the cap belongs in configuration because it is a property of the account tier rather than of this code. A name projection reads one column where the entity read three, and the rule set only ever uses the name. Batching the mutations keeps each request inside the provider's per-request limit. (a) let one large table or one long override produce an unusable rule set. (c) makes the failure mode a provider rejection mid-reconciliation. | A term beyond the cap is silently not registered; the log records the counts, so an operator sees that the set was truncated but not which term was dropped. The cap defaults to the Basic-tier figure, so a higher tier must raise it to use the rules it has paid for. The 512-segment override bound is a constant rather than a property. |

Refusing an out-of-range value rather than accepting (b) means a deployment learns at startup that its figure was rejected, instead of running with a value the library quietly replaced. The ceiling on `maximum-size` bounds the share of the database's connection budget one replica may claim. The graceful shutdown pairs with the pool because the pool closes with the context, so draining the web layer first is what keeps an in-flight request from losing its connection. (c) chooses figures for every deployment. | DL-272 | `api/AuthController` bounds the **credential verifications in progress** in this process and reports rejections at a bounded rate; the **request rate** on the route remains an ingress obligation stated in the class documentation.
