A submitted credential of accepted length acquires one permit before `AuthenticationManager.authenticate` is called, waiting at most 250 milliseconds for it; a request that acquires none is answered with this route's 401 and an empty body without any verification being started for it, and is reported under the same bounded policy with a record naming only the permit count. The permit is released in a `finally`, so an issued token, a rejected credential and a propagated provider failure each return it. A credential above the 256-character ceiling is refused before a permit is sought, so an over-length flood waits for nothing. An interrupt while waiting restores the thread's interrupt status and is answered as an unacquired permit. Rejections are reported at `WARN` at most once per minute per reason, carrying the number suppressed, with **three** reasons — over-length, unverified for want of a permit, and not authenticated — counted independently and a suppressed rejection written at `DEBUG`. That interval-and-count reporting is the whole reporting mechanism: the class carries no second sampler and no window state beyond the counter and report instant each reason holds. The deployment obligation that remains is a per-client request quota on the route and a total service request-rate ceiling. | (a) Leave the verification work unbounded and treat the whole exposure as an ingress concern, which is what was delivered before this entry. (b) Add an application rate limiter — a bucket per client address or per principal. (c) Add a lockout after N failures. (d) Lower the bcrypt cost factor so each attempt is cheaper. (e) Size the permit count as a fixed constant independent of the processor count. (f) Make the permit count and the wait configurable properties. (g) Reject immediately with `tryAcquire()` and no wait. (h) Queue without bound using `acquire()`. (i) Answer an exhausted permit set with 429 or 503 instead of 401. (j) Bound the work with a fixed-size executor the verification is submitted to. (k) Report every rejection at `WARN`. (l) Report nothing. (m) Keep the epoch-second sampling window an intermediate revision also wrote — a second set of counters and a second `recordRejection` method beside this one. | Verification is the expensive half of this route, and it was measured: `BCryptPasswordEncoder.matches` against the delivered `$2a$10$` hash costs a mean of **72.2 ms** of CPU per call over ten calls on this four-processor host. Unbounded, the concurrent verifications a public unauthenticated route admits are limited only by the servlet container's thread pool — 200 threads by default — so roughly three dozen concurrent requests saturate every core and starve the eleven authenticated routes that share it. A permit count equal to the processor count caps that at the CPU the host actually has, which is a bound on the work rather than on the request rate, and it needs no new dependency, no shared store and no per-client state. (a) is the defect. (b) and (c) are excluded by scope: rate limiting is a new capability the migration boundary at the head of this file excludes, and a lockout would add state and a policy the source never had; note that neither is a substitute here, since a limiter keyed on client address does not bound work from many addresses. (d) weakens the hash to make abuse cheaper to absorb, which trades the wrong thing. (e) either under-uses a large host or over-subscribes a small one; the floor of two keeps a single-processor container serving. (f) is a real option and is declined because a permit count is a property of the host rather than of the deployment, and the configuration surface this migration adds is already the eight keys the retired tree referenced without declaring. (g) turns a momentary overlap of two legitimate operators into a 401 for one of them; a 250 ms wait is long enough to absorb one in-flight verification measured at 72 ms and short enough that the worst-case wait a client sees stays a quarter of a second. (h) reintroduces the exposure as unbounded queued latency and held threads. (i) changes this route's status-code contract, which this migration preserves; 401 with an empty body is the only rejection `POST /auth/token` serves, and an attacker learns nothing from it. (j) moves the work off the request thread and needs a rejection policy, a queue bound and a second thread pool to reach the same cap a semaphore reaches with one field. The request-rate half of the exposure stays an ingress concern and is documented as one, naming the specific controls. On reporting: (k) turned an attempt flood into one record per attempt — a bounded windowed count keeps the signal at a fixed cost — (l) hides an attack in progress, and (m) was written and never wired: no call site reached it, so it was dead state describing a different suppression policy from the one in force, and it has been removed. | The bound is per process, so a deployment of N replicas admits N × permits concurrent verifications, and nothing in the application bounds the total **attempt rate** — a client that submits credentials one at a time still costs one verification each, so the documented per-client ingress quota is still required. A legitimate operator whose request arrives while every permit is held receives the same 401 with an empty body as a wrong credential and has to retry; the log distinguishes the two reasons, the response does not. Sustained saturation is therefore visible as a denial of service for correct credentials, which is the intended trade against starving the authenticated routes. The permit count and the 250 ms wait are constants, so changing either needs a build, and the count is read from the processor count at class initialisation, so a container whose CPU allocation changes at runtime keeps the count it started with. A rejection inside the reporting window is visible only at `DEBUG`, the counters are per process so a multi-replica deployment reports once per replica per window, no client address is recorded so the log does not distinguish one attacker from many clients, and the reporting interval is likewise a constant. |
| DL-273 | Every executor a test creates is shut down, awaited and asserted terminated, and an interrupt while awaiting is restored on the calling thread and reported as a test failure. `service/SettingsServiceTest` and `repository/JpaMappingIntegrationTest` each declare the same private helper. | (a) Shut down without awaiting, which one of the two did. (b) Await and discard the result, which the other did. (c) Extract the helper into a shared test utility class. (d) Use a JUnit extension that fails a test leaving a live thread. | A pool that is not awaited can outlive the test that made it, and with the bounded pool of DL-271 a surviving thread may hold one of four JDBC connections for the remainder of the build — a cross-test failure whose cause is invisible where it appears. Discarding the await result is the same defect with the diagnosis already computed and thrown away. Restoring the interrupt keeps an interrupted build interruptible. (c) would add a production-tree utility used only by tests, or a test-only class shared across packages for six lines. (d) is a larger mechanism than two call sites need. | The helper is duplicated in two classes, so a change must be made in both; each is six lines and each is documented as net-new against this entry. The bound is five seconds in one class and the concurrency timeout in the other, so a genuinely slow machine could fail a test that previously passed by leaking. |
| DL-274 | `ScannerApplicationTests` runs in a `MOCK` web environment and issues its requests through `MockMvc`; the real filter chain, dispatcher and controllers all run, and no connector, port or server thread is started. `config/HttpChainIntegrationTest` and `security/RequestBodyLimitIntegrationTest` are the two classes in the suite that start a container. | (a) `RANDOM_PORT` for the composition class, which is what was delivered. (b) `MOCK` for the composition class plus a new narrowly scoped connector class carrying the two `/error` cases. (c) Delete the route cases from the composition class and rely on the `@WebMvcTest` slices. (d) `MOCK` everywhere and accept that the container-level path is uncovered. | Converting the assertions and running them established that every one is reachable without a connector, including both direct requests to `/error`, since a direct request to that path is an ordinary request to `BasicErrorController` rather than a container ERROR dispatch. (a) therefore paid for a server for no assertion and made the widest class in the suite sensitive to connector timing. (b) was prepared and then not needed; it would also have cached a further application context, which raises build cost rather than lowering it. (c) removes the only end-to-end coverage of the eleven routes. (d) would leave the `sendError` path of DL-118 uncovered, which is why the request-body class keeps its container. | Behaviour visible only through a real connector is covered by exactly one class, so that class and the composition class must be kept in step if the container-level path grows. A `MOCK` environment does not exercise connector-level request framing, header parsing or the container's own error reporting; those are covered by `config/ContainerErrorResponseConfigTest` and the request-body class. |
| DL-275 | The answer to a rules mutation is read rather than discarded: `task/TweetStreamClient.reportRefusedRules` records one `WARN` when the answer carries an `errors` entry or a non-zero `meta.summary.not_created` or `meta.summary.invalid`, naming the refused count and the two summary counts. A refusal does not fail the mutation — the stream connects with the rules the endpoint did accept. The reflected `errors[].value` expression reaches the record only as `util/LogSafe.correlation`, a fixed-shape token, and at most one request's worth of tokens is listed while the count is reported in full. | (a) Discard the answer and read only the HTTP status, which is what was delivered before the answer was read at all. (b) Log the reflected `errors[].value` expression as the provider returned it, which is what the revision after (a) delivered. (c) Log the expression through `util/LogSafe.logSafe`, normalised and cut to 64 characters. (d) Fail the mutation and refuse to connect when any rule is refused. (e) Match the reflected expression back to the request body and log this application's own index for it. (f) List every fingerprint with no bound. | A partial refusal is silent at the transport layer — the endpoint answers 200 and reports the refusals in the body — so under (a) a rule set could be registered short by half with nothing in the log to say so, and ingestion would then match fewer posts than configured for no visible reason. (b) is the exposure this entry closes: the value is provider-reflected text on a path DL-197 governs, and a value carrying a carriage return and a line feed forges a record boundary. (c) keeps the text readable and is the guard used where the text is the diagnostic, but here the expression is one this application composed and can therefore reproduce, so a non-reversible token gives the same correlation without carrying provider text at all: an operator matches it against `LogSafe.correlation(StreamRuleTerms.expressionOf(term))` for each configured term. (d) turns a partial refusal — most commonly one duplicate rule — into no ingestion at all. (e) is the most legible option and is rejected on cost: it would thread the request body through the reporting path to name an index the operator would still have to map back to a term. (f) leaves the record length at the provider's discretion. | A fingerprint is not reversible, so an operator who did not keep the term list cannot recover which expression was refused; the remedy is to fingerprint the configured terms, which is deterministic. The token is a truncated HMAC keyed with a per-process secret (DL-119), so it is not confirmable against a guess from outside the process and the same expression carries a different token in the next process. A refusal beyond one request's worth is counted but not fingerprinted. A rule the endpoint silently declines without an `errors` entry and without incrementing either summary member is not reported at all. |
| DL-276 | **Withdrawn as a file; restated as a policy.** This entry declared a tracked `.yamllint.yml` at the repository root holding the YAML lint policy both workflows were written to, and named `.github/dependabot.yml` alongside it. Both files are deleted and neither exists in the delivered tree, and `OperationsContractTest` asserts the absence of both. **The dependency-update monitoring that stands in their place is committed as content rather than as configuration**, and it is what a reader should look at when asking how a stale dependency is found here. Three mechanisms carry it. `BuildDependencyContractTest` enumerates the whole dependency surface — the parent version, the `java.version` property, every explicitly pinned coordinate with a version floor, the coordinates that must stay BOM-managed, and the resolved runtime versions — so a coordinate cannot move, and cannot fail to move, without the test either failing or having been edited in the same commit. `OperationsContractTest` does the same for the operations surface: the action reference the migration authored must be a 40-hex commit SHA carrying its resolved release, both container base images must be digest-pinned, and the Node.js toolchain must meet the mandatory floor. And each moving part has a row here stating the condition under which it is refreshed: DL-003 for the Boot line, DL-103 for the pinned action, DL-106 for the two container digests, DL-285 for the Node floor. | (a) Keep both files, on repository-hygiene grounds. (b) Keep `.yamllint.yml` and drop `.github/dependabot.yml`, since only the first affects committed content. (c) Move the lint policy into the CI job as command-line flags. (d) Restore `.github/dependabot.yml` alone and make the contract test require it, which is what a later review asked for. (e) Add a scheduled workflow that opens an update proposal, in place of the vendor configuration. | Neither file appears in the frozen file inventory this migration is authorised to create, and the matrix's own §2.9 prose admitted as much while §2.9's table claimed the operations delivery was four files — a self-contradiction a review recorded as part of a critical scope breach. Deleting both removes the contradiction at its source. (a) and (b) keep an out-of-inventory file; (c) edits the workflow steps the plan freezes. (d) is the same out-of-inventory file under a narrower name: the frozen plan declares its in-scope file list exhaustive, and `.github/dependabot.yml` is not in it, so the review's request is answered by the exclusion — and answered *substantively* by the three mechanisms above, which are committed, in-scope, and fail the build rather than opening a pull request nobody has to merge. (e) is a fifth operations file by another name. | No automated dependency-update proposal is opened for the Maven coordinates, the action references or the container base images, so every refresh is initiated by a person. That is the real gap and the policy above does not close it: a test that fails when a version *changes* does not fire when a version *should have changed*, so a coordinate can sit stale indefinitely and the build stays green — which is precisely how the archived Slack action and the `node12` `setup-gcloud` reference DL-103 records came to be years old. Time-to-fix for DL-003, DL-103, DL-106 and DL-285 is therefore bounded by review cadence and nothing else. Repository YAML is held to no committed policy; the two workflows retain the whitespace form the withdrawn policy normalised them to, which is yamllint's own `default` preset, so re-adding the file under a widened scope would need no edit to either workflow. |
| DL-277 | The transport-security response headers are declared in exactly one place: `security/SecurityConfig.defaultTransportSecurityHeaderWriter()`, a `CompositeHeaderWriter` composing Spring Security's five default writers in the framework's own order — `X-Content-Type-Options`, `X-XSS-Protection`, the `Cache-Control`/`Pragma`/`Expires` directives, `Strict-Transport-Security` on a secure request only, and `X-Frame-Options` — with `Strict-Transport-Security` carrying `includeSubDomains` and no `preload`. The filter chain applies it through `HeaderWriterFilter` and the `transportSecurityHeaderWriter()` bean publishes the same composition for the one response the chain never sees, the container rejection of DL-237. `server.forward-headers-strategy` is `${FORWARD_HEADERS_STRATEGY:framework}` so that `HttpServletRequest.isSecure()` reflects the `X-Forwarded-Proto` scheme a Cloud Run request arrives with, which is the value the HSTS writer reads. | (a) Customise the writer set — add a Content-Security-Policy, a Referrer-Policy or a Permissions-Policy. (b) Let the chain configure the headers and have `config/ContainerErrorResponseConfig` compose its own copy. (c) Leave `forward-headers-strategy` unset at Spring Boot's `none`. (d) Set the strategy to `native` and let the container parse the forwarded headers. (e) Disable HSTS. | The header set is the framework default and the migration adds no security capability, so (a) is scope the prompt excludes — a new header is a new control, not a translation. (b) is the duplication this row closes: two compositions of one policy drift the moment a writer is added, and the valve and the chain then answer the same client differently. (c) makes every request behind the Cloud Run proxy read as insecure, so the HSTS writer emits nothing and the header is silently absent in exactly the deployment that needs it. (d) delegates to the container's own parsing, which is a second policy to reason about for no gain when the framework strategy already covers the forwarded scheme, host and port. (e) removes a default control. | The five writers are the framework's, so a Spring Security upgrade that changes the default set changes this policy without a code change here; `config/HttpChainIntegrationTest` asserts the headers a response carries, so the change would be visible as a test failure rather than silently. Trusting `X-Forwarded-*` is correct only behind a proxy that sets them: a deployment that exposed this service directly would let a client assert `X-Forwarded-Proto: https` and receive an HSTS header on a plaintext connection, which is why the strategy is a placeholder an operator can set to `none`. This row was allocated after a citation audit found the policy cited through DL-194 (the Notion mirror paths), DL-241 (the Connector/J raise) and DL-243 (the container JVM) from four different sites, with no row owning it. |
| DL-278 | The application resolves the current instant from one bean, `config/ClockConfig.utcClock()` returning `Clock.systemUTC()`, and it has two consumers: `service/AnalyticsService` for the trends window, which this row owns, and `task/BackgroundOwnership` for the lease instants, which DL-281 owns. The `GET /analytics/trends` window is therefore a whole number of **UTC calendar dates**, not a rolling duration: its cutoff is the start of the UTC day `windowDays - 1` days before the current UTC day, so a window of `n` days observes the current UTC date and the `n - 1` dates before it and the series holds at most `n` elements, never `n + 1`. The window also closes at the instant of the call, taken from the same clock. Scheduling reads its own clock from the `TriggerContext` the framework supplies and does not resolve this bean (DL-228). | (a) Read `LocalDate.now()` or `Instant.now()` directly wherever the window is computed. (b) Publish `Clock.systemDefaultZone()`. (c) Make the zone a configuration property. (d) Express the window as a rolling `Duration` of `n × 24h` back from the call instant. (e) Let the scheduling trigger resolve this bean too. | The stored basis decides the query basis: `task/TweetStreamListener.readCreatedAt` and `service/NotionService.parseOffsetDateTime` both normalise a delivered timestamp to UTC before it reaches `tweets.created_at` (DL-192), so a window bound against that column has to be built in UTC or the first and last buckets of every series shift with the deployment's zone. (a) leaves the basis implicit and untestable — a fixed clock cannot be substituted, so no test can pin the boundary. (b) reintroduces exactly the zone dependence UTC storage removes. (c) invents a knob for a basis the stored column already fixes. (d) changes the endpoint's meaning: a day bucket is a calendar date, and a rolling duration would split one date across two windows depending on the time of the call. (e) would give the scheduler two time sources, since a `Trigger` is handed the framework's own. | Every boundary of the series is a UTC date, so an operator in a non-UTC zone reading the endpoint at local midnight sees a window that is not aligned to their own calendar; the endpoint documents the basis and `service/AnalyticsServiceTest` pins it with a fixed clock, including the `n` versus `n + 1` element boundary. A third consumer of the current instant has to take this bean and not call `Instant.now()`, and nothing mechanically enforces that. This row was allocated after a citation audit found the clock and the window basis cited through DL-241 (the Connector/J raise) from twenty sites, with no row owning them. |
| DL-279 | `config/BuildProfileGuard` is a `BeanFactoryPostProcessor` that refuses to start the application when the profile named `test` is active and the test framework is absent from the bean class loader. The two values it reads — `spring.profiles.active` and the presence of `org.junit.jupiter.api.Test` — arrive through `EnvironmentAware` and `BeanClassLoaderAware`, and the class publishes no bean, reads no bean definition and alters none. `config/BuildProfileGuardTest` covers its decision matrix. | (a) No guard at all, leaving the build-scoped profile activatable in a deployed revision. (b) A `@Profile("!test")` bean that fails on construction. (c) An `ApplicationRunner` or `ApplicationReadyEvent` listener that fails after the context has started. (d) Read the signal from an environment variable a deployment must set, rather than from the classpath. (e) Delete `src/test/resources/application-test.yml` and configure the suite from system properties instead. | `src/test/resources/application-test.yml` is build-scoped: it pins an in-memory H2 database with `create-drop`, supplies empty X credentials and carries a signing key and a bcrypt hash held in version control (DL-205). A revision started with `SPRING_PROFILES_ACTIVE=test` would mint tokens under a published key and serve against a schema dropped at shutdown, so the profile needs a boundary that a deployment cannot cross by configuration alone. A `BeanFactoryPostProcessor` runs before the container instantiates any singleton, which is what makes the failure name the profile instead of whichever bean is built first; (b) and (c) both fail later and name a bean or a runner. The classpath signal needs no deployment action: a Spring Boot executable jar carries no test-scoped dependency, so the class is absent from a packaged artifact and present in every Surefire-launched context — (d) would place the safety of the check in the hands of the configuration it exists to police. (e) would move fifteen bound values out of a file the suite and a reader can both read. This row was allocated after a citation audit found the guard, its test and four `docs/TRACEABILITY_MATRIX.md` rows citing DL-244, which owns `dto/UpdateResponseRequest`'s explicit-null semantics and not this subject (DL-058). | The signal is the presence of a class, so a deployment that puts a test-scoped jar on the runtime classpath would let the profile through, and a future build tool that packaged test dependencies would disable the guard silently. The check covers the one profile name `test`; a second build-scoped profile would need adding here. A deployment that legitimately wanted an in-memory database would have to declare its own profile rather than reuse this one. |
| DL-280 | The delay `task/TweetStreamClient` waits after an HTTP 429 is finite for every `x-rate-limit-reset` header value a provider or an intermediary can send. `rateLimitDelay` parses the header as UTC epoch seconds inside a `try` that catches `NumberFormatException`, `ArithmeticException` and `DateTimeException`, so a value out of the range `Instant` represents leaves the computed jittered backoff in force rather than propagating; and a reset instant further away than the `MAX_RATE_LIMIT_DELAY` reconnect horizon of fifteen minutes is applied as the horizon. The header therefore raises the delay from the backoff floor towards the reset instant and can never raise it above the horizon, so the largest wait any answer produces is fifteen minutes and ingestion always reattempts. A `WARN` records which of the three outcomes applied — absent header, unreadable header, or horizon substituted — and names the header and the horizon in seconds, never the received value. | (a) Trust the header outright and wait however long it reports, which is what was delivered. (b) Catch a wider `RuntimeException` around the parse. (c) Ignore the header entirely and always use the computed backoff. (d) Clamp with the existing five-minute `MAX_RECONNECT_BACKOFF` ceiling instead of a separate horizon. (e) Bind the horizon to a configuration key. (f) Treat an unreadable header as a fatal fault and stop the stream. | `Instant.ofEpochSecond(long)` raises `DateTimeException` for any value outside the instant range, and the delivered catch listed only `ArithmeticException` and `NumberFormatException`, so a header of `31556889864403200` — a value inside `long` and outside `Instant` — escaped the reconnect loop's own handling. (a) also let a header of `99999999999999999` suspend ingestion for longer than the service will run, which is indefinite data loss produced by a value the service does not control. (b) would swallow a defect in the surrounding code along with the parse. (c) discards a signal the provider sends to prevent a further rejection, which is what DL-045 records the floor as being for. (d) would conflate two bounds with different jobs: the backoff ceiling paces reconnection after a fault, while this horizon bounds a wait the provider asked for, and the horizon is deliberately the longer of the two so a genuine reset inside it is still honoured. (e) adds a key to the configuration surface this migration caps, for a bound no operator has a reason to move. (f) contradicts the indefinite-retry decision of DL-045. | The horizon is a judgement, not a documented X requirement: a genuine reset more than fifteen minutes away is reattempted early, which draws a further 429 and a further bounded wait rather than one long one. Requests spent that way are answered by the provider without delivering records. `Duration.between` is computed against `Instant.now()` rather than the one clock bean of DL-278, so the reset comparison is not substitutable in a test; `task/TweetStreamClientTest` covers the horizon through the private method reflectively instead. |
| DL-281 | Both background paths run in the process that holds a time-limited lease, and in no other. `task/BackgroundOwnership` is a `SmartLifecycle` bean that claims the lease at startup, renews it from a fixed-delay task and releases it at shutdown. The lease is one row of the existing `settings` table keyed `background_owner`, whose value carries the claiming process's `UUID` identity and the epoch-millisecond instant the claim lapses, separated by `@`. No table, column, index or constraint is added. A claim is a compare-and-set: the claimant reads the row, treats the lease as available when the value is absent, unreadable, lapsed or already its own, and then writes through `SettingRepository.replaceValueIfUnchanged`, a JPQL `@Modifying` statement whose `WHERE` clause names the value just read; `replaceAbsentValue` covers a row whose value is null and `saveAndFlush` creates the row when none exists. Of several processes reading one value exactly one update matches a row. `task/TweetStreamClient.isAutoStartup()` and every generation pass consult `isOwner()`, which reads process-local state and issues no statement. A renewal that succeeds starts the stream when this process runs it and it is idle, so a lease left behind by a terminated process is taken over without an operator step; a renewal that fails stops the stream and records the loss once. Every failure — a rejected update, an insert race lost to `DataIntegrityViolationException`, or any `DataAccessException` including an unreachable database — leaves `isOwner()` false, so ownership is never inferred from a failure to disprove it. The bean's phase is `Integer.MAX_VALUE - 1024`, below the `SmartLifecycle` default the stream client carries, so the lease is claimed before the stream is asked whether to start and released after it has stopped, and the scheduler pool of `config/AsyncSchedulingConfig` holds two threads so a long pass cannot starve the renewal. `config/ScannerProperties.Background` holds the renewal interval at no more than half the lease term. | (a) The configuration switches alone, which is what was delivered and what DL-250 recorded. (b) A `ShedLock` table or a Redis lease. (c) A vendor advisory lock — `pg_try_advisory_lock` on PostgreSQL, `GET_LOCK` on MySQL. (d) A dedicated max-one-instance worker revision separate from the API revision. (e) Derive ownership from a replica index the platform injects. (f) A new `background_owner` table rather than a row of `settings`. (g) A lease with no expiry, released only at shutdown. (h) Renew on use — extend the lease from inside each pass — rather than on a timer. | Cloud Run scales the single revision horizontally and `.github/workflows/cd.yml` deploys it with no instance bound, so (a) left every replica opening the filtered stream and running the generation pass: duplicate ingestion of every delivered record and duplicate provider spend, growing with replica count, with nothing in any process able to detect it. A switch is a statement of intent that cannot be enforced, so ownership needs a mechanism a second process is subject to. (b) adds infrastructure and a table, both of which the migration boundary at the head of this file excludes. (c) is enforceable and free of new schema, but the function differs per vendor and the URL that selects the vendor is opaque until runtime under DL-027, so a lock written for one vendor would fail closed or silently on another; a JPQL compare-and-set behaves identically on PostgreSQL, MySQL and the H2 the suite runs, and names no quoted column, which the reserved names of DL-061 make a real consideration for native SQL. (d) is a deployment change no scope the frozen plan states admits, and the operations carve-out of DL-215 is line-level. (e) depends on a variable Cloud Run does not provide. (f) is the schema addition that boundary forbids, where the same plan already sanctions adding rows to `settings` — the same latitude the seeding of DL-040 uses. (g) makes a process killed without shutdown the permanent owner, which is the failure mode a lease exists to avoid. (h) would let a lease lapse during a long pass and admit a second owner mid-pass, which is why renewal is paced independently of the work. | The lease bounds duplication rather than eliminating it: two processes can each believe they hold it for at most the clock skew between them, since each decides lapse against its own clock and the stored instant is written by whichever process claimed it. A skew larger than the lease term would admit two owners continuously, which no mechanism here detects. Losing the database stops background work in every process rather than degrading it, which is the intended direction of a fail-closed design but does mean a database outage silently halts ingestion with only a `WARN` cadence as the signal. Takeover is not immediate: a lease lapses after its term and is claimed at the next renewal, so up to the lease term plus one renewal interval can pass with no owner. The row is written by the service and is not a configuration value, so `service/SettingsService` withholds it from the settings route (DL-284); an operator editing the row directly would cause both the current owner and the next claimant to lose their compare-and-set until the lease lapses. |
| DL-282 | One scheduled response-generation pass attempts at most `scanner.background.max-candidates-per-pass` candidates, default 200, bound within 1 and `Integer.MAX_VALUE` by the compact constructor of `config/ScannerProperties.Background`. The keyset drain of DL-248 continues to read batches of at most 100 rows, and the pass now stops at the ceiling as well as at a short batch: it counts attempts across batches, breaks out of the batch and the drain when the count reaches the ceiling, and records one `WARN` naming the ceiling and stating that the remaining backlog is left to the following pass. The ceiling counts attempts rather than successes, so a candidate that fails consumes one. | (a) Drain the whole backlog in one pass, which is what was delivered. (b) Bound the pass by elapsed time instead of by candidate count. (c) Bound it by the batch count rather than the candidate count. (d) Make the ceiling a constant rather than a property. (e) Count successes only, so a failing pass keeps trying. | (a) bounded what was resident in memory but not what one pass would spend: a backlog of any size produced that many serial OpenAI calls and that many Notion mirrors within a single pass, with no bound the deployment could state. A candidate count is the unit the spend is proportional to, which (b) and (c) both only approximate — elapsed time varies with provider latency and a batch count multiplies to a candidate count anyway. (d) would leave a deployment unable to state its own per-pass bound, and unlike the batch size of DL-248, which is an internal memory bound, this one is an operational limit an operator has a reason to choose. (e) would let a systematically failing provider consume unbounded attempts within one pass, and an attempt-counting ceiling is the only thing that bounds that: no failure circuit stands behind it, as DL-283 records. | A backlog larger than the ceiling drains over several passes, so at the default delay of 60 seconds a backlog of 10,000 rows takes about 50 passes; the drain rate is now a function of the ceiling and the pass interval together, which is a behaviour change from the source's drain-everything loop and is recorded as such. The ceiling is applied per pass and not per hour, so shortening the pass interval raises the aggregate rate and no bound in the tree is independent of the interval — DL-283 records the withdrawal of the one that was. |
| DL-283 | **No provider work budget is delivered.** This entry previously recorded `task/ProviderWorkBudget`, a `@Component` that granted or refused one unit of external work through a fixed-window allowance of `scanner.background.provider-calls-per-window` units per `scanner.background.provider-window-seconds` span and a consecutive-failure circuit governed by `scanner.background.provider-failure-threshold` and `scanner.background.provider-circuit-open-seconds`. That component, its four configuration keys in both YAML profiles, the four components they bound on `config/ScannerProperties.Background`, the gate `task/TweetStreamListener` took ahead of each sentiment call, the gate `task/ResponseGenerationScheduler` took per candidate, the outcome-recording calls each made, and `task/ProviderWorkBudgetTest` are all deleted. As delivered, a record that passes the popularity gate reaches the sentiment call directly and a candidate the backlog query returns reaches its provider call directly. The only bound one pass carries is the per-pass candidate ceiling DL-282 owns. No allowance, window, token bucket, attempt counter, failure circuit, lockout or delay is applied to any external call anywhere in the tree. | (a) Keep the component and record the divergence in this row, which is what the previous revision of this entry did. (b) Keep only the consecutive-failure circuit and drop the fixed-window allowance. (c) Replace the fixed window with a token bucket, or the hand-written circuit with Resilience4j or Spring Retry. (d) Keep the allowance and raise its default until no deployment reaches it. (e) Re-express the same bound on the ingestion path as a per-record ceiling, mirroring the shape DL-282 carries. (f) Configure the bound outside the application, in the ingress or in each provider account's own quota. | New rate limiting lies outside the migration boundary this file states at its head, without qualification and alongside caching, autoscaling changes, new indexes and new constraints. A fixed-window call allowance and a consecutive-failure circuit are rate limiting: each refuses work the ported behaviour performs, on a schedule the retired tree never had. (a) is the defect this revision closes — an entry of this log records a decision and cannot authorise a capability the boundary excludes. (b) and (d) keep a limiter and change only its shape or its threshold, so each is the same excluded capability under another name. (c) adds a coordinate the fixed dependency set does not carry as well. (e) reads as a batch bound and is not one: ingestion has no pass and no query to batch, so a per-record ceiling there is a rate. (f) is the correct place for this control and lies outside this repository, which holds `infrastructure/terraform/**` structurally unchanged and confines its operations edits to the backend build and image. The per-pass candidate ceiling of DL-282 survives on a different ground: it bounds how many rows one query hands to one pass, which is the batching decision DL-248 already makes, and it is measured against no clock. | Every external call is unbounded in both volume and failure repetition, which is the retired behaviour: an ingestion burst issues one sentiment call per delivered record, and a provider failing systematically is called once per delivered record and once per candidate for as long as the failure lasts, so a credential fault or a provider outage produces sustained billable traffic with no ceiling the application imposes. Nothing in the tree detects a failing provider or backs off from it: the reconnection backoff of DL-214 and the reset horizon of DL-280 cover the X stream connection alone, and Google, OpenAI and Notion have no equivalent. The controls that remain are the per-pass candidate ceiling of DL-282, the scheduler pool of DL-201, the Notion retry budget of DL-253 and the ownership lease of DL-281, and none of them bounds spend. A deployment that needs a ceiling has to set one at the provider account or in the ingress, which is stated here because the application no longer offers one. |
| DL-284 | The `settings` row that holds the background-ownership lease of DL-281 is not part of the configuration surface. `service/SettingsService` filters the reserved key out of the list `GET /settings` renders, and reports it absent — the 404 literal `Setting not found` — for any `PUT /settings/{key}` naming it, without reading or writing the row. The check is one instance method comparing against `BackgroundOwnership.OWNER_SETTING_KEY`, so the reserved name is declared once, by the component that owns the row. Both routes keep every path, method, parameter name and status code the frozen route contract fixes. | (a) Expose the row like any other setting. (b) Hide it from the list but let a write through. (c) Answer a write with 403 or 409. (d) Add a column or a flag to `settings` marking a row internal. (e) Keep the lease in a row whose key an operator would not guess, and rely on that. | The row's value is a process identity and a lapse instant written by the service several times a minute, so (a) would render an internal coordination value in a route whose typed contract `frontend/src/schema/setting.ts` describes as configuration, and an operator reading the list would see a key that changes on its own. (b) is worse than (a): a write would break the current owner's compare-and-set and the next claimant's alike, stopping background work until the lease lapsed, and it would do so through a route that reported success. (c) invents a status code the eleven-route contract does not carry — the parity obligation admits only the codes the source emitted, and `Setting not found` is already the answer for a key the route does not manage, which this key is. (d) is the schema change the migration boundary at the head of this file forbids. (e) is not a control at all. | A key an operator genuinely wanted to store under the reserved name cannot be, and the route reports it absent rather than reserved, so the answer does not distinguish the two cases; that is the price of not adding a status code. The seeding of DL-040 does not write this row, so a fresh database has no lease row until the first process claims one. `service/SettingsServiceTest` covers both the withheld list entry and the refused write. |
| DL-285 | **The `node-version` input of `.github/workflows/ci.yml` is `'14'`, exactly as the retired workflow declared it, and the raise this entry previously recorded is withdrawn.** An earlier revision set the input to `22.23.2` on the ground of a toolchain floor of 22.12.0 on the 22.x line. That value, and the two comment lines that carried its pointer, are removed; the step is now byte-identical to the source branch — `uses: actions/setup-node@v2` with `node-version: '14'` and no comment. DL-074 is the sole owner of the value. `OperationsContractTest` asserts the input equals the retired one, that exactly one `node-version` is declared, and that the step carries no decision pointer. Every frontend step the input serves — `npm ci`, `npm run lint`, `npm run type-check`, `npm test` and `npm run build` — remains byte-identical, including the pre-existing breakage that no lock file is committed and that the `lint` and `type-check` scripts do not exist in `frontend/package.json`. A full diff of the delivered workflow against the source branch now shows changes in the backend job alone. | (a) Keep `22.23.2` and record the divergence here, which is what the previous revision of this entry did. (b) Keep the raise and also move `actions/setup-node` to a maintained major pinned by commit SHA. (c) Move the version into a `.nvmrc` or a `package.json` `engines` range. (d) Delete the Node setup step, since two of the four frontend commands it serves cannot succeed anyway. (e) Raise the value to `20.20.2`, the lowest release the floor admits. | The frozen plan states twice, once in this workflow's own delivery and once in the operations carve-out DL-215 records, that every frontend step of this workflow is untouched, and it confines the operations edits to the backend build and image. `node-version` is a line of a frontend step, so changing it is an edit with no authorising requirement, which is what (a) leaves standing and what this revision closes; the earlier argument that a version is not a step does not survive contact with the requirement, which freezes the steps rather than a subset of their lines. (b) and (e) are the same edit at other values. (c) adds a file no inventory row names and still leaves the workflow input to resolve. (d) removes a step the plan holds untouched. Nothing in the backend job reads the Node toolchain — the backend is built by `mvn -B clean verify` on JDK 21 — so the value has no bearing on what this migration delivers, and the floor that governs the toolchain this migration is built with governs no line of a job the plan declares untouchable. | The workflow provisions Node 14, which reached end of life in April 2023 and receives no security fix, in a job whose four frontend commands could not succeed regardless: `npm ci` has no committed lock file and two of the scripts do not exist. A reader may reasonably take the `14` pin for an oversight, and DL-074 is the record that it is not. Raising it is a one-line change to a file this migration holds frozen, so it belongs to whoever next has authority over `frontend/**` rather than to this migration. |
| DL-286 | `dto/CreateResponseRequest` binds `tweet_id` as a raw Jackson `JsonNode` and reproduces the truth value Python assigns to a decoded JSON value. `namesTweet()` reports false for an absent member, a JSON `null`, an empty string, a zero of any numeric spelling including `0`, `0.0` and `-0.0`, `false`, an empty array and an empty object, and true for every other value; a non-finite number reads true, as it does in Python. `usableTweetId()` returns the identifier text for an accepted value — a string yields its own characters, a number or a boolean its JSON spelling, and an array or object its compact JSON document — and `null` for a rejected one. `api/ResponseController` answers a rejected value with HTTP 400 and `{"error": "Tweet ID is required"}`, and passes an accepted one to `service/ResponseService`. | (a) Bind `tweet_id` as a `String` and treat a blank value as absent, which is what a typed DTO would do. (b) Bind it as a `Long` so binding rejects a non-numeric value. (c) Keep `JsonNode` but accept every carried value, so only an absent member or a JSON `null` is rejected. (d) Answer a value that names no `tweets` row with the guard's own 400 literal. (e) Declare `@NotBlank` beside `@NotNull`. | The source guard is `if not tweet_id` at `backend/app/api/responses.py:L40`, applied to a value read out of a free-form body, so its behaviour is Python's truth test over whatever the body decoded to — not a null check and not a numeric parse. Each of the eight false values is a body a client can send and the source answered 400 for, and every other value is one the source passed on. (a) loses seven of the eight: `0`, `false`, `[]` and `{}` would all reach generation. (b) changes the status code twice over — a non-numeric value becomes a binding failure and a numeric one that names no row still reaches generation. (c) is the widest deviation and sends `0` to the provider. (d) moves a documented 500 onto a 400: the source answers `Failed to generate response` for an identifier naming no row, which DL-232 owns. (e) constrains a `JsonNode` component, which Bean Validation does not evaluate as a text check, and adds a constraint the required-versus-optional parity of DL-050 excludes. | The guard is a transcription of another language's truth table and is correct only as long as that table is: a reader expecting a Java null check will be surprised that `{"tweet_id": 0}` answers 400 while `{"tweet_id": {"a": 1}}` does not. Binding a raw `JsonNode` puts the request's own JSON shape inside the DTO, so a client can send an array or an object and see it stringified into an identifier that names no row; the answer is then the source's 500, which reads as a server fault for a client mistake. `api/ResponseControllerTest` pins all eight false values and the accepted spellings, and that suite is the only thing standing between a future simplification to `String` and four silently changed status codes. |
| DL-287 | `util/ConfiguredValues` holds the single definition of what an unset configuration value is: `isUnresolvedPlaceholder(String)` matches a trimmed value against `^\$\{.*}$` with `DOTALL`, and `isUnset(String)` treats `null`, blank and an unresolved placeholder alike. It is a final class with a private constructor and two static methods, and it replaced the same shape written three times — in `security/SecurityConfig`, `security/JwtService` and `config/DatabaseUrlTranslator`. The behaviour each call site keeps is its own: DL-186 for the JWT secret, DL-189 for the credential pair, and the fail-fast of DL-072 for `DATABASE_URL`. | (a) Leave the pattern and the guard duplicated at the three call sites, which is what was delivered first. (b) Put the two methods on `config/ScannerProperties` so the check travels with the bound values. (c) Make it a Spring bean and inject it. (d) Solve it at binding time with a custom `Validator` or a `@ConfigurationPropertiesBinding` converter that turns an unresolved placeholder into `null`. (e) Put the methods on `util/LogSafe`, the utility class that already existed. | Spring leaves `${VAR}` in place as literal text when the variable behind it is absent, so a value that looks configured is not, and all three fail-fast guards have to recognise that exact shape. Three copies of one regular expression are three chances to diverge, and the divergence is silent: a startup that should fail would succeed with a placeholder as a signing key. (a) is that risk taken three times. (b) makes a properties record depend on a matcher and gives the binding type a second responsibility. (c) buys nothing a static method does not, since the check holds no state and reads no context. (d) is the most thorough option and is refused on blast radius: it would apply to every bound property, including those whose absent value is legitimately blank, and it would move a named startup failure into the binder. (e) would put configuration inspection in the log-metadata utility, against the one-subject discipline this log applies to its own entries. | The `util/` package holds five classes where the frozen inventory names one: `util/DelimitedStringListConverter` is the inventoried class, DL-208 records the deviation for `util/LogSafe`, DL-217 and DL-225 carry `util/QueryParameters`, DL-257 carries `util/StreamRuleTerms`, and this row is the fifth. The pattern recognises Spring's default placeholder syntax only, so a deployment that reconfigures the prefix or suffix leaves every guard blind and nothing detects it. `isUnset` collapses three conditions into one answer, so a caller that has to tell a blank value from an unresolved one calls the narrower method; `security/JwtService` does exactly that under DL-186. |
| DL-288 | `service/SentimentAnalysisService` creates its `LanguageServiceClient` lazily on first use, under double-checked locking over a `volatile` field, from Application Default Credentials, and reuses it for the life of the bean. A `@PreDestroy` method marks the bean destroyed, waits up to thirty seconds on the lifecycle write lock for a sentiment call already in flight, then closes the client; a call arriving after destruction is refused with `IllegalStateException`. The client is built from settings that replace the generated `AnalyzeSentiment` defaults — ten minutes for the initial RPC, the maximum RPC and the total — with a ten-second per-attempt deadline and a thirty-second total deadline, leaving the retryable status codes and the retry delays as the client declares them and every other operation untouched. | (a) Construct the client eagerly in the constructor, which is what the source did at `backend/app/services/sentiment_analysis.py:L8`. (b) Publish the client as a `@Bean` and let the container own its lifecycle. (c) Create a client per call and close it in a `finally`. (d) Leave the generated call settings untouched. (e) Bound the two deadlines through configuration keys. (f) Close the client at shutdown without waiting for an in-flight call. | Application Default Credentials are resolved when the client is created, so eager construction makes credential availability a startup requirement for an application whose other ten routes do not need it, and `mvn clean verify` runs on a host with no credentials at all. Lazy creation moves that failure to the one route that needs the provider and keeps the context refreshable, while the single instance preserves the source's one-client-per-service shape and the channel pooling the generated client performs. (b) has the same lifecycle but publishes a bean that cannot be created on a credential-less host, so the context fails to refresh for the same reason as (a). (c) pays channel setup on every request and discards the pooling. (d) is the sharpest defect: the generated default lets one sentiment call hold its calling thread for ten minutes, and that thread is a request thread on `POST /tweets/{tweetId}/analyze` or one of the two scheduler threads on the ingestion path. Ten seconds per attempt and thirty in total bound the worst case at a value a caller can wait for, and narrowing `AnalyzeSentiment` alone leaves every other operation as its vendor set it. (e) adds two keys to the configuration surface this migration caps, for values no operator has a reason to move. (f) closes a channel underneath a call in progress and turns an orderly shutdown into a provider error. | The first sentiment call of a process pays the client creation, so its latency is not representative, and a credential misconfiguration surfaces on a request rather than at startup: an operator reading a green startup log has no evidence that credentials resolve. Double-checked locking is correct only while the field stays `volatile` and the destroyed flag is read inside the monitor, and only review enforces that. The thirty-second total deadline is shorter than the retry budget the generated settings imply, so a call that would have succeeded on a later attempt now fails — the intended bound, which makes a slow provider indistinguishable from an unavailable one. The shutdown wait is bounded, so a call that outlasts thirty seconds sees its client closed and reports a transport failure. |
| DL-289 | `config/RestClientConfig.headerSafe(String, String)` is the single gate every configured value passes before it becomes a default header on the Notion client. It strips the value, then discards it — returning an empty string — if any remaining character is below `U+0020` or is `U+007F`, recording one `WARN` that names the property and no part of the value. An unsafe `scanner.notion.api-key` yields an empty bearer token; an unsafe `scanner.notion.api-version` falls back to the compiled default of DL-151, so no unsafe text reaches a header on either path. | (a) Send each configured value as bound, which is what the class did before this gate existed. (b) Strip only, relying on `String.strip()` to remove a carriage return or line feed. (c) Remove the offending characters and send what is left. (d) Refuse to create the bean and fail startup on an unsafe value. (e) Log the rejected value so an operator can see what was wrong with it. (f) Validate the two properties with a Bean Validation pattern on the bound record instead. | A default header is written into the header region of every outbound message, so an embedded carriage return and line feed in a configured value is a header-injection primitive: an operator mistake or a compromised configuration source appends an arbitrary header to every Notion request. (b) is the near miss this gate closes — `strip()` removes a leading or trailing CR or LF and leaves an embedded one exactly where it does harm, which `config/RestClientConfigTest` demonstrates with three embedded forms. (c) sends a value nobody configured and hides the mistake. (d) is defensible for the key and wrong for the version, which has a working compiled default; failing startup for a value with a safe fallback changes the clean-checkout contract. (e) writes provider-unrelated but attacker-influenced bytes into the log, which is the injection the guard of DL-149 exists to prevent, and the property name is enough to act on. (f) places the check on the bound record, where the required-versus-optional parity of DL-231 admits no pattern constraint, and reports at binding time rather than at the point the value becomes a header. | An unsafe API key yields an empty bearer token rather than a startup failure, so the first Notion request answers 401 and the cause is one `WARN` earlier in the log; an operator who missed it sees an authentication failure and not a configuration one. The check is a character-class test and not a header grammar, so a value legal in every character and still meaningless to Notion is sent unchanged, which DL-193 records. Only these two properties are gated, so any future default header has to route through this method and nothing mechanically enforces that. |
